# PassReady Prep — Static Question Bank (Hard Copy)

**Generated:** 2026-09-15
**Total items:** 419 across 4 banks

**Legend**

- `✔` marks the keyed (correct) option.
- **Decision-profile items** (Next Best Step, What to Assess Next) carry per-option **weights** and a **Priority Ladder rung**. The bank quality gate (`utils/bankGate.js`) enforces exactly one of each weight `{3, 0, -1, -2}` per item.
- **Recall-profile items** (Core Counseling Attributes, Knowledge Bank) are scored dichotomously and are held to structural checks only (length parity, key-not-longest, no absolutes, per-option rationale).

**Priority Ladder (decision items only)**

1. Imminent safety — suicidality, homicidality, abuse, danger.
2. Medical/substance rule-outs.
3. Stabilization — acute symptom relief, grounding.
4. Alliance and validation — client must feel heard first.
5. Clarify the picture — assessment, history, collateral.
6. Evidence-based treatment — right modality for the right diagnosis.
7. Ethics throughout — confidentiality, mandated reporting, competence.

**Blueprint domains (NBCC NCMHCE content outline)**

- `counseling` — Counseling Skills & Interventions (30%)
- `intake` — Intake, Assessment & Diagnosis (25%)
- `treatment` — Treatment Planning (15%)
- `ethics` — Professional Practice & Ethics (15%)
- `core` — Core Counseling Attributes (15%)

## Next Best Step

- **Items:** 48
- **Profile:** decision
- **Served at:** `/next-best-step.html`
- Scoring: **+3 key**, **0 near-miss** (partial credit), **-1 novice error**, **-2 harmful**. Every item carries a Priority Ladder rung.

### 1. nbs1 · domain: counseling · category: Depressive · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client with moderate MDD discloses passive suicidal ideation ("sometimes I wish I just wouldn’t wake up") but no plan or intent. What is the next best step?

**A. [✔]** Safety-plan first, increase session frequency, then start behavioral activation as the treatment step  _[+3 KEY]_
> Address the SI risk first, then engage an activating low-demand intervention that matches the depression.

**B. [ ]** Refer the client out immediately because any level of suicidal ideation is beyond your role as counselor  _[-2 harmful]_
> Passive SI without plan or intent is within scope to manage; reflexive referral is abandonment.
> **Common mistake:** Reflexively offloading a case a counselor can manage — abandoning a client who was in scope.

**C. [ ]** Teach a brief relaxation exercise this session and reassess the ideation and mood a month from now  _[-1 novice error]_
> Relaxation is not a stand-alone treatment and a month is too long when passive SI is present.
> **Common mistake:** Substituting a coping technique for a real depression plan and delaying reassessment far too long.

**D. [ ]** Begin the full cognitive-restructuring CBT protocol now without formally addressing the passive SI first  _[0 near-miss]_
> CBT is right for MDD, wrong timing: safety planning has to come before intensive protocol work.
> **Common mistake:** Right treatment for MDD but wrong order — starting the protocol before the SI is addressed.

**Rule:** MDD + passive SI → safety plan + increased contact + behavioral activation before a full CBT protocol.

**Reference:** ACA / risk-first sequencing

---

### 2. nbs2 · domain: counseling · category: Anxiety · rung: 4 (Alliance and validation)

A client with GAD understands the rationale for exposure but says, "I can’t do exposure — it’s too much." Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Switch the plan entirely to a relaxation-training-only intervention because the client refused exposure now  _[0 near-miss]_
> Relaxation has a role as an adjunct, but replacing the active ingredient concedes to avoidance.
> **Common mistake:** Retreating to an adjunctive technique instead of working with the client's ambivalence about exposure.

**B. [✔]** Validate the fear and psychoeducate on how avoidance maintains anxiety over time before graded exposure  _[+3 KEY]_
> Meet resistance with validation plus the mechanism to build willingness for graded exposure.

**C. [ ]** Proceed straight into the most-feared exposure so the client can prove to themselves it is survivable  _[-1 novice error]_
> Flooding without buy-in ruptures the alliance; exposure is graded and collaborative in modern CBT.
> **Common mistake:** Applying exposure without a graded ladder or collaboration — misreading fidelity as intensity.

**D. [ ]** Terminate the treatment because the client is not willing to engage with the primary intervention today  _[-2 harmful]_
> Ambivalence about exposure is workable and expected; premature termination abandons the client.
> **Common mistake:** Reading normal ambivalence as non-engagement and abandoning a workable case prematurely.

**Rule:** Refusal of exposure → validate + psychoeducate on avoidance; don’t retreat to relaxation-only or terminate.

**Reference:** CBT for GAD

---

### 3. nbs3 · domain: treatment · category: Trauma · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A client has PTSD and a co-occurring alcohol use disorder. They are not in acute withdrawal. What is the best treatment approach?

**A. [ ]** Require full sobriety first and delay any trauma work indefinitely until abstinence has been established  _[-1 novice error]_
> The "get sober first" model is outdated and indefinite delay of trauma care is exam-punished.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an outdated sequential framework that leaves the trauma untreated for a long delay.

**B. [ ]** Begin prolonged exposure for the PTSD now and simply ignore the drinking while trauma work is underway  _[0 near-miss]_
> PE is right for PTSD, wrong to ignore the SUD, which drives symptoms and relapse.
> **Common mistake:** Correct trauma modality applied while a real driver of symptoms is left completely untreated.

**C. [✔]** Integrated concurrent treatment of the PTSD and the SUD (for example, the COPE protocol) from the start  _[+3 KEY]_
> Concurrent integrated treatment outperforms sequential care for comorbid PTSD and SUD.

**D. [ ]** Refer out because a case with both PTSD and a substance use disorder is too complex for counseling  _[-2 harmful]_
> Comorbid PTSD+SUD is common and treatable in scope; reflexive referral abandons the client.
> **Common mistake:** Confusing complexity with out-of-scope and abandoning a client who was manageable in counseling.

**Rule:** PTSD + SUD (not acute withdrawal) → integrated/concurrent treatment; only acute withdrawal justifies stabilizing that first.

**Reference:** COPE / integrated care

---

### 4. nbs4 · domain: treatment · category: Personality · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A client with BPD reports a recent episode of cutting. In session, what is the first step?

**A. [ ]** Arrange psychiatric hospitalization immediately since the client has engaged in a self-harming behavior  _[-2 harmful]_
> NSSI is not automatically suicidal; escalating care without assessing function is over-triage.
> **Common mistake:** Treating any self-harm as a suicide attempt without function assessment — misreading NSSI.

**B. [ ]** Have the client sign a written no-harm contract with you before proceeding with any further session work  _[-1 novice error]_
> No-harm contracts are not evidence-based; safety planning and skills work replace them.
> **Common mistake:** Using a discredited technique in place of the evidence-based sequence for self-harm in DBT.

**C. [ ]** Immediately teach a distress-tolerance skill such as TIPP so the client has a coping tool right away  _[0 near-miss]_
> TIPP is right in DBT, wrong order: a chain analysis has to come before choosing the skill.
> **Common mistake:** Correct DBT skill offered too soon — skipping the analysis that tells you which skill to teach.

**D. [✔]** Conduct a full behavioral chain analysis of the self-harm episode before selecting a replacement skill  _[+3 KEY]_
> Chain analysis identifies the trigger and function first so the right replacement skill can be chosen.

**Rule:** BPD + self-harm → chain analysis first, then the replacement skill.

**Reference:** DBT sequencing

---

### 5. nbs5 · domain: treatment · category: Personality · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A client with BPD calls the crisis line for the third time this week, distressed after a breakup. You have a crisis plan on file. Next best step?

**A. [✔]** Follow the pre-established crisis plan and use the DBT phone-coaching steps you already agreed on together  _[+3 KEY]_
> Consistency with the collaborative crisis plan avoids reinforcing crisis-driven contact.

**B. [ ]** Tell the client not to call the crisis line again because their pattern of contact has become disruptive  _[-2 harmful]_
> Punitive refusals of help-seeking are unsafe and clinically harmful for a client with BPD.
> **Common mistake:** Treating a legitimate safety route as misconduct — punishing help-seeking rather than shaping it.

**C. [ ]** Ignore the incoming call in order to avoid reinforcing crisis-driven between-session contact from the client  _[-1 novice error]_
> Ignoring a call risks safety; the plan structures a measured response, not a refusal.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an extinction rationale to a safety call — misreading a safety issue as reinforcement.

**D. [ ]** Call the client back immediately and hold a long unscheduled session to get them stabilized this evening  _[0 near-miss]_
> Prompt contact is right for a crisis, but an extended off-schedule session reinforces crisis behavior.
> **Common mistake:** Right instinct to respond, wrong dose — an extended off-schedule session reinforces the pattern.

**Rule:** BPD crisis contact → follow the crisis plan; don’t improvise reinforcing responses.

**Reference:** DBT / crisis planning

---

### 6. nbs6 · domain: counseling · category: Substance · rung: 4 (Alliance and validation)

A client mandated after a DUI says, "I don’t really have a problem — I can handle my drinking." Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Require abstinence from alcohol as a condition of continuing your treatment and set an ultimatum today  _[-2 harmful]_
> Coercive contingencies at precontemplation drive dropout and are a control-based framework error.
> **Common mistake:** Coercive ultimatum imposed at precontemplation — nearly guaranteed to end the treatment.

**B. [✔]** Use motivational interviewing to explore ambivalence with OARS while honoring the client’s autonomy  _[+3 KEY]_
> Precontemplation calls for MI to evoke change talk, not action-stage interventions.

**C. [ ]** Refer the client into an abstinence-based mutual-help group and assign coping skills for this next week  _[0 near-miss]_
> These are right tools, wrong stage — action-stage tools for a precontemplative client are premature.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an action-stage intervention to a precontemplative client and skipping the MI step.

**D. [ ]** Directly confront the client’s denial in order to break through it and force acknowledgment of the problem  _[-1 novice error]_
> Confrontation of "denial" increases reactance and is exam-punished as a wrong framework.
> **Common mistake:** Using a confrontation-based framework that increases reactance instead of evoking change talk.

**Rule:** Precontemplation → MI only; match action-stage tools to Preparation/Action.

**Reference:** Stages of change / MI

---

### 7. nbs7 · domain: treatment · category: Bipolar · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client with Bipolar II presents in an acute depressive episode and wants to start intensive therapy. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Begin an intensive weekly CBT protocol for the depression now while the client is highly motivated for care  _[-1 novice error]_
> Intensive CBT while actively cycling risks destabilization and misses medication as the primary lever.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing unipolar-depression sequencing for a bipolar depression — a framework mismatch here.

**B. [ ]** Support the client’s request to start an antidepressant monotherapy in order to relieve the current depression  _[-2 harmful]_
> Antidepressant monotherapy risks a manic switch and is outside a counselor’s scope to endorse.
> **Common mistake:** Endorsing a pharmacological plan with switch risk that lies outside the counselor’s scope.

**C. [✔]** Refer for mood stabilization first, then add IPSRT or CBT as adjunctive therapy once the client is stable  _[+3 KEY]_
> Stabilize the mood episode before intensive psychotherapy; therapy is adjunctive in bipolar disorders.

**D. [ ]** Start a structured trauma-processing protocol so the underlying material driving the depression is addressed  _[0 near-miss]_
> Trauma work has a place in bipolar care, but processing during an active episode is destabilizing.
> **Common mistake:** Reasonable modality but wrong timing — processing during an active episode destabilizes further.

**Rule:** Bipolar depression → refer for stabilization first; therapy is adjunctive; never endorse antidepressant monotherapy.

**Reference:** Sequencing / scope

---

### 8. nbs8 · domain: treatment · category: Eating · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A 19-year-old with anorexia has a BMI of 15.5, heart rate 46, and reports dizziness on standing. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Begin family-based treatment now because family involvement will help stabilize the eating behavior quickly  _[0 near-miss]_
> FBT is evidence-based, but it still requires medical stability first at these vital signs.
> **Common mistake:** Right modality at the wrong time — medical stabilization has to come before FBT can begin.

**B. [ ]** Continue with weekly outpatient monitoring only, since the client is engaged and coming to sessions on time  _[-2 harmful]_
> Monitoring alone is inadequate for orthostasis and bradycardia; this needs medical care right now.
> **Common mistake:** Under-triage of medically dangerous vital signs — monitoring treated as a substitute for care.

**C. [ ]** Begin outpatient CBT-E as the primary treatment because it is first-line for the eating disorder diagnosis  _[-1 novice error]_
> Starting psychotherapy while medically unstable applies the right framework at the wrong body state.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing outpatient psychotherapy for a client whose vital signs demand medical care instead.

**D. [✔]** Pause psychosocial treatment and facilitate immediate medical stabilization or hospitalization for this client  _[+3 KEY]_
> These vitals are medical red flags that override outpatient psychotherapy for eating disorders.

**Rule:** Anorexia with BMI <18.5 (esp. <16), HR <50, or orthostasis → medical stabilization before any psychosocial treatment.

**Reference:** ED medical thresholds

---

### 9. nbs9 · domain: intake · category: Neurocognitive · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

An older adult is brought in with confusion that started over two days, fluctuates through the day, and includes poor attention. Next best step?

**A. [✔]** Rule out delirium first and refer the client for an urgent medical work-up before any diagnostic decisions  _[+3 KEY]_
> Acute plus fluctuating plus inattention equals delirium — a medical emergency — until proven otherwise.

**B. [ ]** Diagnose a major neurocognitive disorder now and begin caregiver counseling with the family this week  _[0 near-miss]_
> Dementia work-up is on the differential, but dementia is insidious and stable, not acute and fluctuating.
> **Common mistake:** Right chronic-care direction, wrong sequence — an acute fluctuating course points to delirium.

**C. [ ]** Treat the confusion as a normal adjustment reaction to aging and reassure the client and family about it  _[-2 harmful]_
> Minimizing a reversible medical emergency risks real harm from missed acute organic illness.
> **Common mistake:** Dismissing an acute organic emergency as normal aging — a criterion-reversal that endangers life.

**D. [ ]** Diagnose major depressive disorder and begin a course of behavioral activation with the client this week  _[-1 novice error]_
> Mislabeling an acute medical state as depression is a framework error that misses delirium.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a depression framework to what is more likely a reversible medical delirium episode.

**Rule:** Acute, fluctuating confusion with inattention → rule out delirium / medical referral first.

**Reference:** Delirium vs dementia

---

### 10. nbs10 · domain: treatment · category: Psychotic · rung: 3 (Stabilization)

A client is acutely psychotic with active paranoid delusions and disorganized thinking. Next best step in your role?

**A. [ ]** Start supportive counseling in the room while awaiting stabilization and gently teach coping and grounding  _[0 near-miss]_
> Supportive contact is right during stabilization, but omitting the psychiatric referral leaves a gap.
> **Common mistake:** Right supportive presence but missing the urgent psychiatric referral that stabilization needs.

**B. [✔]** Facilitate a psychiatric referral for stabilization and build a calm, low-stimulus alliance in the meantime  _[+3 KEY]_
> Medication and stabilization are foundational; the counselor supports and coordinates during that period.

**C. [ ]** Begin insight-oriented uncovering psychotherapy right away to explore the underlying meaning of the psychosis  _[-2 harmful]_
> Uncovering work is destabilizing during active psychosis and can precipitate further decompensation.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing a destabilizing modality during acute psychosis — treatment that risks decompensation.

**D. [ ]** Directly challenge the paranoid delusions in session and dispute the beliefs to demonstrate they are untrue  _[-1 novice error]_
> Confronting delusions damages the alliance; disputing beliefs in acute psychosis is contraindicated.
> **Common mistake:** Using a cognitive-disputation framework in an acute psychotic state where alliance is fragile.

**Rule:** Active psychosis → refer for stabilization + alliance; no exposure, no confrontation, no uncovering work.

**Reference:** Psychosis management

---

### 11. nbs11 · domain: intake · category: Crisis · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client endorses suicidal ideation with a plan. What is the correct first action?

**A. [ ]** Ask the client to sign a written no-suicide contract and rely on that agreement to keep them safe tonight  _[-1 novice error]_
> No-suicide contracts are not evidence-based; safety planning and means restriction replace them.
> **Common mistake:** Using a discredited technique in place of the current safety-plan + means-restriction sequence.

**B. [ ]** Call 911 immediately with no prior structured assessment and hand the client’s care off to first responders  _[-2 harmful]_
> Over-triage without assessment erodes trust and may be unwarranted; structured assessment comes first.
> **Common mistake:** Over-triage that skips structured assessment — offloading the case in place of the correct sequence.

**C. [✔]** Structured C-SSRS assessment, then a collaborative safety plan with means restriction and level-of-care call  _[+3 KEY]_
> Assess first, then match the response to the risk with a safety plan, means restriction, and level of care.

**D. [ ]** Reassure the client that having a plan is not the same as intent and schedule a routine follow-up next week  _[0 near-miss]_
> Reassurance and follow-up are elements of care but insufficient as a response to a plan-level disclosure.
> **Common mistake:** Right relational tone applied at the wrong intensity — under-response to a plan-level disclosure.

**Rule:** SI with plan → structured assessment + safety plan + means restriction + level-of-care; never a contract.

**Reference:** C-SSRS / Stanley-Brown safety planning

---

### 12. nbs12 · domain: treatment · category: Disruptive · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A 15-year-old with conduct disorder is referred for aggression and theft. Which approach has the best evidence?

**A. [ ]** Individual insight-oriented therapy alone that explores the youth’s inner conflicts about the aggression  _[0 near-miss]_
> Insight is a real ingredient, but insight-only individual work underperforms family and systems models.
> **Common mistake:** Right target but wrong modality — insight-only individual work underperforms family models.

**B. [ ]** A boot-camp or "scared straight" program to correct the youth’s behavior through disciplined confrontation  _[-2 harmful]_
> Boot-camp programs worsen outcomes and are unsupported; recommending them causes measurable harm.
> **Common mistake:** Recommending an evidence-negative program — harm-producing rather than harm-reducing care.

**C. [ ]** Group therapy that brings together several antisocial adolescents in a single peer group with the counselor  _[-1 novice error]_
> Grouping antisocial youth is iatrogenic ("deviancy training") and applies the wrong modality here.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing a modality shown to worsen outcomes for this population — iatrogenic peer effects.

**D. [✔]** Multisystemic Therapy or Functional Family Therapy engaging the family and the client’s prosocial peers  _[+3 KEY]_
> Family and systems interventions have the strongest evidence base for conduct disorder in adolescents.

**Rule:** Conduct disorder → MST/FFT + family; avoid grouping antisocial youth and punitive programs.

**Reference:** MST / FFT evidence

---

### 13. nbs13 · domain: treatment · category: OCD-Related · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A client has OCD with contamination obsessions and washing compulsions. First-line treatment?

**A. [✔]** Exposure and Response Prevention (ERP), coordinating an SSRI referral if pharmacotherapy is warranted  _[+3 KEY]_
> ERP is first-line for OCD; response prevention is the active ingredient that treats the disorder.

**B. [ ]** Help the client arrange their home and work environment so they can avoid the contamination triggers  _[-1 novice error]_
> Accommodation and avoidance reinforce OCD; this is the opposite framework from ERP.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an accommodation framework that reinforces the very compulsions ERP is meant to extinguish.

**C. [ ]** Provide repeated reassurance that the client’s environment is safe and reinforce that whenever asked  _[-2 harmful]_
> Reassurance is a compulsion-equivalent that maintains and worsens OCD across sessions.
> **Common mistake:** Providing reassurance that functions as a compulsion — actively feeding the disorder session by session.

**D. [ ]** Supportive talk therapy that explores the content of the obsessions in session without response prevention  _[0 near-miss]_
> Supportive talk is helpful adjunctively but does not treat OCD without the response-prevention step.
> **Common mistake:** Right supportive stance but the active ingredient — response prevention — is entirely missing.

**Rule:** OCD → ERP (not reassurance, not accommodation).

**Reference:** ERP for OCD

---

### 14. nbs14 · domain: treatment · category: Sleep · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client with Bipolar I disorder also has chronic insomnia. You are planning CBT-I. What is the key caution?

**A. [ ]** Prescribe a long-term hypnotic as the primary treatment and continue it indefinitely for the insomnia  _[-2 harmful]_
> Counselors do not prescribe, and chronic hypnotic monotherapy is not the plan for chronic insomnia.
> **Common mistake:** Recommending an out-of-scope pharmacologic plan that is also not first-line clinically.

**B. [✔]** Avoid sleep restriction because of mania risk; use other CBT-I components and coordinate with the prescriber  _[+3 KEY]_
> Sleep restriction can precipitate mania in Bipolar I; other CBT-I components remain appropriate.

**C. [ ]** Apply standard CBT-I but shorten the sleep-restriction window and monitor closely for hypomanic symptoms  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right direction but wrong dose — even shortened sleep restriction carries switch risk in Bipolar I.
> **Common mistake:** Titrating a contraindicated component instead of dropping it — right instinct, wrong action.

**D. [ ]** Tell the client simply to spend more time in bed each night so they will eventually get more sleep overall  _[-1 novice error]_
> Extending time in bed worsens conditioned arousal and is not a CBT-I framework at all.
> **Common mistake:** Using a folk sleep-hygiene rule that is the opposite of the CBT-I evidence base for insomnia.

**Rule:** CBT-I sleep restriction is contraindicated in Bipolar I (mania) and seizure disorders.

**Reference:** CBT-I contraindications

---

### 15. nbs15 · domain: treatment · category: Somatic · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A client with Somatic Symptom Disorder requests yet another round of medical tests for reassurance. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Tell the client that their physical symptoms are "all in your head" and that no more testing is warranted  _[-2 harmful]_
> Invalidating language misrepresents SSD, ruptures the alliance, and is clinically harmful.
> **Common mistake:** Invalidating language that harms the alliance and misrepresents how SSD is actually experienced.

**B. [ ]** Refer the client to a new specialist for each new symptom that comes up during the course of treatment  _[0 near-miss]_
> A specialist may be needed at times, but doctor-shopping reinforces illness behavior across time.
> **Common mistake:** Right medical instinct but wrong pattern — repeated new referrals reinforce illness behavior.

**C. [✔]** Coordinate one medical home with scheduled (not symptom-triggered) visits and provide CBT alongside that  _[+3 KEY]_
> Scheduled visits plus CBT reduce reassurance-seeking and reinforce functioning across time.

**D. [ ]** Facilitate additional medical testing this week in order to provide reassurance and calm the client down  _[-1 novice error]_
> Additional testing feeds reassurance-seeking and maintains SSD in a framework that fuels the disorder.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing a soothing action that actually maintains the disorder — reassurance as compulsion.

**Rule:** SSD → single medical home + scheduled visits + CBT; don’t reinforce testing/reassurance.

**Reference:** SSD management

---

### 16. nbs16 · domain: treatment · category: Dissociative · rung: 3 (Stabilization)

A client with dissociative identity disorder and extensive trauma wants to "process the memories" in the first month. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Use hypnosis in early sessions to help the client recover additional trauma memories they cannot access  _[-1 novice error]_
> Memory-recovery hypnosis is contraindicated and risks confabulation and false memories in DID.
> **Common mistake:** Using a discredited memory-recovery framework instead of phase-oriented dissociative care.

**B. [ ]** Begin trauma processing of the memories now while the client is motivated and clearly asking to do the work  _[0 near-miss]_
> Client readiness matters but is not sufficient; premature processing destabilizes and retraumatizes.
> **Common mistake:** Reading client willingness as clinical readiness — skipping the phase 1 stabilization work.

**C. [ ]** Treat the internal parts as psychotic hallucinations and coordinate a referral for antipsychotic medication  _[-2 harmful]_
> DID parts are internal experiences, not psychosis; misframing them can cause misdiagnosis and harm.
> **Common mistake:** Misdiagnosing dissociative parts as psychosis — a criterion reversal that misdirects treatment.

**D. [✔]** Prioritize safety and stabilization and build internal communication before any trauma processing begins  _[+3 KEY]_
> Phase-oriented care in DID: stabilize and build communication among parts before processing memories.

**Rule:** DID → stabilize first (phase-oriented); no premature processing or memory-recovery hypnosis.

**Reference:** Phase-oriented DID care

---

### 17. nbs17 · domain: ethics · category: Ethics · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

In session, a client makes a specific, credible threat to seriously harm a named person whose location they know. Next best step?

**A. [✔]** Take reasonable protective steps under state law and disclose only what is necessary to warn or notify  _[+3 KEY]_
> A serious foreseeable threat to an identifiable person triggers a duty-to-protect response.

**B. [ ]** Wait to see whether the client raises the same threat again in next week’s session before doing anything  _[-1 novice error]_
> Delay on an imminent, identifiable threat is unsafe and misapplies the ethics framework.
> **Common mistake:** Deferring an ethical duty as if it were a clinical hypothesis to be re-checked next session.

**C. [ ]** Drive to the victim’s home and share the full contents of the client’s clinical record in person immediately  _[-2 harmful]_
> Over-disclosure well beyond what protection requires is a serious confidentiality breach.
> **Common mistake:** Over-disclosing far beyond the minimum-necessary standard — protection twisted into breach.

**D. [ ]** Contact the client’s emergency contact instead of the identified potential victim to relay the concern  _[0 near-miss]_
> A protective step is right, but bypassing the identifiable victim does not satisfy the duty.
> **Common mistake:** Right protective instinct routed to the wrong person — not the identifiable potential victim.

**Rule:** Serious, foreseeable threat to an identifiable person → duty to protect (warn/notify) with minimum necessary disclosure.

**Reference:** ACA / duty to protect

---

### 18. nbs18 · domain: ethics · category: Ethics · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

A 14-year-old client discloses bruises from a caregiver "getting physical." You cannot be certain it was abuse. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Wait until you are certain that actual abuse has occurred before filing any report with the state agency  _[-1 novice error]_
> Requiring certainty violates the reasonable-suspicion standard and stalls a required response.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a criminal-court standard of proof to a mandated-reporter standard of suspicion.

**B. [✔]** File a mandated report of suspected child abuse per your state law using the reasonable-suspicion threshold  _[+3 KEY]_
> The mandated-reporter threshold is reasonable suspicion, not proof; you report and let the agency investigate.

**C. [ ]** Notify a school counselor at the client’s school and let them file the report on the counselor’s behalf  _[0 near-miss]_
> Coordination is fine, but the mandated duty is personal and delegating does not discharge it.
> **Common mistake:** Delegating a personal mandated duty to another party rather than filing the report yourself.

**D. [ ]** Keep the disclosure fully confidential from the report system because the client asked you to keep it so  _[-2 harmful]_
> A minor’s request cannot waive the reporting duty; withholding a report is a serious violation.
> **Common mistake:** Treating confidentiality as absolute in the face of a mandated-report trigger — misuse of privilege.

**Rule:** Suspected child abuse → report on reasonable suspicion; you don’t investigate or wait for proof.

**Reference:** Mandated reporting

---

### 19. nbs19 · domain: intake · category: Depressive · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A depressed client mentions a past period of several days with little need for sleep, racing thoughts, and unusually high energy. They ask you to support starting an antidepressant. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Tell the client medication questions and any bipolar considerations are not part of your role as counselor  _[-1 novice error]_
> Screening and coordination are within the counselor’s role; disengaging misapplies the scope framework.
> **Common mistake:** Reading coordination and screening as out-of-scope — a scope framework applied too narrowly.

**B. [ ]** Begin CBT immediately for the depressive symptoms and set aside the past episode until later in treatment  _[0 near-miss]_
> CBT is a reasonable modality; setting aside a possible hypomanic history is a differential-diagnosis miss.
> **Common mistake:** Reasonable modality that leaves an unresolved differential intact — a screen was skipped.

**C. [✔]** Screen for a bipolar history and refer for an evaluation before endorsing any antidepressant plan with them  _[+3 KEY]_
> A possible hypomanic episode changes the whole plan; screening now prevents a manic-switch downstream.

**D. [ ]** Support the client’s request for antidepressant monotherapy to give them relief from the depressive symptoms  _[-2 harmful]_
> Endorsing antidepressant monotherapy in a possible bipolar history risks a manic switch and is out of scope.
> **Common mistake:** Endorsing a pharmacological plan with switch risk that lies outside a counselor’s scope entirely.

**Rule:** Screen for hypomania before any antidepressant plan; a positive screen → refer, don’t endorse monotherapy.

**Reference:** Bipolar screening / scope

---

### 20. nbs20 · domain: treatment · category: Anxiety · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A client with panic disorder asks whether they should just avoid the places where attacks happen and take a benzodiazepine daily. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Endorse a standing daily benzodiazepine as the main long-term treatment for the client’s panic disorder  _[-2 harmful]_
> Standing benzodiazepines risk dependence and block exposure learning; also outside counselor scope.
> **Common mistake:** Endorsing a pharmacologic plan that blocks the very learning exposure treatment relies on.

**B. [ ]** Agree that avoiding the places where the attacks occur is a reasonable plan while you continue counseling  _[-1 novice error]_
> Sanctioning avoidance accelerates agoraphobia; this is the opposite framework from exposure-based CBT.
> **Common mistake:** Endorsing avoidance — an anti-therapeutic framework in a disorder maintained by avoidance itself.

**C. [ ]** Offer relaxation training as the sole intervention this session and adjust the plan later if progress stalls  _[0 near-miss]_
> Relaxation has an adjunctive role but alone does not extinguish the fear-of-fear cycle in panic.
> **Common mistake:** Adjunctive technique used as the whole plan — the active ingredient of exposure is missing.

**D. [✔]** CBT with interoceptive and situational exposure, and explain why standing benzodiazepines undercut recovery  _[+3 KEY]_
> Exposure is first-line for panic; standing benzodiazepines and avoidance maintain the fear-of-fear cycle.

**Rule:** Panic → exposure-based CBT; don’t endorse avoidance or standing benzodiazepines.

**Reference:** Panic Control Treatment

---

### 21. nbs21 · domain: intake · category: Substance · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client with severe alcohol use disorder reports tremor, sweating, and rising anxiety since cutting down yesterday. Next best step?

**A. [✔]** Facilitate medical evaluation and management for the withdrawal before continuing any behavioral treatment  _[+3 KEY]_
> Alcohol withdrawal can be medically dangerous; medical stabilization comes before behavioral treatment.

**B. [ ]** Start CBT relapse-prevention skills work in this same session while the client is still in the office  _[-1 novice error]_
> Beginning behavioral relapse work before managing acute withdrawal applies the wrong framework.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an outpatient behavioral framework to a client with an active medical emergency in the room.

**C. [ ]** Refer the client to a 12-step meeting tonight and rely on peer support to hold them through the withdrawal  _[-2 harmful]_
> Peer support does not manage the medical danger of alcohol withdrawal; this misses the medical rule-out.
> **Common mistake:** Under-triage of a medically dangerous state — peer support in place of medical management.

**D. [ ]** Do motivational interviewing about the drinking today and schedule a next session for one week from now  _[0 near-miss]_
> MI is right at another stage; today the acute medical risk of withdrawal has to come first.
> **Common mistake:** Correct stage-matched intervention applied when the priority is an acute medical rule-out first.

**Rule:** Acute alcohol/benzo withdrawal → medical management first; then behavioral treatment.

**Reference:** Withdrawal safety

---

### 22. nbs22 · domain: ethics · category: Scope · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

A client asks you directly, "Which is better for me — sertraline or venlafaxine?" Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Recommend sertraline yourself since it is a well-tolerated first-line agent for many depressed clients  _[-1 novice error]_
> Naming a specific agent exceeds counselor scope; this applies a prescribing framework you do not hold.
> **Common mistake:** Naming a specific agent — a framework a counselor does not hold and cannot ethically apply.

**B. [✔]** Provide psychoeducation on combined treatment and refer to the prescriber for the medication comparison call  _[+3 KEY]_
> Selecting or ranking specific agents is outside counselor scope; educate and route to the prescriber.

**C. [ ]** Advise the client to avoid medication entirely and rely on therapy alone for the depressive symptoms today  _[-2 harmful]_
> Discouraging indicated medication is outside scope and may harm a client who needs pharmacotherapy.
> **Common mistake:** Directing a client away from indicated care — a scope violation that can produce real harm.

**D. [ ]** Explain general side-effect classes in neutral terms and refer to the prescriber for the actual decision  _[0 near-miss]_
> General education is inside scope, but agent-vs-agent comparison should still go to the prescriber.
> **Common mistake:** Close to the correct move but stopping just short — no explicit hand-off to the prescriber.

**Rule:** Counselors educate and refer for medication; they don’t name or rank specific agents.

**Reference:** Scope of practice

---

### 23. nbs23 · domain: treatment · category: Trauma · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client with PTSD is motivated to process their trauma but is currently having active suicidal urges and feels unsafe. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Refer the client out to a specialist team because trauma work combined with suicidality is unmanageable  _[0 near-miss]_
> Referral has a place, but this combination is manageable in scope with proper sequencing first.
> **Common mistake:** Reflexively referring rather than sequencing — a workable case handed off unnecessarily.

**B. [ ]** Tell the client that trauma work is off the table for good so as to keep them safer from now on in therapy  _[-2 harmful]_
> A permanent ban on trauma processing overcorrects and denies the client an appropriate future treatment.
> **Common mistake:** Overcorrecting to permanent avoidance — foreclosing indicated treatment the client will need later.

**C. [✔]** Stabilize the safety concerns first with assessment and a safety plan, then move to trauma processing later  _[+3 KEY]_
> Stabilization must precede exposure and processing when acutely unsafe; this is standard phase-based care.

**D. [ ]** Begin prolonged exposure right now while the client’s motivation for trauma work is unusually high today  _[-1 novice error]_
> Motivation is not the same as clinical readiness; processing during acute SI without stabilization is unsafe.
> **Common mistake:** Framework mismatch — reading motivation as readiness when stabilization has not been completed.

**Rule:** PTSD + acute suicidality → stabilize first, then process; don’t start exposure while unsafe.

**Reference:** Phase-based trauma care

---

### 24. nbs24 · domain: treatment · category: Neurodevelopmental · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

Parents bring a 9-year-old with clear ADHD symptoms causing academic failure. Which plan best fits?

**A. [ ]** Focus only on the child in individual sessions and leave the school system entirely out of the treatment  _[-1 novice error]_
> Leaving the school system out misses a large modifiable factor and applies an incomplete framework.
> **Common mistake:** Restricting the treatment target to the child alone — ignoring the systems ADHD requires.

**B. [ ]** Advise the parents to avoid stimulant medication entirely and try behavioral parenting techniques alone  _[-2 harmful]_
> Discouraging indicated medication is outside scope and not supported by the ADHD evidence base.
> **Common mistake:** Directing away from an evidence-based option — a scope-and-evidence violation combined.

**C. [ ]** Provide individual play therapy with the child as the sole treatment plan for the ADHD symptoms this year  _[0 near-miss]_
> Play therapy has adjunctive value but individual insight alone underserves a skills and behavior problem.
> **Common mistake:** Adjunctive modality treated as the whole plan — behavioral and school levers left unused entirely.

**D. [✔]** Coordinate a medication referral, behavioral strategies, and school accommodations under an IEP or 504 plan  _[+3 KEY]_
> ADHD care is multimodal and addresses the school system, not just the child in a single session.

**Rule:** ADHD → multimodal: medication referral + behavioral strategies + school accommodations.

**Reference:** ADHD multimodal care

---

### 25. nbs25 · domain: intake · category: Sexual-Gender · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client reports erectile difficulties that began abruptly and are present in all situations. Next best step?

**A. [✔]** Refer for a medical evaluation to rule out organic causes before any structured psychological treatment  _[+3 KEY]_
> Global, abrupt onset suggests possible organic contributors that need medical rule-out first.

**B. [ ]** Interpret the difficulty as an unconscious conflict and begin depth-oriented insight work on that basis  _[-1 novice error]_
> Attributing to psychology without excluding medical causes is a differential-diagnosis framework error.
> **Common mistake:** Skipping a differential rule-out and applying a psychodynamic frame to a possibly organic issue.

**C. [ ]** Reassure the client that this pattern typically resolves on its own with time and no evaluation is needed  _[-2 harmful]_
> Dismissing a symptom that warrants medical evaluation risks missing a serious organic cause.
> **Common mistake:** Reassuring away a symptom pattern that would flag a serious medical work-up for many clinicians.

**D. [ ]** Begin sensate focus and CBT right away because these interventions are first-line for sexual dysfunctions  _[0 near-miss]_
> These are right interventions once medical causes are excluded — wrong order to start them today.
> **Common mistake:** Correct psychotherapies at the wrong stage — a medical rule-out was skipped over.

**Rule:** Sexual dysfunction → rule out medical/organic causes first; then address relational/psychological factors.

**Reference:** Medical work-up first

---

### 26. nbs26 · domain: intake · category: Crisis · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client shows you superficial scratches they made "to feel something," with no wish to die. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Address the NSSI in treatment planning as important but not urgent and continue with the session agenda  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right general framing but wrong intensity for a new NSSI disclosure; a brief screen belongs here today.
> **Common mistake:** Right long-run framing but wrong immediate intensity — the disclosure warrants a screen now.

**B. [✔]** Screen to distinguish NSSI from suicidal intent and assess the function the behavior serves for the client  _[+3 KEY]_
> NSSI and suicidality differ; assess intent and function before choosing the clinical response here.

**C. [ ]** Assume the scratches represent a suicide attempt and call emergency services immediately for transport  _[-2 harmful]_
> Over-escalating NSSI to a suicide attempt without assessment can be harmful and misuses emergency care.
> **Common mistake:** Over-triage that skips assessment — treating any self-injury as a de facto suicide attempt.

**D. [ ]** Reassure the client that this is a harmless phase for many young people and just drop it in the session  _[-1 novice error]_
> Under-response to a new NSSI disclosure applies the wrong framework and misses function assessment.
> **Common mistake:** Under-responding to NSSI as a harmless phase — a framework that misses function assessment entirely.

**Rule:** NSSI → assess intent and function; don’t assume suicidality or dismiss it.

**Reference:** NSSI vs suicidality

---

### 27. nbs27 · domain: treatment · category: Eating · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

An adolescent with anorexia is now medically stable after treatment. Which is the best next step for ongoing care?

**A. [ ]** Return the client to inpatient medical hospitalization because eating disorders as a class need higher care  _[-2 harmful]_
> Higher LOC is not indicated once medically stable; over-triage misuses inpatient resources here.
> **Common mistake:** Over-escalating a medically stable case — inpatient LOC applied outside its clinical criteria.

**B. [ ]** Individual insight-oriented therapy alone that explores what the anorexia means to the adolescent client  _[0 near-miss]_
> Insight has a role but insight-only underperforms structured evidence-based eating-disorder treatments.
> **Common mistake:** Insight-only individual work chosen where a structured evidence-based ED protocol is indicated.

**C. [✔]** Begin Family-Based Treatment (or CBT-E) and continue medical monitoring on a scheduled routine basis  _[+3 KEY]_
> Once medically stable, FBT for adolescents (or CBT-E) is the evidence-based psychotherapy of choice.

**D. [ ]** Hold off on any therapy until the client says they truly want to change their eating behavior on their own  _[-1 novice error]_
> Waiting on motivation delays needed structured care and applies a wrong stage-of-change framework here.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a stages-of-change wait when structured ED treatment is indicated whatever the ambivalence.

**Rule:** Medically stable adolescent anorexia → FBT/CBT-E with monitoring.

**Reference:** ED psychotherapy

---

### 28. nbs28 · domain: treatment · category: Substance · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A client with severe opioid use disorder asks about Suboxone but worries it is "just trading one addiction for another." Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Require residential inpatient treatment before any medication and refuse to discuss MAT until they attend  _[-2 harmful]_
> Mandating residential is not required, delays effective care, and increases overdose risk in OUD.
> **Common mistake:** Erecting an unnecessary care barrier that delays evidence-based treatment for a fatal condition.

**B. [ ]** Insist that the client attempt an abstinence-only treatment first before you will discuss any medication  _[-1 novice error]_
> Abstinence-only first increases overdose risk versus MAT; this applies an outdated OUD framework.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an outdated abstinence-first framework in a condition with an evidence-based medication.

**C. [ ]** Offer contingency management and continue counseling without pursuing MAT since the client is engaged now  _[0 near-miss]_
> CM is a real evidence-based add-on, but omitting MAT drops the strongest evidence-based option in OUD.
> **Common mistake:** Real evidence-based tool added while the strongest treatment for OUD is skipped over entirely.

**D. [✔]** Provide psychoeducation that MAT is evidence-based treatment for OUD and coordinate a prescriber referral  _[+3 KEY]_
> Address the stigma directly with education; MAT has the strongest evidence for opioid use disorder.

**Rule:** OUD → support MAT (evidence-based) + counseling; don’t reinforce "substitution" stigma.

**Reference:** MAT for OUD

---

### 29. nbs29 · domain: counseling · category: Skills · rung: 4 (Alliance and validation)

A client shares painful news and the counselor responds, "I hear that — you sound devastated and completely blindsided." The client says, "Exactly." What did the counselor do correctly first?

**A. [✔]** Reflected the feeling first, before moving to any intervention, and let the client feel accurately heard  _[+3 KEY]_
> Accurate empathy communicated first builds alliance and earns the right to intervene later in the session.

**B. [ ]** Validated the feeling and then immediately offered a reframe of the situation to help the client cope now  _[-2 harmful]_
> A reframe before full reflection minimizes the experience and short-circuits the empathic contact.
> **Common mistake:** Rushing to a cognitive reframe that overrides the client's felt experience before it lands.

**C. [ ]** Asked a clarifying question to gather more facts about the situation before offering any empathic response  _[-1 novice error]_
> Facts-first before the client feels heard is a framework mismatch for a moment of acute affect.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a high-affect moment as a fact-gathering moment — a skill/framework mismatch.

**D. [ ]** Summarized the session content in one line so the topic could be closed and the agenda could move forward  _[0 near-miss]_
> Summary is a real skill, but at this moment it would terminate exploration rather than deepen it.
> **Common mistake:** Right microskill deployed at the wrong moment — closing where a reflection deepens engagement.

**Rule:** Distress → reflect feeling first; gather facts and intervene only after the client feels heard.

**Reference:** Microskills hierarchy

---

### 30. nbs30 · domain: counseling · category: Skills · rung: 4 (Alliance and validation)

A client repeatedly answers questions with one word and looks at the floor. The counselor has tried open questions without success. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** End the session and reschedule it for a later date when the client feels ready and willing to talk with you  _[-2 harmful]_
> Premature termination abandons a workable clinical moment and models avoidance of relational data.
> **Common mistake:** Ending early when the moment is exactly the one the treatment is meant to work with — abandonment risk.

**B. [✔]** Name the observation tentatively and invite the client to help you understand what is going on right now  _[+3 KEY]_
> Naming the pattern without blame opens a door and addresses the process rather than pushing harder on content.

**C. [ ]** Provide an extended reflection of what the client seems to be feeling and stay silent while they respond  _[0 near-miss]_
> A reflection has a place, but naming the observable process is the more targeted move for this withdrawal.
> **Common mistake:** Right family of skill applied to the wrong level — process observation would land better here.

**D. [ ]** Switch to a series of closed questions so you can get at least some usable data from the client this hour  _[-1 novice error]_
> Closed questions typically deepen withdrawal rather than opening it in a client already going one-word.
> **Common mistake:** Reading withdrawal as a data problem instead of a relational one — the wrong lever entirely.

**Rule:** Client withdrawal → tentatively name what you observe and invite their perspective before shifting technique.

**Reference:** Alliance repair / immediacy

---

### 31. nbs31 · domain: ethics · category: Ethics · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

A counselor realizes mid-session that a new client is the estranged sibling of a current client. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Immediately terminate the current client’s treatment with no explanation to keep both cases entirely separate  _[-2 harmful]_
> Abrupt termination without a transition plan constitutes abandonment of an ongoing client.
> **Common mistake:** Rushing to a termination without a transition plan — solving a conflict by creating abandonment.

**B. [ ]** Continue the session as if nothing were unusual because you plan to treat both clients equally throughout  _[-1 novice error]_
> The conflict is real regardless of intent to treat equally; ignoring it violates the ethics framework.
> **Common mistake:** Reading impartial intent as absolution — a common misreading of the dual-relationship standard.

**C. [✔]** Pause the session, disclose the potential conflict of interest, and consult before proceeding any further  _[+3 KEY]_
> A dual-relationship risk requires disclosure and consultation before continuing the therapeutic contact.

**D. [ ]** Complete this session in full and consult before the next scheduled session with the new client afterward  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right in outline, wrong timing — pausing now protects both clients better than finishing then consulting.
> **Common mistake:** Right ethical process but delayed a session too long — momentum treated as more important than care.

**Rule:** Dual-relationship discovery mid-session → disclose, pause, consult; do not continue or terminate without a plan.

**Reference:** ACA A.5 / dual relationships

---

### 32. nbs32 · domain: ethics · category: Ethics · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

A client asks the counselor to write a letter exaggerating their symptoms to support a disability claim. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Refuse to write any documentation at all so the counselor stays entirely uninvolved in the disability claim  _[-1 novice error]_
> Refusing all documentation is unnecessarily restrictive; an accurate letter is appropriate to offer.
> **Common mistake:** Overcorrecting to blanket refusal — the ethics rule addresses fraud, not honest documentation.

**B. [ ]** Suggest the client ask their own psychiatrist to write the same exaggerated letter for the disability claim  _[0 near-miss]_
> Delegating to deflect does not resolve the ethics issue and could pull another clinician into the same request.
> **Common mistake:** Handing off an ethical decision to another clinician instead of resolving it in this relationship.

**C. [ ]** Write the letter as requested since strong advocacy for the client’s benefits is part of the counselor role  _[-2 harmful]_
> Advocacy does not extend to fraud regardless of intent; this crosses documentation and legal lines.
> **Common mistake:** Confusing advocacy with fraud — extending the counselor role well beyond what ethics permits.

**D. [✔]** Decline to falsify the documentation and offer to write a clinically accurate letter for the same claim  _[+3 KEY]_
> Falsifying documentation is fraud and an ethics violation; an accurate letter is the appropriate alternative.

**Rule:** Request to falsify records → decline fraud; offer accurate documentation; do not simply refer away.

**Reference:** ACA C.6.b / documentation ethics

---

### 33. nbs33 · domain: treatment · category: Trauma · rung: 3 (Stabilization)

A client with PTSD begins flooding with trauma material in the second session before any stabilization work has been done. Next best step?

**A. [✔]** Slow the pace, use grounding, and redirect toward stabilization before any trauma processing continues today  _[+3 KEY]_
> Phase-oriented trauma care: stabilization must precede processing to prevent retraumatization.

**B. [ ]** Redirect to an entirely different topic today so you can avoid the trauma content until a later session  _[-1 novice error]_
> Full avoidance abandons the clinical moment; grounding and redirect toward stabilization is more skillful.
> **Common mistake:** Confusing avoidance with pacing — over-correcting to a full topic change instead of grounding.

**C. [ ]** Allow the client’s narrative to continue in full because their willingness now means they are ready to process  _[-2 harmful]_
> Client willingness does not equal clinical readiness; letting flooding continue produces retraumatization.
> **Common mistake:** Reading willingness as readiness — a criterion reversal that endangers the client in the room.

**D. [ ]** Refer the client immediately for EMDR because trauma processing is what they clearly need at this point  _[0 near-miss]_
> EMDR is a real trauma option, but referring during flooding shifts the problem rather than solving it now.
> **Common mistake:** Right modality routed off — the immediate stabilization is not something you can outsource in real time.

**Rule:** Trauma flooding before stabilization → ground, slow, redirect; never process before the client has coping resources.

**Reference:** Phase-oriented trauma care

---

### 34. nbs34 · domain: counseling · category: Trauma · rung: 4 (Alliance and validation)

A veteran client with PTSD refuses to discuss the trauma directly and says, "I just need to move on." Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Agree to permanently avoid the trauma content and focus only on symptoms as the client’s complete treatment plan  _[-2 harmful]_
> Over-accommodating avoidance forecloses on any future path to trauma resolution and does the client harm.
> **Common mistake:** Overcorrecting to permanent avoidance — closing off indicated treatment the client may need later.

**B. [✔]** Respect the current position, explore functional goals, and build alliance before revisiting trauma processing  _[+3 KEY]_
> Forcing trauma processing without alliance replicates the helplessness of trauma; goals and alliance first.

**C. [ ]** Focus on symptom management and daily functioning goals while gently leaving space to revisit trauma later  _[0 near-miss]_
> Very close — same direction, but the correct version explicitly names alliance-building as the mechanism.
> **Common mistake:** Right practical plan but missing the alliance-building rationale that guides the whole approach.

**D. [ ]** Confront the avoidance directly because clinical avoidance is what maintains chronic PTSD symptoms over time  _[-1 novice error]_
> Confrontation without alliance triggers defensive entrenchment, not engagement, and misapplies exposure logic.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an exposure-logic framework to a moment that calls for alliance work instead.

**Rule:** Client refusing trauma processing → honor current readiness; build alliance and functional goals; revisit when trust is established.

**Reference:** Trauma-informed care / client autonomy

---

### 35. nbs35 · domain: core · category: Cultural · rung: 4 (Alliance and validation)

A client from a collectivist background says their family disagrees with the counselor’s recommendation that they live independently. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Explain to the family why independence is clinically indicated for this client so they will support the goal  _[-2 harmful]_
> Imposing a culturally specific goal on the family doubles down on the original cultural competence error.
> **Common mistake:** Doubling down on a culturally imposed goal — using the counselor's authority against the family.

**B. [ ]** Re-open the goal-setting conversation and check whether the current goal fits the client’s stated values  _[0 near-miss]_
> Same direction — but doesn’t explicitly bring cultural context in, which is the point in this vignette.
> **Common mistake:** Right process but missing the cultural-context lens that is the point of this vignette.

**C. [✔]** Explore what independence actually means within the client’s cultural context before recommending it as a goal  _[+3 KEY]_
> Western individualism is not universally appropriate; explore cultural meaning before imposing a framework.

**D. [ ]** Continue the goal as originally set because the client is the identified client, not the client’s family  _[-1 novice error]_
> Ignoring the cultural and systemic context undermines culturally competent care and is a framework error.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an individualist framework as if it were culturally neutral — a common competence gap.

**Rule:** Western goal conflicts with collectivist values → explore cultural meaning first; never impose independence as the default good.

**Reference:** Cultural competence / MSJCC

---

### 36. nbs36 · domain: ethics · category: Cultural · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

A client who is undocumented expresses fear about their status and asks whether therapy records could be used against them. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Explain confidentiality in general terms and offer a legal referral for the immigration questions themselves  _[0 near-miss]_
> Legal referral is a good adjunct, but the counselor still owes accurate specifics about the record itself.
> **Common mistake:** Deferring to a legal referral for questions the counselor is required to answer directly.

**B. [ ]** Reassure the client that therapy records are totally private with no exceptions of any kind in any situation  _[-2 harmful]_
> False assurance of absolute confidentiality is dishonest and may cause real harm if records are subpoenaed.
> **Common mistake:** Making a false absolute promise about confidentiality — informed consent is directly violated.

**C. [ ]** Avoid documenting anything sensitive in the file so records cannot be used against the client in the future  _[-1 novice error]_
> Failing to document appropriately creates liability and is not a sanctioned response to a records question.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a records concern as a chart-hiding problem — an under-documentation framework error.

**D. [✔]** Explain the limits of confidentiality honestly and clarify what a subpoena or law-enforcement request requires  _[+3 KEY]_
> Clients have a right to accurate information about their records; honest disclosure supports informed consent.

**Rule:** Undocumented client asks about records → give accurate, honest information about confidentiality limits; do not falsely reassure.

**Reference:** ACA B.1 / informed consent

---

### 37. nbs37 · domain: treatment · category: Depressive · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A client with MDD has responded well to CBT but tells you they have run out of medication and cannot afford to refill it. Next best step?

**A. [✔]** Help the client access medication assistance (patient-assistance program, community health, sliding-scale)  _[+3 KEY]_
> Advocacy and resource navigation are within counselor scope; abrupt discontinuation is a real clinical risk.

**B. [ ]** Increase session frequency this month to compensate for the interruption in the medication regimen for now  _[0 near-miss]_
> More therapy is a reasonable stopgap but does not substitute for medication in moderate-severe MDD.
> **Common mistake:** Right supportive impulse but wrong lever — therapy dose treated as a substitute for pharmacotherapy.

**C. [ ]** Focus only on the therapy side of care because medication access is entirely the psychiatrist’s domain  _[-1 novice error]_
> Dismissing an urgent practical access barrier undermines effective integrated care and misuses scope.
> **Common mistake:** Reading care coordination as out-of-scope — narrowing the counselor role too far to help the client.

**D. [ ]** Discharge the client and refer them to a community mental-health center to handle their whole treatment plan  _[-2 harmful]_
> Discharging a stable, responding client over an access barrier is unnecessarily disruptive and harmful.
> **Common mistake:** Discharging over a solvable access barrier — abandonment presented as a referral.

**Rule:** Client cannot afford medication → advocate for access (PAP, sliding scale); don’t ignore a real clinical risk by staying in your lane too narrowly.

**Reference:** Advocacy / integrated care

---

### 38. nbs38 · domain: treatment · category: Anxiety · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A client with social anxiety says their goal is to "feel less anxious at work." As written, this goal is not adequate for treatment planning. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Tell the client the goal is not good enough and ask them to try again on their own before the next session  _[-2 harmful]_
> Criticizing the client’s goal damages the alliance and treats goal-setting as a graded assignment.
> **Common mistake:** Framing a collaborative task as pass/fail feedback — an alliance rupture disguised as clarity.

**B. [✔]** Collaboratively restate the goal as a specific, measurable, behavioral, and time-bound treatment objective  _[+3 KEY]_
> A SMART goal such as "initiate one conversation with a coworker weekly" is trackable and actionable.

**C. [ ]** Set a symptom-reduction goal on GAD-7 instead of the client’s own goal so the target is clearly measurable  _[-1 novice error]_
> Replacing the client’s own goal with a clinician measure is not collaborative goal-setting.
> **Common mistake:** Substituting a clinician-chosen metric for a client-owned goal — collaboration is dropped.

**D. [ ]** Continue with the vague goal but track weekly GAD-7 scores so at least a symptom measure is available now  _[0 near-miss]_
> A symptom measure helps but does not replace collaboratively restated behavioral goals for treatment.
> **Common mistake:** Adding a symptom measure as a workaround instead of doing the collaborative goal-setting itself.

**Rule:** Vague goal → collaboratively make it specific, measurable, behavioral, and time-bound before beginning treatment.

**Reference:** SMART goals / treatment planning

---

### 39. nbs39 · domain: treatment · category: Personality · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client with BPD calls between sessions in distress saying they are thinking about cutting. This is the third call this week. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Refuse the call outright to reinforce that between-session contact is not allowed in the current treatment plan  _[-1 novice error]_
> Refusing during a safety situation misapplies boundary logic and can constitute clinical abandonment.
> **Common mistake:** Applying rigid-boundary logic to a safety call — a framework mismatch that risks abandonment.

**B. [ ]** Terminate the treatment because the client is not complying with the boundaries set around session structure  _[-2 harmful]_
> Terminating a client in distress for boundary-testing behavior is abandonment and clinically harmful.
> **Common mistake:** Using a "non-compliance" frame to abandon a client whose distress is exactly what treatment addresses.

**C. [✔]** Assess current safety, use DBT phone coaching, and address the between-session pattern in the next session  _[+3 KEY]_
> DBT phone coaching is a legitimate part of treatment: assess, use skills, and address the pattern therapeutically.

**D. [ ]** Assess safety on the call and end the call as briefly as possible so the pattern is not reinforced further  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right start, wrong finish — DBT phone coaching also uses skills before hanging up on the client.
> **Common mistake:** Cutting the coaching short in the name of reinforcement — the middle DBT step is skipped.

**Rule:** BPD between-session crisis call → safety first, DBT skills; address the pattern in-session, not by refusing the call.

**Reference:** DBT phone coaching

---

### 40. nbs40 · domain: ethics · category: Crisis · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

After a client makes a credible threat to hurt a named coworker, the client leaves before the session ends. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Document the threat carefully and take no external action since the threat might turn out not to happen  _[-2 harmful]_
> A reasonable belief of a serious threat triggers the duty; certainty of harm is not the standard.
> **Common mistake:** Requiring certainty before acting — a criterion reversal that leaves an identifiable person unwarned.

**B. [ ]** Try to reach the client’s emergency contact first so the family can talk them down before you do anything else  _[0 near-miss]_
> Contacting family may be part of a fuller plan, but it does not satisfy the duty to protect the named victim.
> **Common mistake:** Reasonable coordination that still does not discharge the duty owed to the identifiable third party.

**C. [ ]** Call the client and ask them not to follow through on the threat because you take their safety seriously  _[-1 novice error]_
> Calling the client first is not the legal protective action the statute requires here.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a therapeutic-influence framework where a legal protective-action framework is required.

**D. [✔]** Notify the identifiable potential victim and law enforcement per state law and document each action you take  _[+3 KEY]_
> Once the client has left, the duty to protect the third party is unchanged; Tarasoff steps apply.

**Rule:** Credible specific threat + client has left → Tarasoff duty activates; warn identifiable victim and/or notify law enforcement now.

**Reference:** Tarasoff / duty to protect

---

### 41. nbs41 · domain: intake · category: Neurodevelopmental · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

Parents bring a 7-year-old for "not listening" and failing to complete homework. The counselor suspects ADHD but also sees signs of anxiety. Next best step?

**A. [✔]** Conduct a comprehensive evaluation with rating scales, screeners, developmental history, and a teacher report  _[+3 KEY]_
> ADHD and anxiety overlap; differential diagnosis requires multiple informants and multiple instruments.

**B. [ ]** Start anxiety-focused CBT first because anxiety is more treatable and can be reassessed if progress is slow  _[0 near-miss]_
> Sequencing by treatability without a diagnosis is guessing; a full evaluation guides the sequence.
> **Common mistake:** Sequencing by ease of treatment before completing the differential — a shortcut with real cost.

**C. [ ]** Diagnose ADHD and recommend a stimulant medication trial to a prescriber based on the presenting complaint  _[-2 harmful]_
> Diagnosing and recommending medication without a full evaluation is premature and potentially harmful.
> **Common mistake:** Diagnosing from a single informant and moving toward medication without a full evaluation.

**D. [ ]** Attribute the behavior to inconsistent parenting and begin a parent-management training as first-line care  _[-1 novice error]_
> Jumping to parenting intervention before ruling out a neurodevelopmental condition misses the primary driver.
> **Common mistake:** Using a family-systems frame to explain behavior a neurodevelopmental frame explains better here.

**Rule:** Inattention + possible anxiety in a child → comprehensive multi-informant evaluation first; do not diagnose from parent report alone.

**Reference:** ADHD/anxiety differential

---

### 42. nbs42 · domain: treatment · category: Substance · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client in early recovery from alcohol use disorder mentions they have been drinking NyQuil each night to sleep. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Refer the client to a sleep specialist without addressing the current NyQuil use in this session at all  _[-1 novice error]_
> Referring without addressing the immediate substance issue leaves a relapse risk clearly unmanaged.
> **Common mistake:** Handing off a substance-related risk to a sleep specialist as if it were not a substance issue.

**B. [✔]** Name the alcohol content in OTC cold medications as a relapse risk and explore non-alcohol sleep alternatives  _[+3 KEY]_
> Many OTC medications contain alcohol; this is a common relapse vector in early recovery worth addressing.

**C. [ ]** Require that the client stop taking NyQuil immediately or face discharge from your ongoing treatment plan  _[-2 harmful]_
> Ultimatums without psychoeducation and alternatives are coercive and clinically counterproductive.
> **Common mistake:** Using a coercive contingency instead of psychoeducation and alternatives — an MI framework failure.

**D. [ ]** Explore sleep hygiene and set a follow-up next week to see whether the NyQuil use is still happening then  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right general topic, wrong timing — the alcohol-content risk should be surfaced today, not next week.
> **Common mistake:** Delaying a direct address of a live relapse vector until the next scheduled session.

**Rule:** Early recovery + OTC alcohol-containing products → psychoeducate on alcohol content, explore non-alcohol alternatives; don’t dismiss it.

**Reference:** Relapse prevention / recovery

---

### 43. nbs43 · domain: treatment · category: Eating · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client with bulimia nervosa describes a new pattern of fasting for 3 days after each binge-purge cycle. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Focus this session on the cognitive-behavioral shame content that appears to be driving the fasting behavior  _[-1 novice error]_
> Cognitive work is part of treatment, but medical safety is the first concern given extended fasting.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a cognitive framework when medical safety is the primary ladder rung to address.

**B. [ ]** Praise the client for reducing the binge frequency this week even as the fasting has become more extended  _[-2 harmful]_
> Reframing fasting as progress ignores medical danger and can reinforce restriction as a coping strategy.
> **Common mistake:** Reinforcing a medically dangerous compensatory behavior as improvement — a criterion reversal.

**C. [✔]** Assess for medical complications, increase session frequency, and evaluate whether a higher level of care fits  _[+3 KEY]_
> Extended fasting after purging escalates medical risk (electrolytes, cardiac) and warrants intensification.

**D. [ ]** Continue the current outpatient plan and simply note the new pattern in your progress notes for later review  _[0 near-miss]_
> Documentation matters, but noting without responding to an escalating medical risk is inadequate.
> **Common mistake:** Right documentation impulse but wrong intensity — active escalation is treated as a chart entry.

**Rule:** Escalating restriction in bulimia → medical risk assessment first; consider higher LOC; do not frame restriction as improvement.

**Reference:** Eating disorder level of care

---

### 44. nbs44 · domain: treatment · category: Psychotic · rung: 3 (Stabilization)

A client with schizophrenia, who has been stable on antipsychotics, reports they stopped their medication two weeks ago because "I feel fine." Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Provide psychoeducation on the risk and monitor for prodromal symptoms without contacting the prescriber today  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right content, wrong urgency — the two-week window without meds is exactly when a prescriber contact matters.
> **Common mistake:** Right content, wrong urgency — a prescriber contact is treated as optional in a two-week discontinuation.

**B. [ ]** Accept the client’s decision since they feel fine and appear to be a competent adult making their own choice  _[-1 novice error]_
> Feeling fine two weeks after discontinuation is the prodromal window for relapse, not evidence of safety.
> **Common mistake:** Reading the prodromal-window "feeling fine" as evidence of safety — a criterion misread.

**C. [ ]** Discharge the client and route them to a psychiatrist to handle the entire case and close out your file today  _[-2 harmful]_
> Discharging at the moment of medication non-adherence removes the alliance exactly when it is most needed.
> **Common mistake:** Ending care at the moment care is most needed — abandonment framed as an appropriate referral.

**D. [✔]** Explore the decision without shaming, psychoeducate on discontinuation risk, and contact the prescriber urgently  _[+3 KEY]_
> Non-adherence is the primary driver of relapse in schizophrenia; explore, educate, and coordinate urgently.

**Rule:** Medication non-adherence in schizophrenia → explore, psychoeducate, contact prescriber urgently; feeling fine ≠ safe to discontinue.

**Reference:** Schizophrenia relapse prevention

---

### 45. nbs45 · domain: treatment · category: Sleep · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A client with chronic insomnia asks for a sleep aid prescription. As a counselor (not a prescriber), next best step?

**A. [✔]** Introduce CBT-I (the evidence-based first-line treatment) and explain why it outperforms hypnotics long-term  _[+3 KEY]_
> CBT-I has stronger long-term outcomes than sleep medications for chronic insomnia and is a counseling role.

**B. [ ]** Tell the client this request lies outside your scope and end the conversation about sleep in this session  _[-2 harmful]_
> Sleep treatment is inside counseling scope; CBT-I is a counseling intervention, not a prescription.
> **Common mistake:** Using a false scope claim to decline an intervention counselors are trained and expected to offer.

**C. [ ]** Refer the client to a physician for a prescription and do not offer any counseling approach in this session  _[-1 novice error]_
> Referring alone without offering CBT-I means the client never hears about the evidence-based counseling option.
> **Common mistake:** Framing this as out-of-scope when CBT-I is a first-line counseling intervention for the diagnosis.

**D. [ ]** Agree that medication is the most effective approach for chronic insomnia and support the requested referral  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right about medication as one option, wrong about it being best — long-term outcomes favor CBT-I clearly.
> **Common mistake:** Endorsing a client preference as clinically best when the evidence favors a different first-line.

**Rule:** Chronic insomnia + prescription request → offer CBT-I first and explain the evidence; refer for medication evaluation only as augmentation.

**Reference:** CBT-I / insomnia first-line treatment

---

### 46. nbs46 · domain: intake · category: Bipolar · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client with known Bipolar I returns saying they feel "amazing" — sleeping only 4 hours, started two businesses, and "everything is coming together." Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Increase therapy frequency to twice weekly for the next two weeks to keep close tabs on the client’s new plans  _[0 near-miss]_
> More contact is right in outline, but the urgent prescriber call and safety assessment must come first.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a mania signal as a "watch closely" moment rather than a same-day escalation moment.

**B. [✔]** Screen for a manic or hypomanic episode, contact the prescriber immediately, and assess current safety today  _[+3 KEY]_
> Racing energy, decreased sleep, and grandiosity in a Bipolar I client is a manic episode until ruled out.

**C. [ ]** Continue supporting the client’s two new business ventures and help plan next steps for both of them today  _[-2 harmful]_
> Supporting mania-driven projects reinforces the episode and can produce large real-world harm.
> **Common mistake:** Reinforcing manic activity — a criterion reversal treating symptoms as strengths.

**D. [ ]** Validate the improved mood because the client has worked hard in therapy and finally feels genuinely well  _[-1 novice error]_
> Validating a manic episode as recovery delays urgent intervention and applies the wrong framework here.
> **Common mistake:** Treating a mania signal as therapy progress — a framework mismatch that delays urgent care.

**Rule:** Bipolar I + decreased sleep + grandiosity + elevated energy → presume mania; prescriber contact urgently; do not validate as wellness.

**Reference:** Mania recognition / Bipolar I

---

### 47. nbs47 · domain: ethics · category: Scope · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

A client asks the counselor to diagnose their adult child who has never been seen by the counselor, based on the client’s description of the child’s behavior. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Offer a hypothesis about the child that the client can share with a clinician who can evaluate the child  _[0 near-miss]_
> Even a "hypothesis" about an unseen third party is closer to a diagnosis than the framework permits.
> **Common mistake:** Softening a third-party diagnosis into a hypothesis rather than declining it outright.

**B. [ ]** Give a tentative provisional diagnosis for the child so the client has something to work with going forward  _[-1 novice error]_
> A tentative label of an unassessed third party still causes harm and applies the wrong framework here.
> **Common mistake:** Treating "tentative" as if it neutralized the ethical issue of diagnosing an unseen person.
