**C. [✔]** Decline to diagnose the absent person and focus on the client’s own experience of the relationship instead  _[+3 KEY]_
> Diagnosing third parties never assessed violates diagnostic integrity and professional ethics.

**D. [ ]** Diagnose with a disclaimer that this is just your professional opinion of the child based on the description  _[-2 harmful]_
> A disclaimer does not make a third-party diagnosis ethical or clinically sound; it just labels the violation.
> **Common mistake:** Using a disclaimer as ethical cover — a violation stays a violation once it lands as a diagnosis.

**Rule:** Request to diagnose an absent third party → decline; focus on the client’s own experience of the relationship.

**Reference:** Diagnostic ethics / scope of practice

---

### 48. nbs48 · domain: counseling · category: Skills · rung: 4 (Alliance and validation)

During a session, a client’s affect suddenly shifts from engaged to flat, and they give one-word answers mid-topic. The counselor notices feeling a subtle heaviness in the room. Next best step?

**A. [ ]** Summarize the session and end the meeting early to give the client space to settle before the next session  _[-2 harmful]_
> Ending early avoids the relational moment rather than working with it and models avoidance for the client.
> **Common mistake:** Escaping a here-and-now moment via early termination — the exact moment the treatment is meant to work with.

**B. [ ]** Reflect the observable change in tone briefly and continue the previous topic while watching for more signals  _[0 near-miss]_
> A brief reflection helps, but not naming the process and not inviting exploration underuses the moment.
> **Common mistake:** Halfway to immediacy — noticing the shift without opening it up for the client to explore.

**C. [ ]** Ask the client directly, "Are you angry at me right now?" so the interpersonal issue is on the table today  _[-1 novice error]_
> A direct accusatory question escalates rather than opens; tentative naming is more skillful here.
> **Common mistake:** Confusing directness with skillfulness — a lead question that narrows the client’s room to respond.

**D. [✔]** Use immediacy — name what you notice in the room right now and invite the client to explore it with you  _[+3 KEY]_
> Immediacy brings the here-and-now shift into the open as therapeutic data instead of ignoring the signal.

**Rule:** Mid-session affect shift → use immediacy; name the shift tentatively and invite exploration before continuing.

**Reference:** Immediacy / here-and-now

---


## What to Assess Next

- **Items:** 41
- **Profile:** decision
- **Served at:** `/assess-next.html`
- Scoring: **+3 key**, **0 near-miss** (partial credit), **-1 novice error**, **-2 harmful**. Every item carries a Priority Ladder rung.

### 1. as1 · domain: intake · category: Safety · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client mentions, for the first time, that "some days I don’t see the point of going on." What should you do next?

**A. [✔]** Administer a structured suicide-risk assessment such as the C-SSRS to characterize ideation, plan, and intent  _[+3 KEY]_
> A first disclosure of possible ideation calls for structured risk assessment before any other next step.

**B. [ ]** Note the disclosure in your chart and continue with the planned session agenda for the rest of the hour  _[-2 harmful]_
> Deferring assessment of possible suicidality is unsafe and misuses documentation as a substitute for care.
> **Common mistake:** Treating a possible suicide disclosure as a chart entry — documentation used to defer action, not to enable it.

**C. [ ]** Administer a PHQ-9, score the depression severity, and use that number to gauge the current suicide risk  _[-1 novice error]_
> A depression screen does not characterize acute suicide risk (plan, intent, means) at all.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a symptom-severity framework as if it were a risk-assessment framework — the wrong instrument entirely.

**D. [ ]** Move straight to building a safety plan with the client without first characterizing the level of risk today  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right instrument in the sequence, wrong order — the risk assessment has to come before the safety plan.
> **Common mistake:** Skipping the assessment step and jumping to the intervention that would follow it — right tool, wrong order.

**Rule:** Possible suicidality → structured risk assessment (C-SSRS) first; the safety plan follows.

**Reference:** C-SSRS / suicide-risk tree

---

### 2. as2 · domain: intake · category: Substance · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client who drinks heavily every day reports tremor and sweating whenever he cuts back since yesterday. What do you assess next?

**A. [ ]** Administer the PHQ-9 for depression and choose next steps based only on the client’s depression severity  _[-2 harmful]_
> A mood screen misses the medical emergency in front of you and applies a wrong framework entirely.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a possible medical emergency as a mood issue — a criterion reversal that endangers the client.

**B. [✔]** Assess withdrawal severity with the CIWA-Ar and arrange a medical evaluation for the emerging withdrawal signs  _[+3 KEY]_
> Emerging alcohol-withdrawal signs require severity assessment and prompt medical management first.

**C. [ ]** Do motivational interviewing about cutting down further and schedule a next session about a week from now  _[-1 novice error]_
> MI does not address the acute physiological risk of alcohol withdrawal in front of you right now.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an ambivalence-focused framework to a medical rule-out moment — a framework mismatch.

**D. [ ]** Administer the AUDIT to screen for an alcohol use disorder before deciding on the next clinical step to take  _[0 near-miss]_
> AUDIT is the right tool for screening the disorder, but it does not gauge the acute medical danger today.
> **Common mistake:** Reasonable screening choice at the wrong moment — right tool, wrong stage in the assessment sequence.

**Rule:** Signs of alcohol/benzo withdrawal → CIWA-Ar + medical evaluation, not a screening questionnaire.

**Reference:** CIWA / withdrawal tree

---

### 3. as3 · domain: intake · category: Neurocognitive · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A 72-year-old is brought in with confusion that began two days ago, fluctuates through the day, and impairs attention. What is the priority assessment?

**A. [ ]** Administer the MoCA and use the score to diagnose an appropriate neurocognitive disorder for this client  _[-1 novice error]_
> Cognitive testing during possible delirium cannot validly establish a dementia diagnosis under any protocol.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a chronic-neurocognitive framework to an acute fluctuating presentation — a criterion mismatch.

**B. [ ]** Complete a mental status exam and reassure the family that things will settle down within the next few days  _[0 near-miss]_
> An MSE has a place in the workup, but it does not address the underlying acute medical possibility here.
> **Common mistake:** Right tool, wrong timing — a bedside exam used in place of the urgent medical rule-out step.

**C. [✔]** Refer for a medical work-up (labs, medication review) to rule out delirium before any other assessment step  _[+3 KEY]_
> Acute plus fluctuating plus inattention is delirium until proven otherwise; medical rule-out comes first.

**D. [ ]** Administer the PHQ-9 for depression and initiate a course of counseling for late-life depression this week  _[-2 harmful]_
> This misreads an acute medical state as depression and can defer needed medical care with real harm.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a possible medical emergency as depression — a criterion reversal missing delirium entirely.

**Rule:** Acute + fluctuating + inattention → medical work-up to rule out delirium before cognitive testing.

**Reference:** Neurocognitive assessment

---

### 4. as4 · domain: intake · category: Mood · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A depressed adult asks to begin treatment for depression. Before you plan treatment, what should you assess?

**A. [ ]** Administer the GAD-7 to check for a co-occurring anxiety disorder that would inform the treatment planning  _[0 near-miss]_
> GAD-7 is a real intake add-on, but it does not answer the bipolar-vs-unipolar question that has to come first.
> **Common mistake:** Right intake instrument at the wrong point in the sequence — a bipolar screen belongs before it.

**B. [ ]** Administer the PHQ-9 to score the current depression severity and begin a course of CBT during that session  _[-1 novice error]_
> Severity scoring skips the crucial bipolar screen that determines what treatment plan is even safe.
> **Common mistake:** Confusing depression severity with depression differential — starting the plan without a differential step.

**C. [ ]** Refer the client for an antidepressant right away so the depressive symptoms can begin to lift immediately  _[-2 harmful]_
> Endorsing an antidepressant before screening for hypomania risks a manic switch and is outside scope.
> **Common mistake:** Endorsing a pharmacologic plan with switch risk that also lies outside a counselor's scope entirely.

**D. [✔]** Screen for a history of hypomania or mania using an instrument such as the MDQ before treatment planning  _[+3 KEY]_
> Screening for bipolarity before treating depression prevents a manic switch and changes the whole plan.

**Rule:** Screen for hypomania before treating depression (bipolar screen first).

**Reference:** Mood assessment sequencing

---

### 5. as5 · domain: intake · category: Psychotic · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A young adult presents with new-onset hallucinations and disorganized thinking. What is the first assessment step?

**A. [✔]** Obtain a medical and substance work-up (labs, tox screen) to rule out organic causes of the new psychosis  _[+3 KEY]_
> Substance and medical causes must be excluded before diagnosing a primary psychotic disorder in a young adult.

**B. [ ]** Diagnose schizophrenia now and begin CBT for psychosis in the counseling relationship starting this week  _[-1 novice error]_
> Duration and medical rule-outs are not established; diagnosing schizophrenia now applies the wrong framework.
> **Common mistake:** Jumping to a chronic psychotic diagnosis before rule-outs — a framework applied out of order.

**C. [ ]** Administer a comprehensive personality inventory such as the MMPI-2 to characterize the presentation broadly  _[0 near-miss]_
> A personality inventory has an intake role but is irrelevant and premature in an acute psychotic presentation.
> **Common mistake:** Right family of instrument at the wrong moment — an acute rule-out belongs before broad testing.

**D. [ ]** Administer the PHQ-9 to score depression severity and treat the psychosis with a course of behavioral therapy  _[-2 harmful]_
> A depression screen does not address the psychotic presentation and misses the medical differential.
> **Common mistake:** Substituting a depression framework for a psychosis workup — a criterion reversal that misses the diagnosis.

**Rule:** New psychosis → rule out substances/medical causes before a primary psychotic diagnosis.

**Reference:** Psychotic spectrum assessment

---

### 6. as6 · domain: intake · category: Eating · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A 17-year-old with restrictive eating is at a very low body weight and reports dizziness. What should you assess first?

**A. [ ]** Administer the EDE-Q as the primary assessment tool and begin a course of CBT-E on the presenting problem  _[-1 novice error]_
> Starting the psychological work skips the medical safety assessment and applies the wrong sequencing here.
> **Common mistake:** Starting psychotherapy for a medically at-risk eating disorder without the medical rule-out first.

**B. [✔]** Obtain vital signs, orthostatic BP/HR, and basic labs — a medical assessment for stability before any next step  _[+3 KEY]_
> Medical stability must be established before any psychological assessment or treatment in low-weight eating disorders.

**C. [ ]** Administer the SCOFF eating-disorder screen and schedule weekly outpatient sessions with the same clinician  _[0 near-miss]_
> A brief screen has an intake place, but it does not capture the acute medical risk that low weight signals.
> **Common mistake:** Using a brief screen at a moment when the medical safety data is the more urgent piece of information.

**D. [ ]** Reassure the client that low weight and dizziness are common and defer any specialty assessment for now  _[-2 harmful]_
> Reassuring away a set of medical red flags in a low-weight eating disorder can result in real physical harm.
> **Common mistake:** Reassuring away a medical warning pattern — a criterion reversal that endangers the client.

**Rule:** Low-weight eating disorder → vitals, orthostatics, and labs before psychosocial assessment.

**Reference:** Eating disorder assessment

---

### 7. as7 · domain: intake · category: Neurodevelopmental · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

You want to characterize an adult client’s attention symptoms and rule ADHD in or out during the assessment. What is the best strategy?

**A. [ ]** Diagnose ADHD from a single office observation with no rating scale, developmental history, or informant data  _[-2 harmful]_
> A single-visit diagnosis without validated data violates the assessment standard and risks a wrong diagnosis.
> **Common mistake:** Diagnosing from surface impression alone — the assessment framework for ADHD is entirely skipped.

**B. [ ]** Administer the PHQ-9 to score depression severity and stop the workup once you have that score in hand  _[-1 novice error]_
> Screening for depression does not assess the ADHD question at all and applies the wrong instrument here.
> **Common mistake:** Using a mood screen as if it addressed the attention differential — a framework mismatch.

**C. [✔]** Use a validated adult ADHD scale such as the ASRS and gather a developmental history and collateral report  _[+3 KEY]_
> Adult ADHD assessment requires validated symptom rating plus developmental history and collateral evidence.

**D. [ ]** Base the diagnosis on the client’s current self-report of attention problems from this session alone  _[0 near-miss]_
> Self-report has a real role, but ADHD assessment additionally needs a developmental history and collateral.
> **Common mistake:** Right source of data but not enough of it — collateral and history rounds out the picture.

**Rule:** Adult ADHD → validated scale (e.g., ASRS) + developmental history + collateral.

**Reference:** ADHD assessment

---

### 8. as8 · domain: intake · category: Neurodevelopmental · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

Parents report their 8-year-old is inattentive and disruptive. What is the best assessment strategy?

**A. [ ]** Administer an adult depression scale to the child and base the treatment plan on the score you obtain  _[-2 harmful]_
> Wrong instrument and wrong target — this applies an unrelated framework to a childhood behavior problem.
> **Common mistake:** Using an adult mood scale to answer a child behavioral question — an instrument-and-target mismatch.

**B. [ ]** Base the assessment on a single office observation of the child’s behavior during today’s visit only  _[0 near-miss]_
> A live observation is a real data point, but one setting cannot establish the cross-situational pattern.
> **Common mistake:** Taking one observation as the pattern — cross-setting data is the framework this diagnosis needs.

**C. [ ]** Rely on the child’s own self-report of symptoms as the primary source for the current assessment step  _[-1 novice error]_
> Young children are poor sole reporters of their own behavior; the framework needs multiple informants.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an adult-style self-report framework to a young child — the informants do not match.

**D. [✔]** Collect multi-informant ratings across settings (parent and teacher, e.g., the Vanderbilt scales) with history  _[+3 KEY]_
> Child behavioral assessment requires cross-setting, multi-informant data to establish the pattern.

**Rule:** Child behavior → multi-informant, cross-setting rating scales.

**Reference:** Child assessment

---

### 9. as9 · domain: intake · category: Trauma · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A client has a Criterion-A trauma history and you have established current safety. What do you administer to assess PTSD?

**A. [✔]** A validated PTSD measure (the PCL-5 self-report; the CAPS-5 clinician interview for a formal diagnosis)  _[+3 KEY]_
> Validated trauma measures assess PTSD; the CAPS-5 is the clinician-administered gold standard for diagnosis.

**B. [ ]** Begin exposure therapy immediately in this same session rather than administering a validated instrument  _[-2 harmful]_
> Exposure treatment before assessment and stabilization is premature and applies the wrong sequence.
> **Common mistake:** Skipping the assessment step and starting exposure — treatment before assessment is the wrong order.

**C. [ ]** Administer the MMSE as the primary assessment tool for characterizing this trauma-exposed client today  _[-1 novice error]_
> A cognitive screen is unrelated to trauma symptom assessment and applies the wrong instrument.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a cognitive framework where a trauma symptom framework belongs — instruments do not match.

**D. [ ]** Administer the GAD-7 to characterize the generalized-anxiety component of the client’s trauma reaction  _[0 near-miss]_
> A generalized-anxiety scale can be a useful add-on but does not itself assess PTSD symptom clusters.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing a related but non-PTSD instrument — the diagnostic question is left unanswered.

**Rule:** PTSD → validated measure (PCL-5 / CAPS-5); CAPS-5 is clinician-administered.

**Reference:** Trauma assessment tools

---

### 10. as10 · domain: intake · category: Anxiety · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A 40-year-old has a first-ever episode of racing heart, chest tightness, and terror. Before diagnosing panic, what should you assess?

**A. [ ]** Administer the PDSS to characterize severity and use the score to proceed to a panic treatment plan today  _[0 near-miss]_
> A severity scale is a real tool, but it assumes the diagnosis you have not yet actually confirmed.
> **Common mistake:** Skipping the differential and going straight to a severity measure — assuming the diagnosis.

**B. [✔]** Rule out medical causes such as thyroid dysfunction, cardiac issues, caffeine, or substances before diagnosing  _[+3 KEY]_
> Medical mimics of panic must be excluded before a first-time panic-disorder diagnosis is applied.

**C. [ ]** Diagnose panic disorder in this session and begin a course of exposure-based CBT for panic starting today  _[-1 novice error]_
> Diagnosing before ruling out medical causes is premature and applies the wrong sequencing framework here.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a psychiatric framework before medical rule-outs — a framework applied out of order.

**D. [ ]** Refer the client for a standing daily benzodiazepine as the primary treatment plan for the panic symptoms  _[-2 harmful]_
> This is a treatment step, not an assessment step — and it is the wrong treatment step at that.
> **Common mistake:** Recommending a standing pharmacologic plan where a differential belongs — treatment before assessment.

**Rule:** First-presentation panic → rule out medical/substance mimics before diagnosing.

**Reference:** Anxiety assessment

---

### 11. as11 · domain: intake · category: Assessment skills · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

You want to track a depressed client’s symptom severity across sessions in routine outpatient care. Best tool?

**A. [ ]** HAM-D at every session so the counselor gets a clinician-rated depression score after each meeting occurs  _[0 near-miss]_
> The HAM-D is a valid measure but is clinician-administered, longer, and used mainly in research or inpatient settings.
> **Common mistake:** Selecting a research-grade measure for routine outpatient monitoring — right family, wrong setting.

**B. [ ]** Informal check-in only with no formal measure and rely on clinical impression to track change across sessions  _[-2 harmful]_
> Measurement-based care outperforms clinical impressions for tracking change; dropping the measure risks drift.
> **Common mistake:** Skipping measurement-based care in favor of impression — a framework the evidence does not support.

**C. [✔]** PHQ-9 (brief self-report) administered at consistent intervals across the course of the outpatient treatment  _[+3 KEY]_
> The PHQ-9 is a practical self-report measure well-suited to repeated monitoring in outpatient depression care.

**D. [ ]** MoCA administered each session to characterize any cognitive changes that come with the depressive episode  _[-1 novice error]_
> A cognitive screen does not track depression severity and applies the wrong instrument to the wrong target.
> **Common mistake:** Mismatching instrument and target — MoCA measures cognition, not depressive symptom severity here.

**Rule:** PHQ-9 = brief self-report for monitoring; HAM-D is clinician-rated (know the difference).

**Reference:** Depression measures

---

### 12. as12 · domain: intake · category: Neurocognitive · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

You want to screen for mild cognitive impairment in an older adult with subtle memory complaints. Best instrument?

**A. [ ]** MMSE only, because it is the traditional cognitive screen and covers the same domains as the MoCA does today  _[0 near-miss]_
> The MMSE is a real cognitive screen but is less sensitive to mild impairment and executive dysfunction here.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing a related instrument with lower sensitivity for the exact case type presented today.

**B. [ ]** PHQ-9 as the primary cognitive screen and rule out pseudodementia before any further neurocognitive workup  _[-1 novice error]_
> A depression screen does not assess cognition; pseudodementia is a differential but not a cognitive screen.
> **Common mistake:** Substituting a mood screen for a cognitive screen — the target and the instrument do not match up.

**C. [ ]** No screening at all — reassure the client that mild forgetting is normal aging and monitor over the next year  _[-2 harmful]_
> Subtle complaints warrant an objective cognitive screen, not reassurance alone at this stage of assessment.
> **Common mistake:** Reassuring away a screen indication — a criterion reversal that can miss reversible causes early.

**D. [✔]** MoCA — more sensitive to mild cognitive impairment than the MMSE and appropriate for subtle presentations  _[+3 KEY]_
> The MoCA detects mild cognitive impairment and executive dysfunction that the MMSE often misses.

**Rule:** For mild impairment, the MoCA is more sensitive than the MMSE.

**Reference:** Cognitive screening

---

### 13. as13 · domain: intake · category: Substance · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

During intake, a client’s answers suggest possible problem drinking, but there is no acute withdrawal. What do you administer?

**A. [✔]** A brief screening tool such as the AUDIT, or the CAGE or DAST as an appropriate substance-use screener  _[+3 KEY]_
> AUDIT/CAGE/DAST are the standard screening instruments for identifying substance-use problems at intake.

**B. [ ]** CIWA-Ar as the initial screening tool for this client’s substance use during the intake session with them  _[-1 novice error]_
> CIWA measures acute withdrawal severity, not screening for a use problem; the instrument does not fit.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing an in-family but wrong-purpose instrument — CIWA is for withdrawal, not screening.

**C. [ ]** Administer the PHQ-9 for depression and rely on the resulting score to decide whether alcohol is a concern  _[-2 harmful]_
> A mood screen does not assess the substance question and misses the presenting concern entirely here.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a substance concern as a mood concern — an instrument-and-target mismatch.

**D. [ ]** Diagnose an alcohol-use disorder in this session without formally counting the DSM-5 criteria for the diagnosis  _[0 near-miss]_
> The right diagnosis category, but jumping to it without counting criteria collapses assessment and diagnosis.
> **Common mistake:** Right category, wrong process — a hunch-based diagnosis skipping the DSM-5 criteria count.

**Rule:** Screen substance use with AUDIT/CAGE/DAST; CIWA is for acute withdrawal, not screening.

**Reference:** Substance screening

---

### 14. as14 · domain: intake · category: Assessment skills · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

It is the first session with a new client. Before any standardized tools, what grounds your assessment?

**A. [ ]** Diagnose from the intake paperwork alone by scoring the forms and giving the client the resulting diagnosis  _[-1 novice error]_
> Forms do not substitute for a clinical assessment; framework and setting for a diagnosis are missing here.
> **Common mistake:** Reading intake forms as diagnostic evidence in themselves — the clinical interview is skipped.

**B. [✔]** A clinical interview with a mental status exam that grounds the picture before any standardized instruments  _[+3 KEY]_
> The interview and MSE are the structured foundation that guides which standardized tools to use next.

**C. [ ]** Skip formal assessment entirely and begin an intervention based on the presenting complaint at intake today  _[-2 harmful]_
> Intervening before any assessment misapplies the standard of care and can produce a wrong plan.
> **Common mistake:** Skipping assessment altogether — treatment planned without the interview or MSE that guides it.

**D. [ ]** Immediately hand the client a full battery of self-report questionnaires so nothing important gets missed today  _[0 near-miss]_
> A battery has a place after the interview, but leading with instruments before the picture is premature.
> **Common mistake:** Leading with instruments before the clinical picture — right tool, wrong point in the sequence.

**Rule:** The clinical interview + MSE ground assessment and point to the right instruments.

**Reference:** MSE / intake

---

### 15. as15 · domain: intake · category: Mood · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A client became tearful and withdrawn after a parent’s recent death. What is the appropriate assessment focus?

**A. [ ]** Diagnose MDD and begin an antidepressant referral without further characterizing the specific presentation  _[-2 harmful]_
> Diagnosing without assessing distinguishing features and jumping to medication is premature here.
> **Common mistake:** Skipping the differential and going straight to a pharmacologic plan — assessment stage bypassed.

**B. [ ]** Administer the PHQ-9 and use the score alone as the primary basis for making the diagnostic decision today  _[0 near-miss]_
> A score can inform the picture, but a number alone cannot separate grief from a major depressive episode.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a score as a diagnosis — grief and MDD overlap on the same instrument at similar totals.

**C. [✔]** Assess time course, functional impairment, and specific depressive features such as worthlessness or SI  _[+3 KEY]_
> Distinguishing normal grief from MDD depends on course, function, and the specific depressive symptoms.

**D. [ ]** Assume the presentation will pass on its own and defer any structured assessment for at least six months  _[-1 novice error]_
> Some bereaved clients do develop MDD, and assessment is warranted; the framework of "watchful waiting" is thin.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a wait-and-see framework where structured assessment is warranted — an under-response.

**Rule:** Grief vs MDD → assess course, function, and specific depressive symptoms, not just a score.

**Reference:** Mood differential assessment

---

### 16. as16 · domain: ethics · category: Ethics & scope · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

A cognitively impaired adult’s ability to consent to treatment is unclear, and no guardian is in place. What do you do?

**A. [ ]** Proceed with treatment for now and document that the client seemed to understand the discussion in session  _[0 near-miss]_
> Documentation matters, but an informal impression is insufficient when capacity is genuinely in question.
> **Common mistake:** Substituting a chart note for a required formal evaluation — impression treated as capacity finding.

**B. [ ]** Defer all care indefinitely and provide nothing until an outside decision-maker appears on their own accord  _[-2 harmful]_
> Indefinite delay is not the answer; the client can still be involved to the extent possible during assessment.
> **Common mistake:** Turning a capacity question into an indefinite denial of care — over-restrictive misuse of consent.

**C. [ ]** Have any available family member sign for the client without any formal capacity evaluation on file today  _[-1 novice error]_
> A relative is not automatically a legal decision-maker, and this misapplies the informed-consent framework.
> **Common mistake:** Reading familial closeness as legal authority — a framework mismatch on informed consent.

**D. [✔]** Obtain a formal capacity evaluation before proceeding with any treatment that depends on informed consent  _[+3 KEY]_
> When capacity is unclear and no proxy exists, a formal evaluation protects both the client and the clinician.

**Rule:** Unclear capacity, no proxy → formal capacity evaluation before consent-dependent treatment.

**Reference:** Informed consent / capacity

---

### 17. as17 · domain: ethics · category: Ethics & scope · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

A teen makes an ambiguous comment that could indicate abuse. What is the appropriate assessment step?

**A. [✔]** Gently clarify the disclosure only to the point needed to determine whether reasonable suspicion is present  _[+3 KEY]_
> You assess to the threshold of reasonable suspicion — that is the trigger for a mandated report.

**B. [ ]** Investigate the family situation yourself before deciding anything and hold off on filing a report for now  _[-1 novice error]_
> You do not investigate; you report on reasonable suspicion, and the framework here is applied incorrectly.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an investigator framework to a mandated-reporter role — the two are separate.

**C. [ ]** Wait for clear proof of abuse before pursuing any further assessment of the ambiguous statement made today  _[-2 harmful]_
> The threshold is suspicion, not proof, and waiting for certainty violates the mandated-reporter standard.
> **Common mistake:** Requiring proof — a criminal-court standard misapplied to a mandated-reporter standard.

**D. [ ]** Conduct a detailed forensic interview of the child yourself before making any mandated-report decision today  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right instinct to find out more, wrong role — forensic interviewing is the investigating agency's job.
> **Common mistake:** Assuming an investigating-agency role — right instinct to clarify, wrong depth of interview.

**Rule:** Assess only to the point of reasonable suspicion — you don’t investigate or wait for proof.

**Reference:** Mandated reporting

---

### 18. as18 · domain: ethics · category: Ethics & scope · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

You need records from a client’s previous provider to inform your assessment. What comes first?

**A. [ ]** Have the client verbally okay the request in session and skip the written form so the process moves quickly  _[0 near-miss]_
> Verbal consent is close but the documented signed release is the standard and what a provider expects.
> **Common mistake:** Treating verbal agreement as an ROI — right idea, wrong documentation for the consent standard.

**B. [✔]** Obtain a signed release of information from the client before requesting any records from the prior provider  _[+3 KEY]_
> Collateral records require the client's written consent through a signed release of information first.

**C. [ ]** Call the prior provider and ask for the entire chart before any consent has been obtained from the client  _[-2 harmful]_
> Contacting collaterals without consent breaches confidentiality and is a serious ethics violation.
> **Common mistake:** Contacting a collateral without a release — an ethics violation on the confidentiality standard.

**D. [ ]** Proceed with the assessment without seeking any records at all so you can avoid any confidentiality concerns  _[-1 novice error]_
> Useful collateral is worth obtaining — with proper consent; skipping it is under-utilizing available data.
> **Common mistake:** Avoiding collateral to avoid the paperwork — an under-utilization of a legitimate assessment step.

**Rule:** Collateral/records → signed release of information first.

**Reference:** Consent / records

---

### 19. as19 · domain: intake · category: Anxiety · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

You want a brief measure to track a client’s generalized anxiety severity over time. Best tool?

**A. [ ]** No measure at all — clinical impression will track anxiety severity over time without measurement-based care  _[-2 harmful]_
> A brief validated measure tracks change better than impression alone; dropping measurement risks real drift.
> **Common mistake:** Discarding measurement-based care in favor of impression — a framework the evidence does not support.

**B. [ ]** PHQ-9 to track anxiety severity over time using the same instrument the client already fills out each session  _[-1 novice error]_
> The PHQ-9 tracks depression, not anxiety severity, and applies the wrong instrument here.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a familiar depression instrument as anxiety-appropriate — a target-and-instrument mismatch.

**C. [✔]** GAD-7 administered at consistent intervals across the course of the treatment to track change over time  _[+3 KEY]_
> The GAD-7 is the brief self-report measure for tracking generalized-anxiety severity over time.

**D. [ ]** PDSS administered at similar intervals as an alternative brief anxiety measure for a broad anxiety picture  _[0 near-miss]_
> PDSS is a valid anxiety measure, but it is specific to panic and not to generalized-anxiety severity.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing an in-family instrument whose target is a different anxiety disorder than the one asked about.

**Rule:** GAD-7 for generalized-anxiety severity; PHQ-9 is for depression; PDSS for panic.

**Reference:** Anxiety measures

---

### 20. as20 · domain: treatment · category: Safety · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

You have completed a structured suicide-risk assessment and the client is at moderate risk but can stay safe with support. What next?

**A. [ ]** Re-administer the C-SSRS repeatedly this session instead of moving directly into the intervention right now  _[0 near-miss]_
> Reassessment has a role, but you have already assessed the risk — the next step is to act on it today.
> **Common mistake:** Repeating the assessment step when the plan step is what the risk level actually calls for now.

**B. [ ]** End the session with routine follow-up next week and rely on that schedule to manage the moderate risk today  _[-1 novice error]_
> Moderate risk warrants an active safety plan, not routine follow-up alone; the response undercorrects here.
> **Common mistake:** Under-responding to moderate risk — treating "moderate" as if it needed no active intervention today.

**C. [ ]** Have the client sign a written no-suicide contract and rely on that written agreement to keep them safe today  _[-2 harmful]_
> No-suicide contracts are not evidence-based and can create a false sense of safety at moderate risk.
> **Common mistake:** Using a discredited technique in place of a safety plan — right intent, wrong evidence base.

**D. [✔]** Collaboratively build a safety plan such as the Stanley-Brown plan and address means restriction with the client  _[+3 KEY]_
> After risk is assessed, the collaborative safety plan and means restriction are the appropriate next steps.

**Rule:** After risk assessment → collaborative safety plan (Stanley-Brown) + means restriction; never a contract.

**Reference:** Stanley-Brown SPI

---

### 21. as21 · domain: intake · category: Eating · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

During a routine intake you want to briefly screen for a possible eating disorder. Best tool?

**A. [✔]** SCOFF — the brief eating-disorder screening instrument suitable for a fast intake screen with any adult client  _[+3 KEY]_
> SCOFF is the short screening instrument for eating disorders and fits a brief-screen use case at intake.

**B. [ ]** PHQ-9 alone to screen for any eating-disorder possibility because mood problems often co-occur with these  _[-2 harmful]_
> A depression screen does not screen for eating disorders and misses the presenting concern entirely.
> **Common mistake:** Using a mood screen as if it were an eating-disorder screen — instrument and target do not match.

**C. [ ]** No screening — most people are not going to have a diagnosable eating disorder anyway on this intake today  _[-1 novice error]_
> A brief screen is appropriate when there is any concern; skipping it under-utilizes an available step.
> **Common mistake:** Skipping a screening step by pointing at base rates — an under-utilization of a brief instrument.

**D. [ ]** A full Eating Disorder Examination interview conducted on the spot as the initial intake screening step here  _[0 near-miss]_
> The EDE is a detailed diagnostic interview, not a brief screen — right family, wrong depth at intake.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing a diagnostic interview at a screening moment — right family of instrument, wrong depth.

**Rule:** SCOFF is the brief eating-disorder screen.

**Reference:** Eating disorder screening

---

### 22. as22 · domain: intake · category: Psychotic · rung: 6 (Evidence-based treatment)

A stabilized client on antipsychotics still hears occasional commenting voices. What assessment is most useful now?

**A. [ ]** Conclude that the antipsychotic treatment has failed and coordinate a full medication switch with the prescriber  _[0 near-miss]_
> Residual symptoms are common; the framework here is CBTp response, not automatic medication failure.
> **Common mistake:** Reading residual symptoms as treatment failure — a criterion misread that drives the wrong step.

**B. [✔]** Assess the client’s relationship to the voices — distress, beliefs, and coping — to guide CBT for psychosis  _[+3 KEY]_
> Residual voices are addressed by assessing the person's response to them and using that to inform CBTp.

**C. [ ]** Administer the MMSE to assess whether the antipsychotic is affecting cognition and adjust the plan on that  _[-2 harmful]_
> A cognitive screen does not address residual voices at all; instrument and target do not match here.
> **Common mistake:** Choosing a cognitive instrument to address a psychotic-symptom question — wrong instrument entirely.

**D. [ ]** Re-run a comprehensive tox screen this session on the assumption that the voices signal a substance relapse  _[-1 novice error]_
> Persistent residual symptoms in a stable client do not by themselves imply a substance relapse.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a substance-relapse framework to a residual-symptom presentation — a mismatch.

**Rule:** Residual psychosis → assess the relationship to symptoms to target CBTp coping.

**Reference:** CBT for psychosis

---

### 23. as23 · domain: intake · category: Trauma · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A client discloses distress after an event that does not meet Criterion A, with symptoms below threshold. What is the appropriate assessment posture?

**A. [ ]** Dismiss the distress as not clinically relevant and take no further assessment or monitoring action from here  _[-2 harmful]_
> Distress warrants assessment and monitoring even when sub-threshold; dismissing risks missed escalation.
> **Common mistake:** Dismissing sub-threshold distress — an under-response that can miss later worsening.

**B. [ ]** Administer the PCL-5 and use the resulting score to establish a working diagnosis before the next session  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right instrument for PTSD in general, but the presentation does not meet Criterion A — the framework is off.
> **Common mistake:** Selecting a PTSD measure when Criterion A is not present — right family, wrong applicability today.

**C. [✔]** Assess functioning and monitor the client over time, reassessing if symptoms intensify or the picture changes  _[+3 KEY]_
> Sub-threshold, non-Criterion-A distress is monitored with reassessment rather than immediately labeled.

**D. [ ]** Administer the CAPS-5 and give the client a PTSD diagnosis based on the interview during today’s session  _[-1 novice error]_
> Without a Criterion A event, PTSD does not apply, so this interview cannot legitimately lead to that diagnosis.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a PTSD framework without the anchoring criterion — assessment used to force a diagnosis.

**Rule:** Sub-threshold/non-Criterion-A distress → assess function and monitor, reassess over time.

**Reference:** Trauma assessment

---

### 24. as24 · domain: intake · category: Substance · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client in early alcohol detox needs monitoring. Which tool tracks their withdrawal over time?

**A. [ ]** MoCA administered daily to characterize cognitive changes that accompany alcohol withdrawal in early detox  _[-1 novice error]_
> A cognitive screen does not track withdrawal severity and applies the wrong instrument to the wrong target.
> **Common mistake:** Mismatching instrument and target — cognition is not the withdrawal-severity variable here.

**B. [ ]** AUDIT repeated daily so the counselor has a daily read on the alcohol-use pattern while the client detoxes  _[0 near-miss]_
> AUDIT screens for a use disorder over time; it does not track acute withdrawal severity like the CIWA does.
> **Common mistake:** Using an in-family instrument whose target is the disorder, not the acute withdrawal in the room.

**C. [ ]** PHQ-9 administered daily to score depression severity and use the trajectory to manage the client’s detox  _[-2 harmful]_
> A mood measure does not track withdrawal and misdirects the management of a medical detox setting.
> **Common mistake:** Reading detox management as a depression problem — a serious framework and instrument mismatch.

**D. [✔]** CIWA-Ar administered serially so severity can be tracked across the withdrawal window for management  _[+3 KEY]_
> The CIWA-Ar quantifies and tracks alcohol-withdrawal severity to guide clinical management.

**Rule:** CIWA-Ar tracks alcohol-withdrawal severity; AUDIT is a use-disorder screen.

**Reference:** Withdrawal monitoring

---

### 25. as25 · domain: intake · category: Mood · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A client screens positive on a bipolar screen (past hypomania likely). Before making treatment decisions, what next?

**A. [✔]** Refer for a full diagnostic evaluation to confirm the bipolar picture and coordinate care with a prescriber  _[+3 KEY]_
> A positive screen prompts a fuller diagnostic evaluation before a treatment plan is actually set.

**B. [ ]** Ignore the screen result and treat the client as unipolar depression using standard antidepressant-plus-therapy planning  _[-1 novice error]_
> Disregarding a positive bipolar screen risks the exact error the screen exists to prevent.
> **Common mistake:** Ignoring the very screen result that changes the framework — the screen's purpose is dismissed.

**C. [ ]** Start an antidepressant to relieve the current depression before the bipolarity picture has been confirmed  _[-2 harmful]_
> Antidepressant monotherapy before confirming or stabilizing risks a manic switch and is outside scope.
> **Common mistake:** Endorsing a pharmacologic plan with switch risk in a client whose bipolar picture is not confirmed.

**D. [ ]** Re-administer the same bipolar screen a few more times over successive sessions to see if it stays positive  _[0 near-miss]_
> Repeating a screen has some place, but the next step after a positive screen is a fuller evaluation, not more screening.
> **Common mistake:** Repeating a screening step where the diagnostic evaluation step is what is actually indicated.

**Rule:** Positive bipolar screen → diagnostic evaluation before treatment; don’t start antidepressant monotherapy.

**Reference:** Mood assessment sequencing

---

### 26. as26 · domain: intake · category: Assessment skills · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A client’s presentation includes odd experiences you want to place in cultural context before assessing pathology. What helps most?

**A. [ ]** Rely on your general cultural knowledge of the client’s community and skip a formal cultural interview today  _[0 near-miss]_
> General knowledge helps but is not a substitute for the structured cultural interview the framework provides.
> **Common mistake:** Substituting general cultural knowledge for the structured tool the framework recommends.

**B. [✔]** Use the DSM-5-TR Cultural Formulation Interview to structure cultural context alongside symptom assessment  _[+3 KEY]_
> The CFI structures the assessment of cultural context to avoid over- or under-pathologizing the presentation.

**C. [ ]** Assume everything the client describes is culturally normative and diagnose nothing at all during the assessment  _[-2 harmful]_
> Over-attributing to culture can miss genuine pathology; the framework is not "attribute everything to culture."
> **Common mistake:** Over-correcting to a culture-only framework — the criterion for pathology is dropped entirely.

**D. [ ]** Diagnose from the surface presentation alone and note the cultural context briefly in your assessment write-up  _[-1 novice error]_
> Skipping cultural context risks mislabeling normative experiences as pathology; the framework is under-applied.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a symptom-only framework to a presentation the culture-aware framework specifically targets.

**Rule:** Use the Cultural Formulation Interview to weigh cultural context in assessment.

**Reference:** Cultural assessment

---

### 27. as27 · domain: intake · category: Safety · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client discloses passive suicidal ideation but says they would "never actually do it" because of their children. The counselor should next:

**A. [ ]** Immediately initiate psychiatric hospitalization on the basis of the client’s current SI disclosure alone  _[-1 novice error]_
> Passive SI without plan or intent typically does not warrant hospitalization; but assessment comes first.
> **Common mistake:** Over-triage that skips assessment — treating any SI disclosure as an automatic hospitalization event.

**B. [ ]** Accept the protective factor as sufficient evidence of safety today and move on to the rest of the agenda  _[-2 harmful]_
> Protective factors reduce risk but never eliminate the need for a full assessment of plan, intent, and means.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a protective factor as an absolute guarantee — a criterion reversal that skips assessment.

**C. [✔]** Conduct a full structured risk assessment covering ideation, plan, intent, means, and protective factors present  _[+3 KEY]_
> Protective factors are relevant but do not eliminate the need for a full risk assessment of plan and means.

**D. [ ]** Note the protective factor in the chart and schedule a follow-up in two weeks to monitor risk from a distance  _[0 near-miss]_
> Follow-up matters, but an SI disclosure requires full assessment before the session ends, not distant monitoring.
> **Common mistake:** Right relational tone applied at the wrong intensity — assessment is deferred when it should occur now.

**Rule:** Any SI disclosure → full structured risk assessment; protective factors inform but do not replace it.

**Reference:** Suicide risk assessment

---

### 28. as28 · domain: treatment · category: Safety · rung: 3 (Stabilization)

A client with known PTSD is referred after a traumatic incident two days ago. They are acutely distressed and struggling to function. What do you assess first?

**A. [ ]** A full PTSD symptom checklist (PCL-5) so the acute distress can be characterized against the diagnostic criteria  _[0 near-miss]_
> The PCL-5 requires 30 days post-trauma; symptoms at 48 hours are expected acute stress rather than PTSD.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a PTSD measure to a window where acute stress, not PTSD, is what the criteria describe.

**B. [ ]** Dive into the trauma narrative right now to help the client process what happened while it is still recent  _[-1 novice error]_
> Trauma processing in the acute phase increases the risk of ASD/PTSD rather than reducing it, per evidence.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a processing framework at a stabilization moment — a sequencing error with real risk.

**C. [ ]** Refer the client for EMDR immediately so the processing can begin without waiting past the acute period  _[-2 harmful]_
> EMDR is contraindicated in the acute phase; stabilization has to come first before any processing modality.
> **Common mistake:** Recommending a contraindicated modality — a framework applied where the evidence forbids it.

**D. [✔]** Safety, stabilization, and acute functional capacity before any structured trauma exploration is initiated  _[+3 KEY]_
> In the acute 48-72 hour window, safety and stabilization come first; trauma processing is contraindicated.

**Rule:** Acute trauma (< 72 hrs) → safety and stabilization first; no trauma processing or PCL-5 yet.

**Reference:** Acute trauma / Psychological First Aid

---

### 29. as29 · domain: intake · category: Intake · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A new client arrives appearing disheveled, speaking rapidly, and making grand statements about being chosen to solve climate change. What do you assess first?

**A. [✔]** A mental status exam covering appearance, speech, thought process, thought content, and current insight  _[+3 KEY]_
> A potentially psychotic or manic presentation requires an MSE before any other assessment or formulation.

**B. [ ]** A PHQ-9 depression screen to gauge the current mood state and pick the counseling intervention for this session  _[-2 harmful]_
> The presentation suggests elevated mood or psychosis, not depression; PHQ-9 is the wrong tool here.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a depression instrument to what looks more like mania or psychosis — target mismatch.

**C. [ ]** A substance-use history to rule out intoxication before you characterize the presenting picture with any tool  _[0 near-miss]_
> A substance history is important later, but the MSE has to come first to characterize what you are seeing.
> **Common mistake:** Right intake element ordered wrong — substance history is a step, but not the very first step here.

**D. [ ]** A trauma history to understand the beliefs in context and generate a more culturally sensitive formulation today  _[-1 novice error]_
> Trauma and cultural context matter but the MSE characterizes the acute presentation first in the sequence.
> **Common mistake:** Prioritizing a contextual history where the acute-picture MSE is what the sequence needs first.

**Rule:** Possible psychosis or mania → MSE first; characterize the presentation before screening for cause.

**Reference:** MSE / psychosis evaluation

---

### 30. as30 · domain: intake · category: Intake · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A 16-year-old is brought in by a parent for "attitude problems." The teen refuses to talk in front of the parent. How do you structure the intake?

**A. [ ]** Decline to see the teen at all until they agree to talk in front of the parent for the full session today  _[-1 novice error]_
> Coercive pre-conditions fail to engage the actual client and misapply the adolescent-alliance framework.
> **Common mistake:** Making session terms a condition of care — a framework that undoes the alliance the intake needs.

**B. [✔]** Meet with the teen separately first after explaining confidentiality and its limits, then meet with the parent  _[+3 KEY]_
> Adolescent engagement requires private time; explain confidentiality to both and gather data from each.

**C. [ ]** Conduct the full intake with both present because parents have the right to all information about their teen  _[-2 harmful]_
> Minors in therapy retain confidentiality rights within age and safety limits; this structure silences the teen.
> **Common mistake:** Treating parental access as unlimited — the adolescent's confidentiality rights are misread.

**D. [ ]** See the parent alone first for the full history and then meet the teen briefly at the end of the intake hour  _[0 near-miss]_
> Some parent-alone time has a place, but excluding the adolescent from the primary intake damages alliance.
> **Common mistake:** Right family structure ordered wrong — the teen loses the first-alliance move by going last.

**Rule:** Adolescent intake → structure separate teen time with confidentiality discussion; also meet with parent.

**Reference:** Adolescent intake structure

---

### 31. as31 · domain: intake · category: Intake · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

During the first session, a client discloses they have previously attempted suicide twice. What do you assess next?

**A. [ ]** Note the disclosure in the chart and proceed with the standard intake agenda you had planned for today  _[-1 novice error]_
> Treating prior attempts as background data rather than primary risk information is a clinical error.
> **Common mistake:** Reading the highest-risk indicator as chart background — the framework here is entirely under-applied.

**B. [ ]** Administer the PHQ-9 for depression severity and use that number to guide the risk stratification today  _[0 near-miss]_
> PHQ-9 is a depression screen; it does not characterize prior attempt history or current suicide risk.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a depression severity score as a suicide risk stratification — the instrument does not fit.

**C. [✔]** A detailed history of each attempt (method, intent, circumstances, rescue) plus current ideation and means  _[+3 KEY]_
> Prior attempts are the strongest predictor of future suicide; detailed history is required for stratification.

**D. [ ]** Immediately initiate psychiatric hospitalization paperwork on the basis of the prior-attempt history alone  _[-2 harmful]_
> Prior history alone does not mandate hospitalization; a current assessment drives level-of-care decisions.
> **Common mistake:** Over-triage from historical data without a current risk assessment — decision without assessment.

**Rule:** Prior attempt disclosure → detailed attempt history (method, intent, rescue) + current ideation/means; this is the highest-risk clinical indicator.

**Reference:** Suicide risk / attempt history

---

### 32. as32 · domain: ethics · category: Ethics & scope · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A supervisee tells you they have been seeing clients independently without supervision for the past month due to "scheduling issues." What do you assess first?

**A. [ ]** The supervisee’s schedule and the specific reasons that supervision was missed over the past several weeks  _[0 near-miss]_
> Scheduling is a real topic later, but client safety is the immediate concern the supervisor has to address first.
> **Common mistake:** Right admin topic explored at the wrong point in the sequence — before client welfare is addressed.

**B. [ ]** File a licensing board complaint immediately based on the supervisee’s report before assessing client welfare  _[-2 harmful]_
> A licensing complaint may follow, but the first obligation is to assess current client welfare right now.
> **Common mistake:** Escalating externally before ensuring client safety — the primary duty is skipped in the process.

**C. [ ]** Whether to terminate the supervisory relationship because of the boundary violation the supervisee reported  _[-1 novice error]_
> Terminating supervision does not protect the clients who are currently at risk from this scope violation.
> **Common mistake:** Framing a supervision decision as the first move — clients still in care are not protected by ending it.

**D. [✔]** Whether clients were harmed and whether the supervisee had the competence level for the cases carried  _[+3 KEY]_
> Client welfare is the primary concern; the scope violation must be assessed for harm before any admin response.

**Rule:** Supervisee practicing without supervision → assess client welfare first; administrative and licensing responses come after.

**Reference:** Supervisor liability / client protection

---

### 33. as33 · domain: ethics · category: Ethics & scope · rung: 7 (Ethics throughout)

A managed care company requests your client’s full therapy notes to approve continued sessions. What is the appropriate response?

**A. [✔]** Release only the minimum necessary information — usually a treatment summary, not the full psychotherapy notes  _[+3 KEY]_
> Psychotherapy notes carry heightened protection under HIPAA; minimum necessary applies to all disclosures.

**B. [ ]** Release the full file including psychotherapy notes because the payer is paying for the treatment received  _[-2 harmful]_
> Payer status does not override HIPAA minimum necessary or the extra protection given to psychotherapy notes.
> **Common mistake:** Treating payer relationship as a waiver of the psychotherapy-notes standard — a compliance failure.

**C. [ ]** Ask the client to send the records to the insurer themselves rather than having the counselor transmit them  _[0 near-miss]_
> Having the client transmit clinical records is not standard practice and may not satisfy the insurer's process.
> **Common mistake:** Solving a confidentiality question by transferring the disclosure task to the client themselves.

**D. [ ]** Refuse all disclosure so the client’s privacy is fully protected regardless of what coverage may require  _[-1 novice error]_
> Total refusal jeopardizes coverage and is not required; minimum-necessary disclosure is permitted and appropriate.
> **Common mistake:** Over-protecting privacy at the cost of coverage — the framework allows scoped disclosure.

**Rule:** Insurer requests records → minimum necessary disclosure; psychotherapy notes require separate authorization; do not release the full file.

**Reference:** HIPAA minimum necessary / psychotherapy notes

---

### 34. as34 · domain: intake · category: Ethics & scope · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client presents with a possible traumatic brain injury following an accident and is describing significant cognitive changes. What is the first step?

**A. [ ]** Diagnose adjustment disorder and begin CBT for the emotional response to the accident before any evaluation  _[-1 novice error]_
> Attributing cognitive changes to emotional adjustment without ruling out a neurological cause is a diagnostic error.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an emotional-adjustment framework where a neurocognitive framework belongs — a criterion mismatch.

**B. [✔]** Refer for a neuropsychological evaluation and medical assessment before proceeding with any psychotherapy  _[+3 KEY]_
> Cognitive changes post-injury require medical and neuropsychological evaluation before psychotherapy is planned.

**C. [ ]** Administer the MoCA in session as a first screen for cognitive impairment and plan therapy from that score  _[0 near-miss]_
> MoCA is a brief screen, not a diagnostic tool for TBI; specialized neuropsychological evaluation is needed.
> **Common mistake:** Right family of instrument, wrong depth — a brief screen substitutes for the specialized evaluation.

**D. [ ]** Begin trauma-focused psychotherapy for the accident immediately without a medical or neuropsych evaluation  _[-2 harmful]_
> Trauma processing before characterizing cognitive deficits post-TBI is premature and may be contraindicated.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a trauma-focused modality before medical rule-outs — a serious sequencing error.

**Rule:** Post-accident cognitive changes → medical/neuropsychological evaluation before psychotherapy; do not attribute to emotional causes without ruling out organic etiology.

**Reference:** TBI assessment / medical referral

---

### 35. as35 · domain: intake · category: Mood · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A client reporting depression also mentions significant weight gain, cold intolerance, fatigue, and constipation over the past 6 months. Next assessment step?

**A. [ ]** Diagnose MDD with vegetative features and refer for an antidepressant while the medical picture is still open  _[-2 harmful]_
> Antidepressants may be ineffective while a medical cause remains unaddressed; premature and misdirecting.
> **Common mistake:** Endorsing a pharmacologic plan before ruling out an easily testable medical cause — real harm.

**B. [ ]** Begin CBT for depression immediately while the medical evaluation happens later at the client’s convenience  _[-1 novice error]_
> Treating a potential hypothyroid depression with CBT alone while the medical cause is untreated is insufficient.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a psychotherapy framework where a medical framework has to come first — sequencing error.

**C. [✔]** Refer for thyroid function testing before treating the presentation as a primary depressive disorder today  _[+3 KEY]_
> Hypothyroidism produces a complete depressive syndrome; medical causes must be ruled out before diagnosing MDD.

**D. [ ]** Administer the PHQ-9 and treat by the score using the standard depression care pathway from that number  _[0 near-miss]_
> PHQ-9 measures depressive symptoms but does not distinguish medical from primary psychiatric causes.
> **Common mistake:** Right symptom instrument, wrong differential step — the medical rule-out is skipped over.

**Rule:** Depression + somatic symptoms (weight gain, cold intolerance, fatigue, constipation) → rule out hypothyroidism before treating as primary MDD.

**Reference:** Medical causes of depression

---

### 36. as36 · domain: intake · category: Mood · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client being treated for MDD tells you they feel better since starting antidepressants but now has 4 days of barely sleeping, unusual energy, and is spending money impulsively. What do you assess next?

**A. [ ]** Reduce therapy session frequency going forward since the client is reporting improvement in mood and energy  _[0 near-miss]_
> The right global instinct in improvement, but wrong at this moment — the pattern is a red flag, not progress.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a mania signal as improvement — a framework mismatch that reduces contact when it matters.

**B. [ ]** Celebrate the improved mood and energy with the client and reinforce the changes they are experiencing daily  _[-2 harmful]_
> The symptom cluster (decreased sleep, energy, impulsivity) after starting an antidepressant is a red flag.
> **Common mistake:** Reinforcing mania-driven activity as therapy progress — a criterion reversal with real consequences.

**C. [ ]** Continue the current treatment plan without any changes because the elevated energy will normalize on its own  _[-1 novice error]_
> Waiting when a possible manic switch is occurring delays clinically necessary action and misses the signal.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a watchful-waiting framework where an urgent-action framework is what the pattern calls for.

**D. [✔]** Screen for a manic or hypomanic switch right now and contact the prescriber urgently about the current picture  _[+3 KEY]_
> Antidepressant-induced hypomania/mania is a known adverse event; urgent prescriber contact is required today.

**Rule:** Decreased sleep + energy + impulsivity after antidepressant initiation → screen for manic switch; contact prescriber urgently.

**Reference:** Bipolar misdiagnosis / antidepressant-induced mania

---

### 37. as37 · domain: intake · category: Neurodevelopmental · rung: 2 (Medical/substance rule-outs)

A parent reports their 10-year-old has dramatic, episodic behavioral outbursts daily that don't fit the child's baseline and began 6 months ago after a strep infection. What do you assess next?

**A. [✔]** Ask about medical history and refer for pediatric evaluation to rule out PANDAS/PANS before diagnosing  _[+3 KEY]_
> Sudden onset OCD-like or behavioral symptoms post-strep may indicate PANDAS — a medical condition first.

**B. [ ]** Assess for trauma exposure or family stressors that might explain the acute behavior change over six months  _[0 near-miss]_
> Trauma history is worth assessing later, but it does not explain a sudden post-infection onset first.
> **Common mistake:** Right add-on topic ordered wrong — a medical rule-out belongs before trauma exploration.

**C. [ ]** Screen for ADHD using the Conners scales and pursue a behavioral diagnosis from the scale results directly  _[-1 novice error]_
> ADHD is developmental (onset before 12, chronic, not episodic); this acute-onset and episodic picture does not fit.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an ADHD framework to a pattern that does not match its onset or course criteria.

**D. [ ]** Diagnose ODD in this session and begin parent-management training as the primary intervention for the child  _[-2 harmful]_
> Diagnosing a sudden-onset episodic behavioral change without ruling out medical etiology is premature.
> **Common mistake:** Diagnosing a behavior disorder without the medical differential — a criterion reversal for onset.

**Rule:** Sudden behavioral onset post-strep in a child → consider PANDAS/PANS; refer for pediatric evaluation before behavioral diagnosis.

**Reference:** PANDAS / medical causes in children

---

### 38. as38 · domain: intake · category: Neurocognitive · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A 78-year-old client has a 2-year history of gradual memory decline, word-finding difficulty, and is now repeating questions. What do you assess next?

**A. [ ]** Diagnose Alzheimer’s disease and begin supportive counseling with the client and family starting this week  _[-2 harmful]_
> Counselors do not diagnose neurocognitive disorders without neuropsychological evaluation and treatable-cause rule-outs.
> **Common mistake:** Diagnosing a specific neurocognitive disorder without the workup that the diagnostic framework requires.

**B. [✔]** Administer a cognitive screen (MoCA or MMSE) and refer for neuropsychological evaluation and medical work-up  _[+3 KEY]_
> Gradual progressive decline requires structured screening and specialist referral to characterize the picture.

**C. [ ]** Administer a depression screen first because pseudodementia should be considered before neurocognitive workup  _[0 near-miss]_
> Pseudodementia is a valid differential, but 2 years of progressive decline points primarily to neurocognitive workup.
> **Common mistake:** Right differential item ordered wrong — depression screen comes alongside, not before, cognitive testing.

**D. [ ]** Ask the family to observe and report back over another six months because any diagnosis is premature right now  _[-1 novice error]_
> A two-year progressive decline is sufficient to warrant structured assessment now rather than more observation.
> **Common mistake:** Applying a watchful-waiting framework where a workup is already indicated by the two-year trajectory.

**Rule:** 2-year progressive cognitive decline in older adult → structured cognitive screen + specialist referral; do not diagnose or defer without evaluation.

**Reference:** Neurocognitive disorder assessment

---

### 39. as39 · domain: intake · category: Substance · rung: 1 (Imminent safety)

A client in an opioid treatment program on methadone presents to their counseling session slurring words and appearing sedated. Next assessment step?

**A. [ ]** Administer a brief substance-use screener in the room before deciding whether medical attention is warranted  _[-2 harmful]_
> Screening is not appropriate when the client may be in acute medical danger; medical staff comes first.
> **Common mistake:** Reaching for a screening framework at a medical-emergency moment — dangerous under-triage.

**B. [ ]** End the session now and reschedule the appointment for a time when the client appears to be more clear  _[0 near-miss]_
> Right instinct to not proceed clinically, but leaving without medical assessment is inadequate here.
> **Common mistake:** Reading a possible overdose as a scheduling problem — pausing without medical assessment.

**C. [✔]** Assess for overdose risk and contact medical staff immediately — do not conduct the session with this client  _[+3 KEY]_
> Sedation and slurring on methadone may indicate overdose or polysubstance interaction — a medical emergency.

**D. [ ]** Conduct the session as planned and document the observation for the client’s prescriber in the shared note  _[-1 novice error]_
> Conducting a session when overdose risk is present delays life-saving intervention that is time-critical.
> **Common mistake:** Documenting a potential emergency without acting — chart entries in place of immediate medical action.

**Rule:** Sedated/slurring client on methadone → possible overdose; medical staff immediately before any clinical activity.

**Reference:** Opioid overdose recognition

---

### 40. as40 · domain: intake · category: Assessment skills · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A counselor has been using the same standardized tool for all clients regardless of their cultural background. What is the primary psychometric concern?

**A. [ ]** Construct validity — the tool may be measuring somewhat different constructs across different cultural groups  _[0 near-miss]_
> Construct validity is a real concern, but the primary issue in cross-cultural use is the normative reference group.
> **Common mistake:** Right psychometric concept, wrong emphasis for the specific cross-cultural application asked about.

**B. [ ]** Inter-rater reliability — different counselors will score the same tool differently across cultural client groups  _[-2 harmful]_
> Inter-rater reliability concerns examiner agreement; it does not address cultural applicability of the norms.
> **Common mistake:** Confusing an examiner-agreement question with a norms-representativeness question — a concept swap.

**C. [ ]** Test-retest reliability — different clients will score somewhat differently across administrations of the tool  _[-1 novice error]_
> Test-retest reliability is a property of the instrument itself, not a cross-cultural application issue.
> **Common mistake:** Applying an instrument-property framework to a cross-cultural application question — a category mismatch.

**D. [✔]** Cultural bias — the normative sample may not represent the client’s group, producing invalid comparisons  _[+3 KEY]_
> An instrument normed on an unrepresentative population compares the client's scores against the wrong reference.

**Rule:** Standardized tools across cultural groups → primary concern is cultural bias in the normative sample; check whether the client’s group is represented.

**Reference:** Cultural bias in assessment

---

### 41. as41 · domain: intake · category: Assessment skills · rung: 5 (Clarify the picture)

A counselor completes an intake and notes three possible diagnoses that could explain the client's presentation. What is the correct next step?

**A. [✔]** Differential diagnosis — use added history, screening tools, and collateral to rule each option in or out today  _[+3 KEY]_
> Differential diagnosis is the systematic process of distinguishing between competing diagnostic possibilities.

**B. [ ]** Pick the most common of the three possible diagnoses as a starting point and revise later if progress stalls  _[0 near-miss]_
> Frequency does not determine accuracy; each candidate diagnosis needs to be evaluated against the evidence.
> **Common mistake:** Substituting a base-rate heuristic for the systematic differential-diagnosis framework at intake.

**C. [ ]** List all three possible diagnoses at once and begin treating each of them simultaneously with different modalities  _[-1 novice error]_
> Multiple untested diagnoses driving simultaneous treatment produces disorganized and ineffective care.
> **Common mistake:** Skipping the differential step and treating three hypotheses at once — the diagnostic framework is dropped.

**D. [ ]** Refer the client to a psychiatrist because multiple candidate diagnoses seem to exceed counselor scope  _[-2 harmful]_
> Differential diagnosis is within counselor scope; refer for medical evaluation, not to avoid the diagnostic work.
> **Common mistake:** Using a scope claim to avoid diagnostic work — a scope framework misapplied to sidestep the task.

**Rule:** Multiple possible diagnoses → conduct differential diagnosis; evaluate each against history, screening tools, and collateral — do not default to frequency or refer to avoid the work.

**Reference:** Differential diagnosis process

---


## Core Counseling Attributes

- **Items:** 30
- **Profile:** recall
- **Served at:** `/core-attributes-quiz.html`
- Scoring: dichotomous. Correct = key (✔), all others wrong.

### 1. ca01 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

A client discloses she has been having an affair. The counselor says, "Tell me more about what that experience has been like for you." This BEST demonstrates:

**A. [✔]** Unconditional positive regard — nonjudgmental acceptance of the client regardless of behavior
> Inviting exploration without moral evaluation is the operational marker of UPR.

**B. [ ]** Accurate empathy — the counselor is reflecting the emotional content of what she has just shared
> Empathy reflects felt experience; an open invitation is not a reflection.

**C. [ ]** Congruence — the counselor is authentically expressing curiosity about the affair disclosure
> Congruence is inner-outer alignment; simple inquiry does not display it.

**D. [ ]** Advanced empathy — the counselor is reflecting the implied shame and guilt behind her disclosure
> Advanced empathy names implicit affect; the response only invites her to speak.

**Rationale:** UPR is valuing the client's personhood regardless of behavior. Inviting exploration without implied judgment operationalizes UPR.

---

### 2. ca02 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

A counselor responds: "You sound exhausted by all of this — like you've been carrying it alone for a long time." The client says, "Exactly. That's exactly it." This exchange BEST illustrates:

**A. [ ]** Advanced empathy — the counselor is reflecting a meaning the client has not yet put into words
> Advanced empathy names the unspoken; exhaustion had already been implied.

**B. [✔]** Accurate empathic understanding successfully communicated to the client in a way that landed
> The client's 'Exactly' confirms empathy was tracked AND communicated.

**C. [ ]** Unconditional positive regard — the counselor is signaling nonjudgmental acceptance of the client
> UPR is acceptance of personhood; the reflection tracks affect, not worth.

**D. [ ]** Congruence — the counselor is aligning inner experience with outer expression in the response
> Congruence is genuineness; nothing here reveals the counselor's own experience.

**Rationale:** Accurate empathy is felt tracking PLUS successful communication; the client's confirmation shows both landed.

---

### 3. ca03 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

During a session, a counselor notices they feel genuinely bored and distracted. Acting on congruence, the MOST appropriate response is:

**A. [ ]** End the session early and reschedule the appointment for a time when the counselor is more focused
> Terminating avoids the transaction; congruence is about naming, not withdrawing.

**B. [ ]** Ask the client to slow their pace so the counselor can follow the material more closely in session
> Locating the problem in the client masks the counselor's own experience.

**C. [✔]** Disclose the experience when relevant: "I notice my attention drifting; I want to stay with you here."
> Brief, relevant disclosure returned to the client's process operationalizes congruence.

**D. [ ]** Continue the session without any disclosure because showing boredom would violate the UPR condition
> Congruence requires disclosure when therapeutically relevant, not suppression.

**Rationale:** Congruence means inner experience, outward expression, and words align. Brief, relevant, returned to client.

---

### 4. ca04 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

Rogers argued that the three core conditions are "necessary and sufficient" for therapeutic change. This means:

**A. [ ]** Techniques are helpful additions but are secondary to the relational conditions that produce change
> This softens Rogers' claim; he held techniques were not required at all.

**B. [ ]** Skilled counselors need the three conditions plus at least one evidence-based technique to work
> This reverses Rogers' claim by making a technique necessary.

**C. [ ]** All three of the conditions must be present at the same time in every moment of every session
> Rogers required consistency in the relationship, not moment-by-moment perfection.

**D. [✔]** No additional therapeutic techniques are required as long as all three conditions are present
> "Sufficient" is Rogers' claim that the conditions alone can produce change.

**Rationale:** The conditions are necessary (required) AND sufficient (nothing else needed) — Rogers' radical relational claim.

---

### 5. ca05 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

A counselor says, "I think you should reconsider leaving that relationship — you've worked hard on it." This response MOST directly violates:

**A. [✔]** Unconditional positive regard — the counselor is directing and evaluating the client's own choices
> Prescribing a choice conveys conditional acceptance based on the client's decision.

**B. [ ]** The therapeutic frame — advice-giving is prohibited across nearly every counseling modality
> Directive input is used in many modalities; the Rogerian violation is UPR.

**C. [ ]** Congruence — the counselor is not being genuine or authentic about their own inner state right now
> The response may be genuine; the flaw is evaluative direction, not falseness.

**D. [ ]** Empathy — the counselor has not first reflected the feelings that the client had just expressed
> Failure to reflect is present but secondary; the primary violation is evaluative.

**Rationale:** UPR is violated when the counselor evaluates or directs the client's choices rather than accepting the person.

---

### 6. ca06 · domain: core · topic: Bordin's Working Alliance

A client says, "I don't understand why we're doing all this breathing stuff — I came here because my marriage is falling apart." This represents a rupture in:

**A. [ ]** Bond and tasks equally — safety concerns and questions about methods are both being expressed
> The stem raises method only; treating both as equal misreads the rupture.

**B. [✔]** Tasks — the client is questioning whether the current session methods connect to their own goals
> A task rupture is exactly this: shared goal, disputed means.

**C. [ ]** Goals — the client and the counselor do not agree about what therapy is supposed to be for
> The client accepts the marriage as the goal; disagreement is with the method.

**D. [ ]** Bond — the client does not feel emotionally safe with the counselor sitting across from them
> No safety concern is stated; the complaint targets methods, not the relationship.

**Rationale:** Task rupture: goal is intact (save the marriage) but the client cannot see how the method serves it.

---

### 7. ca07 · domain: core · topic: Bordin's Working Alliance
