A client attends sessions but repeatedly says things like "I guess if you think that's helpful" and "sure, whatever you say." This pattern MOST likely signals:

**A. [ ]** A bond rupture — the client no longer feels emotionally safe with the counselor in session
> The client is still attending and speaking; bond signals differ (withdrawal).

**B. [ ]** A task rupture — the client is questioning the counselor's methods, not their overall aims
> Task ruptures voice method complaints; passive assent points to goals.

**C. [✔]** A goal rupture — the client and the counselor are not sharing the same treatment objectives
> Compliance without buy-in is the classic goal-rupture presentation.

**D. [ ]** Good therapeutic compliance — the client is genuinely engaged in the direction set by the counselor
> Passive assent is not engagement; it hides disagreement from the counselor.

**Rationale:** Passive compliance flags a goal rupture: the client is going along without endorsing the aim.

---

### 8. ca08 · domain: core · topic: Bordin's Working Alliance

A client who was abused as a child says tearfully, "I just don't think I can talk about what happened — it doesn't feel safe." The FIRST clinical priority is:

**A. [ ]** Refer the client to an outside trauma specialist who can provide the felt safety they are needing
> Referral abandons a workable rupture; bond can be repaired in-session.

**B. [ ]** Introduce EMDR right away as a nonverbal alternative to discussing the trauma out loud in session
> Starting an exposure-based method before safety is established repeats the harm.

**C. [ ]** Renegotiate the treatment plan tasks to include stabilization work ahead of trauma processing
> Right principle, wrong level: bond precedes both task and goal renegotiation.

**D. [✔]** Address the bond component: create conditions of felt safety before any trauma processing occurs
> "Doesn't feel safe" names a bond issue; bond is foundational to trauma work.

**Rationale:** "Doesn't feel safe" is a bond signal. Bond is foundational — everything else waits for it.

---

### 9. ca09 · domain: core · topic: Bordin's Working Alliance

Research on the therapeutic alliance consistently shows that it is:

**A. [✔]** The strongest common-factor predictor of treatment outcome across a wide range of modalities
> Wampold and Lambert converge on the alliance as the top common factor.

**B. [ ]** Determined mostly by pre-existing client factors rather than by observable counselor behavior
> Counselor behavior contributes; the alliance is a two-person process.

**C. [ ]** Important only in humanistic or person-centered approaches, not the more directive modalities
> Alliance effects are found in CBT, psychodynamic, and other modalities.

**D. [ ]** Less predictive of client outcome than the specific evidence-based technique used in a modality
> Meta-analyses find the reverse: alliance predicts more variance than technique.

**Rationale:** Alliance is the strongest common-factor predictor of outcome across modalities (Wampold, Lambert).

---

### 10. ca10 · domain: core · topic: Rupture & Repair

A client who usually engages openly arrives, gives one-word answers, avoids eye contact, and does not elaborate. The counselor's BEST response is:

**A. [ ]** Confront the withdrawal by asking directly, "What's wrong today? You seem completely different now."
> The demand for an answer intensifies withdrawal instead of inviting engagement.

**B. [✔]** Tentatively name the shift: "I notice you seem quieter than usual today — I want to check in."
> Naming the observation and offering warmth is the metacommunication repair move.

**C. [ ]** Continue the session agenda as planned because the client may just be having a difficult day today
> Proceeding as if nothing changed leaves the withdrawal rupture unaddressed.

**D. [ ]** Interpret the behavior as resistance and say, "It seems like you're not ready to work today."
> Labeling withdrawal as resistance is blaming and hardens the rupture.

**Rationale:** Withdrawal ruptures need tentative metacommunication — name the shift, invite exploration.

---

### 11. ca11 · domain: core · topic: Rupture & Repair

A client says directly, "I feel like you're not really listening to me — you keep steering us back to CBT when I just want to be heard." This is BEST classified as:

**A. [ ]** Transference — the client is displacing frustration from another relationship onto the counselor
> A concrete complaint about a real behavior is not a transference reading.

**B. [ ]** A termination signal that would require an explicit discussion of ending the counseling arrangement
> The client is engaged and asking for change, not signaling termination.

**C. [✔]** A confrontation rupture — the client is directly challenging the counselor's approach in the session
> Direct challenge to the counselor is the definition of a confrontation rupture.

**D. [ ]** A withdrawal rupture — the client is pulling back and disengaging from the session in front of you
> The client is actively confronting, not withdrawing.

**Rationale:** Confrontation ruptures involve direct disagreement with or challenge to the counselor.

---

### 12. ca12 · domain: core · topic: Rupture & Repair

Research on therapeutic rupture and repair (Safran & Muran) found that:

**A. [ ]** Effective repair mostly requires interpreting the rupture as transference from the past instead
> Metacommunication in the here-and-now, not transference talk, is the model.

**B. [ ]** A single unrepaired rupture reliably predicts that the client will drop out of treatment shortly
> One rupture does not reliably cause dropout; repair rate matters more.

**C. [ ]** Ruptures should be prevented at all costs so as to maintain a positive therapeutic climate over time
> Ruptures are inevitable; the outcome depends on repair, not prevention.

**D. [✔]** Successfully repaired ruptures can strengthen the alliance and improve counseling outcomes overall
> Safran & Muran show repaired ruptures deepen alliance and improve outcome.

**Rationale:** Repaired ruptures strengthen the alliance and improve outcomes (Safran & Muran).

---

### 13. ca13 · domain: core · topic: Rupture & Repair

A client says, "I felt hurt when you cut me off last session." The MOST effective repair response is:

**A. [✔]** "I appreciate you telling me. Can you say more about how that landed? I want to understand it."
> Acknowledge, invite exploration, and stay with it — the Safran & Muran move.

**B. [ ]** "That's not what happened — I was trying to redirect us to something more clinically productive."
> Correcting the client denies their experience and prevents repair.

**C. [ ]** "Let's put that aside so we can focus on what you actually came in to work on today in session."
> Deferring the rupture blocks metacommunication and models avoidance.

**D. [ ]** "I'm sorry, that was completely wrong of me. I'll be more mindful and I will make sure to fix it."
> Premature apology closes the exploration and prioritizes counselor comfort.

**Rationale:** Repair sequence: acknowledge, explore what happened for the client, stay with it before repairing.

---

### 14. ca14 · domain: counseling · topic: Stages of Counseling

A new client arrives for their first session and immediately says, "So what should I work on?" The counselor's FIRST priority is:

**A. [ ]** Start a formal assessment battery so the counselor can rule in or out major clinical diagnoses
> Structured assessment belongs to stage 2, after alliance is established.

**B. [✔]** Establish rapport and collaboratively explore what brought them in and what they are hoping for
> Stage 1 is alliance and shared understanding before any goal-setting.

**C. [ ]** Give the client several evidence-based options and encourage them to pick the one that fits best
> Choosing a modality before alliance and goals is a stage error.

**D. [ ]** Provide psychoeducation about the counseling process and set homework for the second session
> Psychoeducation is fine but not the priority before the alliance is formed.

**Rationale:** Stage 1 (Egan / Ivey): rapport and shared story precede assessment, goals, and intervention.

---

### 15. ca15 · domain: counseling · topic: Stages of Counseling

A client and counselor have completed a thorough assessment and share a working understanding of the problem. The NEXT stage is:

**A. [ ]** Terminate services because the client can now work on the problem independently outside sessions
> Termination follows goal attainment, not the completion of assessment.

**B. [ ]** Continue exploration of the presenting problem to gather still more information from the client
> Further exploration delays goal-setting once shared understanding exists.

**C. [✔]** Set concrete, measurable, collaborative goals for what the client wants to change in counseling
> After the shared story, stage 2 collaboratively sets change-focused goals.

**D. [ ]** Move directly into confrontation of the client's defenses about the problem they have described
> Confrontation of defenses is not a standard stage of the counseling model.

**Rationale:** Model sequence: alliance/story -> goal setting -> intervention -> maintenance/termination.

---

### 16. ca16 · domain: counseling · topic: Stages of Counseling

A client says, "I feel so much better — I don't know if I need to come back." The counselor's BEST response is:

**A. [ ]** Interpret the wish to stop as a rupture, avoidance, or resistance that requires deeper exploration
> Reading normal termination readiness as resistance pathologizes progress.

**B. [ ]** Agree to terminate today and wish them well since improvement is the goal of the entire process
> Abrupt termination skips relapse planning and consolidation of gains.

**C. [ ]** Warn the client that stopping now will cause a relapse and firmly recommend continuing sessions
> Alarmist warnings undermine autonomy and the alliance.

**D. [✔]** Explore the improvement and collaboratively plan a stepped-down termination with relapse prevention
> Stage 4 termination is stepped, planned, and includes relapse prevention.

**Rationale:** Termination is a stage: explore the improvement, plan a stepped exit, include relapse prevention.

---

### 17. ca17 · domain: core · topic: Cultural Responsiveness

A client from a collectivist cultural background says, "I can't decide about the job without talking to my family first." The BEST response is:

**A. [✔]** Explore how the family conversations fit the client's own decision-making process and identity
> Cultural humility is exploring the client's frame, not correcting it.

**B. [ ]** Refer the client to a counselor from the same culture on the grounds that scope has been exceeded
> Reflexive referral is not required and can feel like abandonment.

**C. [ ]** Encourage the client to prioritize their own individual autonomy over the family's preferences here
> Imposing individualism ignores culturally embedded decision-making.

**D. [ ]** Interpret the deference to family as enmeshment and set a clearer boundary with them in session
> Labeling collectivism "enmeshment" is a Western pathologizing frame.

**Rationale:** Cultural humility explores the client's frame without imposing an individualist standard.

---

### 18. ca18 · domain: core · topic: Cultural Responsiveness

The MSJCC (Multicultural and Social Justice Counseling Competencies) emphasize which sequence?

**A. [ ]** Complete cultural competence first, then skills, and finally social-justice advocacy work with clients
> MSJCC treats advocacy as concurrent, not a later stage after competence.

**B. [✔]** Attitudes and beliefs first, then knowledge, then skills, then action — a developmental sequence
> The MSJCC domains build in this order across all counselor–client interactions.

**C. [ ]** Techniques first, followed by knowledge of the client's culture, and only then counselor awareness
> This reverses the MSJCC ordering, which begins with awareness.

**D. [ ]** Client demographics first, then interventions matched to the demographic profile, and then advocacy
> Demographics are not the entry point; counselor awareness is.

**Rationale:** MSJCC ordering: attitudes/beliefs -> knowledge -> skills -> action, across four domains.

---

### 19. ca19 · domain: core · topic: Cultural Responsiveness

A White counselor notices they feel uncomfortable when a Black client discusses experiences of racism. The BEST first step is:

**A. [ ]** Change the topic to prevent the discomfort from spilling into the therapeutic relationship over time
> Avoidance silences the client and reinforces the counselor's discomfort.

**B. [ ]** Refer the client to a counselor of color to avoid causing further harm in the current relationship
> Reflexive referral offloads the counselor's work onto the client.

**C. [✔]** Explore the discomfort in supervision or consultation without burdening the client with it in session
> Cultural humility requires the counselor's own work; the client is not the classroom.

**D. [ ]** Disclose the discomfort in session to model transparency and openness with the client immediately
> Well-meant disclosure here centers the counselor's reaction over the client's.

**Rationale:** Counselor discomfort about race is worked in supervision — not offloaded to the client.

---

### 20. ca20 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor asks a client to complete a thought record examining automatic thoughts, evidence for and against, and a balanced alternative view. This is:

**A. [ ]** Person-centered therapy — reflecting the client's felt experience without directive intervention here
> Person-centered work does not use structured cognitive worksheets.

**B. [ ]** Psychodynamic therapy — interpretation of the underlying conflicts driving the client's current distress
> Thought records are Beckian CBT, not psychodynamic interpretation.

**C. [ ]** Solution-focused therapy — building exceptions and scaling toward the preferred future outcomes
> SFBT uses exceptions and scales, not thought records.

**D. [✔]** Cognitive-behavioral therapy — examining automatic thoughts, evidence, and balanced alternatives
> Thought records are the signature CBT technique for restructuring cognitions.

**Rationale:** Thought records = Beckian CBT: identify automatic thoughts, weigh evidence, generate a balanced view.

---

### 21. ca21 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor helps a client identify their family birth order, sibling constellation, and early recollections. This is:

**A. [✔]** Adlerian (individual psychology) — using lifestyle assessment, family constellation, early recollections
> Adler's hallmark tools: birth order, constellation, and early recollections.

**B. [ ]** Jungian analytical therapy — exploring the archetypes and the deeper collective unconscious material
> Jungian work centers archetypes and the collective unconscious, not birth order.

**C. [ ]** Bowenian family systems therapy — genogram construction and analysis of multigenerational patterns
> Genograms and differentiation are Bowen; Adler owns the earlier tools listed.

**D. [ ]** Object-relations therapy — the exploration of internalized representations of parents and caregivers
> Object relations focuses on internalized objects, not constellation data.

**Rationale:** Adler = lifestyle assessment, family constellation, birth order, early recollections.

---

### 22. ca22 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor uses "the miracle question" and asks the client to scale progress from 1-10. This is:

**A. [ ]** Reality therapy (choice theory) — addressing the client's five basic needs and current choices
> Reality therapy uses WDEP and choice theory, not miracle questions.

**B. [✔]** Solution-focused brief therapy — using the miracle question and scaling questions to build progress
> Miracle question and scaling questions are the signature SFBT tools.

**C. [ ]** Narrative therapy — externalizing the problem so it becomes something the client can act against in life
> Externalizing is White & Epston narrative; miracles/scales are SFBT.

**D. [ ]** Motivational interviewing — evoking the client's own change talk about the target behavior over time
> MI evokes change talk with OARS; miracle/scaling are not MI signatures.

**Rationale:** de Shazer / Berg SFBT: miracle question, exceptions, scaling toward the preferred future.

---

### 23. ca23 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor asks a client to accept their thoughts as thoughts (not facts) and commit to values-based action. This is:

**A. [ ]** Dialectical behavior therapy — balancing acceptance with change work through the DBT skill modules
> DBT is dialectical but not organized around psychological flexibility.

**B. [ ]** Traditional cognitive therapy — disputing the content of the automatic thoughts and replacing them
> ACT changes relationship with thoughts; content-disputing is Beck / Ellis.

**C. [✔]** Acceptance and commitment therapy — building psychological flexibility through defusion and values
> Defusion + values-guided action is Hayes' ACT model of flexibility.

**D. [ ]** Reality therapy — helping the client take responsibility for their choices and current commitments
> Reality therapy uses WDEP; ACT uses defusion and values.

**Rationale:** ACT builds psychological flexibility: acceptance, defusion, present moment, values, committed action.

---

### 24. ca24 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor confronts a client's "musts" and "shoulds" and asks them to dispute these rigid demands actively. This is:

**A. [ ]** Beckian cognitive therapy — a Socratic examination of the evidence for automatic thoughts held today
> Beck uses Socratic inquiry; Ellis directly disputes "musts" and "shoulds."

**B. [ ]** Motivational interviewing — evoking the client's own reasons for change through open-ended reflection
> MI is nondirective evocation; direct disputation is not MI-consistent.

**C. [ ]** Person-centered therapy — providing accurate reflections without any directive challenge in session
> Person-centered work does not confront or dispute the client's beliefs.

**D. [✔]** Rational emotive behavior therapy — disputing the client's irrational beliefs, demands, and "musts"
> Ellis' REBT names and disputes musturbation and rigid demands.

**Rationale:** REBT (Ellis): identify absolute demands (must, should, ought) and dispute them directly.

---

### 25. ca25 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor helps a client construct a preferred alternative story by asking about unique outcomes and exceptions. This is:

**A. [✔]** Narrative therapy — externalizing the problem and identifying unique outcomes to re-author the story
> White & Epston: externalize, find unique outcomes, thicken a preferred story.

**B. [ ]** Existential therapy — addressing the givens of existence such as freedom, isolation, and mortality
> Existential work targets the givens, not story re-authoring.

**C. [ ]** Solution-focused brief therapy — scaling and exception questions to build small changes over sessions
> SFBT uses exceptions too, but narrative owns "unique outcomes" and re-storying.

**D. [ ]** Adlerian therapy — exploring the client's lifestyle patterns and their subjective view of the past
> Adler uses lifestyle, not unique outcomes and re-storying.

**Rationale:** Narrative therapy (White & Epston): externalize the problem, find unique outcomes, thicken the alternative story.

---

### 26. ca26 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A client says, "I don't know why I even bother trying anymore." The counselor responds, "You sound worn down." This is:

**A. [ ]** An interpretation — the counselor is offering a deeper meaning that the client has not yet articulated
> "Worn down" mirrors what was implied; it does not add a new meaning.

**B. [✔]** A reflection of feeling — the counselor is naming the emotional experience behind what the client said
> Naming the client's emotion IS a reflection of feeling.

**C. [ ]** A summary — the counselor is pulling together the major themes covered so far in the session material
> A summary integrates several statements; this reflects a single feeling.

**D. [ ]** A paraphrase — the counselor is restating the informational content of what the client just described
> A paraphrase mirrors the content, not the underlying emotion.

**Rationale:** Reflection of feeling names the emotion; paraphrase mirrors content; interpretation adds new meaning.

---

### 27. ca27 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A client says, "My boss said my work isn't up to standard." The counselor: "Your boss criticized your work." This is:

**A. [ ]** Confrontation — the counselor is pointing out an inconsistency in the client's described experience
> No discrepancy is pointed out; this is a straight content mirror.

**B. [ ]** Reflection of feeling — the counselor is naming the underlying emotional experience of the client
> No feeling is named; only the content is restated.

**C. [✔]** Paraphrase — the counselor is restating the informational content of what the client just described
> Restating the factual content in the counselor's own words is a paraphrase.

**D. [ ]** Summary — the counselor is pulling together the major themes covered so far in the client's session
> A summary integrates multiple statements, not one.

**Rationale:** Paraphrase = restating factual content; reflection of feeling = naming affect.

---

### 28. ca28 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A client says, "I love my job, but I dread going to work every morning." The counselor: "You say you love it, and you dread it." This is:

**A. [ ]** Reflection of feeling — the counselor is naming the emotional experience behind what the client said
> No feeling label is used; the counselor highlighted a discrepancy.

**B. [ ]** Paraphrase — the counselor is restating the informational content of what the client just described
> The counselor pointed out contradictory statements, not merely restated content.

**C. [ ]** Interpretation — the counselor is offering a deeper explanation of the meaning of the discrepancy
> The counselor named the discrepancy without offering a new explanatory frame.

**D. [✔]** Confrontation — the counselor is naming a discrepancy or mixed message in the client's statements
> Naming a "you say X and Y" contradiction is Ivey-style confrontation.

**Rationale:** Confrontation (Ivey) names a discrepancy without judgment: "on one hand X, on the other Y."

---

### 29. ca29 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A client speaks for 10 minutes about a difficult week. The counselor pauses and offers a brief summary of the themes. This BEST reflects:

**A. [✔]** A summary that integrates the major themes the client raised across the entire ten-minute segment
> Integrating several statements into themes is a summary.

**B. [ ]** A reflection of feeling that names the primary emotion running through the client's difficult week
> Feeling reflections name one affect; a summary integrates themes.

**C. [ ]** Immediacy — the counselor is naming what is happening between them in the here-and-now interaction
> Immediacy speaks to the counselor–client interaction, not week themes.

**D. [ ]** An interpretation that offers a deeper unconscious meaning that the client has not yet articulated
> A theme summary is not an interpretation of unconscious material.

**Rationale:** Summaries integrate major themes across a stretch of session content, orienting both parties.

---

### 30. ca30 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A counselor says, "I notice we've been going back and forth about your job, but you keep pulling us into work when I ask about home." This is:

**A. [ ]** A paraphrase — the counselor is restating the informational content of what the client just described
> A paraphrase mirrors content; immediacy names the process.

**B. [✔]** Immediacy — the counselor is naming what is happening between them in the here-and-now interaction
> Naming what is happening between counselor and client right now is immediacy.

**C. [ ]** A summary — the counselor is integrating major themes discussed across the previous session material
> A summary integrates themes; this names an in-session pattern.

**D. [ ]** Interpretation — the counselor is offering a deeper meaning of the pattern in the client's life history
> Interpretation adds new meaning; immediacy stays with the observable process.

**Rationale:** Immediacy speaks to the here-and-now counselor–client relationship in the session.

---


## Knowledge Bank (Timed Exam / Drill)

- **Items:** 300
- **Profile:** recall
- **Served at:** `/knowledge-drill.html`
- Scoring: dichotomous. Correct = key (✔), all others wrong.

### 1. tpse001 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: easy

The cognitive model proposes that psychological distress is primarily maintained by:

**A. [✔]** Distorted or unhelpful patterns of thinking
> Beck's cognitive model holds that automatic thoughts, intermediate beliefs, and core beliefs shape emotional and behavioral responses. Distorted thinking is the central mechanism in CBT.

**B. [ ]** Incongruence between self-concept and experience
> "Incongruence between self-concept and experience" is not what the item tests — Beck's cognitive model holds that automatic thoughts, intermediate beliefs, and core beliefs shape emotional and behavioral responses.

**C. [ ]** Unconscious conflicts from early childhood now
> "Unconscious conflicts from early childhood now" is not what the item tests — Beck's cognitive model holds that automatic thoughts, intermediate beliefs, and core beliefs shape emotional and behavioral responses.

**D. [ ]** Reinforcement of maladaptive behaviors here
> "Reinforcement of maladaptive behaviors here" is not what the item tests — Beck's cognitive model holds that automatic thoughts, intermediate beliefs, and core beliefs shape emotional and behavioral responses.

**Rationale:** Beck's cognitive model holds that automatic thoughts, intermediate beliefs, and core beliefs shape emotional and behavioral responses. Distorted thinking is the central mechanism in CBT.

---

### 2. tpse002 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: medium

A client says, "If I'm not perfect at everything, I'm a total failure." This reflects which cognitive distortion?

**A. [ ]** Mind reading
> "Mind reading" is not what the item tests — All-or-nothing (black-and-white) thinking involves seeing situations in only two categories with no middle ground.

**B. [✔]** All-or-nothing thinking
> All-or-nothing (black-and-white) thinking involves seeing situations in only two categories with no middle ground. The absolute "total failure" for any imperfection is the hallmark distortion.

**C. [ ]** Emotional reasoning
> "Emotional reasoning" is not what the item tests — All-or-nothing (black-and-white) thinking involves seeing situations in only two categories with no middle ground.

**D. [ ]** Catastrophizing
> "Catastrophizing" is not what the item tests — All-or-nothing (black-and-white) thinking involves seeing situations in only two categories with no middle ground.

**Rationale:** All-or-nothing (black-and-white) thinking involves seeing situations in only two categories with no middle ground. The absolute "total failure" for any imperfection is the hallmark distortion.

---

### 3. tpse003 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: medium

Socratic questioning in CBT is used primarily to:

**A. [ ]** Guide clients toward relaxation techniques during counseling
> "Guide clients toward relaxation techniques during counseling" is not what the item tests — Socratic questioning is guided discovery — using targeted questions to help clients evaluate the accuracy and utility of their thoughts rather than

**B. [ ]** Teach clients new coping skills through instruction at intake
> "Teach clients new coping skills through instruction at intak…" is not what the item tests — Socratic questioning is guided discovery — using targeted questions to help clients evaluate the accuracy and utility of their thoughts rather than

**C. [✔]** Help clients examine evidence for and against their thoughts
> Socratic questioning is guided discovery — using targeted questions to help clients evaluate the accuracy and utility of their thoughts rather than accepting or disputing them directly.

**D. [ ]** Challenge the client's beliefs through direct confrontation now
> "Challenge the client's beliefs through direct confrontation …" is not what the item tests — Socratic questioning is guided discovery — using targeted questions to help clients evaluate the accuracy and utility of their thoughts rather than

**Rationale:** Socratic questioning is guided discovery — using targeted questions to help clients evaluate the accuracy and utility of their thoughts rather than accepting or disputing them directly.

### 4. tpse004 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: hard

In Beck's cognitive model, "schemas" are best described as:

**A. [ ]** Automatic thoughts that occur in specific triggering situations at the intake stage
> "Automatic thoughts that occur in specific triggering situati…" is not what the item tests — Schemas are the deepest level in Beck's hierarchy (core beliefs → intermediate beliefs → automatic thoughts).

**B. [ ]** Conscious beliefs clients can identify with minimal prompting as usually described
> "Conscious beliefs clients can identify with minimal promptin…" is not what the item tests — Schemas are the deepest level in Beck's hierarchy (core beliefs → intermediate beliefs → automatic thoughts).

**C. [ ]** Rules and assumptions that govern behavior in multiple domains at the intake stage
> "Rules and assumptions that govern behavior in multiple domai…" is not what the item tests — Schemas are the deepest level in Beck's hierarchy (core beliefs → intermediate beliefs → automatic thoughts).

**D. [✔]** Deep, enduring cognitive structures that organize information about self and world
> Schemas are the deepest level in Beck's hierarchy (core beliefs → intermediate beliefs → automatic thoughts). They are stable cognitive templates formed in early experience and typically outside awareness.

**Rationale:** Schemas are the deepest level in Beck's hierarchy (core beliefs → intermediate beliefs → automatic thoughts). They are stable cognitive templates formed in early experience and typically outside awareness.

### 5. tpse005 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: medium

A CBT therapist assigns a client to keep a thought record between sessions. This exemplifies:

**A. [✔]** Collaborative empiricism through homework
> Thought records embody CBT's collaborative empiricism — client acts as scientist, testing their own beliefs between sessions rather than taking them as given.

**B. [ ]** Response prevention during a routine session
> "Response prevention during a routine session" is not what the item tests — Thought records embody CBT's collaborative empiricism — client acts as scientist, testing their own beliefs between sessions rather than taking them as given.

**C. [ ]** Behavioral activation at the intake stage
> "Behavioral activation at the intake stage" is not what the item tests — Thought records embody CBT's collaborative empiricism — client acts as scientist, testing their own beliefs between sessions rather than taking them as given.

**D. [ ]** Systematic desensitization in this domain
> "Systematic desensitization in this domain" is not what the item tests — Thought records embody CBT's collaborative empiricism — client acts as scientist, testing their own beliefs between sessions rather than taking them as given.

**Rationale:** Thought records embody CBT's collaborative empiricism — client acts as scientist, testing their own beliefs between sessions rather than taking them as given.

### 6. tpse006 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: hard

Which statement BEST distinguishes CBT from REBT?

**A. [ ]** CBT protocols are typically time-limited; REBT is open-ended by design
> "CBT is time-limited; REBT is open-ended as clinicians typica…" is not what the item tests — REBT (Ellis) specifically targets musturbatory thinking — absolutistic demands.

**B. [✔]** REBT disputes absolutistic demands; CBT restructures thoughts
> REBT (Ellis) specifically targets musturbatory thinking — absolutistic demands. CBT (Beck) focuses on identifying and modifying specific automatic thoughts, intermediate beliefs, and core beliefs through guided discovery.

**C. [ ]** REBT has a stronger evidence base than CBT (in the recall-item usage)
> "REBT is evidence-based; CBT is not as the reference material…" is not what the item tests — REBT (Ellis) specifically targets musturbatory thinking — absolutistic demands.

**D. [ ]** CBT centrally relies on exposure; REBT does not use it in the same way
> "CBT uses exposure; REBT does not as the reference material f…" is not what the item tests — REBT (Ellis) specifically targets musturbatory thinking — absolutistic demands.

**Rationale:** REBT (Ellis) specifically targets musturbatory thinking — absolutistic demands. CBT (Beck) focuses on identifying and modifying specific automatic thoughts, intermediate beliefs, and core beliefs through guided discovery.

### 7. tpse007 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: medium

Behavioral activation for depression targets:

**A. [ ]** Unconscious motivation behind depressive symptoms itself
> "Unconscious motivation behind depressive symptoms itself" is not what the item tests — Behavioral activation directly targets the avoidance-inactivity-depression cycle.

**B. [ ]** Relaxation responses to anxiety triggers during counseling
> "Relaxation responses to anxiety triggers during counseling" is not what the item tests — Behavioral activation directly targets the avoidance-inactivity-depression cycle.

**C. [✔]** Withdrawal and inactivity that perpetuate depressed mood
> Behavioral activation directly targets the avoidance-inactivity-depression cycle. Increasing engagement with meaningful or pleasurable activities improves mood and often precedes cognitive change.

**D. [ ]** Replacing negative thoughts with positive ones at intake
> "Replacing negative thoughts with positive ones at intake" is not what the item tests — Behavioral activation directly targets the avoidance-inactivity-depression cycle.

**Rationale:** Behavioral activation directly targets the avoidance-inactivity-depression cycle. Increasing engagement with meaningful or pleasurable activities improves mood and often precedes cognitive change.

---

### 8. tpse008 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: medium

Beck's cognitive triad in depression consists of negative views about:

**A. [ ]** Thoughts, feelings, and behaviors
> "Thoughts, feelings, and behaviors" is not what the item tests — Beck's cognitive triad: negative view of self ("I am defective"), world ("The world is demanding"), and future ("Nothing will improve").

**B. [ ]** Past, present, and future events
> "Past, present, and future events" is not what the item tests — Beck's cognitive triad: negative view of self ("I am defective"), world ("The world is demanding"), and future ("Nothing will improve").

**C. [ ]** Family, work, and relationships
> "Family, work, and relationships" is not what the item tests — Beck's cognitive triad: negative view of self ("I am defective"), world ("The world is demanding"), and future ("Nothing will improve").

**D. [✔]** Self, world, and future
> Beck's cognitive triad: negative view of self ("I am defective"), world ("The world is demanding"), and future ("Nothing will improve"). All three maintain and deepen depressive cognition.

**Rationale:** Beck's cognitive triad: negative view of self ("I am defective"), world ("The world is demanding"), and future ("Nothing will improve"). All three maintain and deepen depressive cognition.

### 9. tpse009 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: hard

Exposure with Response Prevention (ERP) is the primary behavioral intervention for:

**A. [✔]** Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder
> ERP targets OCD's obsession-compulsion cycle. The client is exposed to feared stimuli while refraining from compulsions, allowing anxiety to extinguish through habituation and corrective learning.

**B. [ ]** Social Anxiety Disorder
> "Social Anxiety Disorder" is not what the item tests — ERP targets OCD's obsession-compulsion cycle.

**C. [ ]** Generalized Anxiety Disorder
> "Generalized Anxiety Disorder" is not what the item tests — ERP targets OCD's obsession-compulsion cycle.

**D. [ ]** Specific Phobia
> "Specific Phobia" is not what the item tests — ERP targets OCD's obsession-compulsion cycle.

**Rationale:** ERP targets OCD's obsession-compulsion cycle. The client is exposed to feared stimuli while refraining from compulsions, allowing anxiety to extinguish through habituation and corrective learning.

---

### 10. tpse010 · domain: theories · subdomain: CBT · difficulty: medium

A client says, "I feel anxious, so I must be in danger." This illustrates:

**A. [ ]** Personalization
> "Personalization" is not what the item tests — Emotional reasoning treats feelings as facts ("I feel it, therefore it must be true").

**B. [✔]** Emotional reasoning
> Emotional reasoning treats feelings as facts ("I feel it, therefore it must be true"). The logical error is using an internal emotional state as evidence for an external reality.

**C. [ ]** Magnification
> "Magnification" is not what the item tests — Emotional reasoning treats feelings as facts ("I feel it, therefore it must be true").

**D. [ ]** Fortune telling
> "Fortune telling" is not what the item tests — Emotional reasoning treats feelings as facts ("I feel it, therefore it must be true").

**Rationale:** Emotional reasoning treats feelings as facts ("I feel it, therefore it must be true"). The logical error is using an internal emotional state as evidence for an external reality.

### 11. tpse011 · domain: theories · subdomain: REBT · difficulty: easy

In Ellis's ABC model, "A" = Activating Event, "B" = Beliefs. "C" stands for:

**A. [ ]** Core values
> "Core values" is not what the item tests — In Ellis's ABC model, C = emotional and behavioral Consequences.

**B. [ ]** Cognitions
> "Cognitions" is not what the item tests — In Ellis's ABC model, C = emotional and behavioral Consequences.

**C. [✔]** Consequences (emotional/behavioral)
> In Ellis's ABC model, C = emotional and behavioral Consequences. Key insight: A does not cause C directly — B (beliefs about A) causes C. Disputing (D) irrational beliefs leads to new Effects (E).

**D. [ ]** Coping strategies
> "Coping strategies" is not what the item tests — In Ellis's ABC model, C = emotional and behavioral Consequences.

**Rationale:** In Ellis's ABC model, C = emotional and behavioral Consequences. Key insight: A does not cause C directly — B (beliefs about A) causes C. Disputing (D) irrational beliefs leads to new Effects (E).

---

### 12. tpse012 · domain: theories · subdomain: REBT · difficulty: medium

Which statement BEST represents an irrational belief per REBT?

**A. [ ]** "Some situations are difficult and frustrating." as used now
> ""Some situations are difficult and frustrating." as used now" is not what the item tests — REBT identifies musturbatory thinking as irrational.

**B. [ ]** "I dislike rejection and will work to reduce it." itself
> ""I dislike rejection and will work to reduce it." itself" is not what the item tests — REBT identifies musturbatory thinking as irrational.

**C. [ ]** "I prefer to succeed, though failure is possible." today
> ""I prefer to succeed, though failure is possible." today" is not what the item tests — REBT identifies musturbatory thinking as irrational.

**D. [✔]** "I must be loved and approved of by virtually everyone."
> REBT identifies musturbatory thinking as irrational. "Must be loved by virtually everyone" is Ellis's classic first irrational belief — an absolutistic demand that is empirically false and produces distress when violated.

**Rationale:** REBT identifies musturbatory thinking as irrational. "Must be loved by virtually everyone" is Ellis's classic first irrational belief — an absolutistic demand that is empirically false and produces distress when violated.

### 13. tpse013 · domain: theories · subdomain: REBT · difficulty: hard

REBT distinguishes "unhealthy" from "healthy" negative emotions. This distinction is based on:

**A. [✔]** Whether they stem from rational or irrational beliefs
> REBT distinguishes: concern (healthy, rational) vs. anxiety (unhealthy, irrational); sadness vs. depression; regret vs. guilt. The basis is not intensity or duration but whether the emotion stems from preferences or absolute demands.

**B. [ ]** Duration — healthy emotions are shorter-lived as used
> "Duration — healthy emotions are shorter-lived as used" is not what the item tests — REBT distinguishes: concern (healthy, rational) vs.

**C. [ ]** Whether they prompt help-seeking behavior in practice now
> "Whether they prompt help-seeking behavior in practice now" is not what the item tests — REBT distinguishes: concern (healthy, rational) vs.

**D. [ ]** Intensity — healthy emotions are less intense as used
> "Intensity — healthy emotions are less intense as used" is not what the item tests — REBT distinguishes: concern (healthy, rational) vs.

**Rationale:** REBT distinguishes: concern (healthy, rational) vs. anxiety (unhealthy, irrational); sadness vs. depression; regret vs. guilt. The basis is not intensity or duration but whether the emotion stems from preferences or absolute demands.

### 14. tpse014 · domain: theories · subdomain: REBT · difficulty: medium

The REBT concept of "unconditional self-acceptance" (USA) means:

**A. [ ]** Developing high self-esteem through positive self-talk during a routine session
> "Developing high self-esteem through positive self-talk durin…" is not what the item tests — USA: humans can and should evaluate behaviors but should not globally rate themselves as a person.

**B. [✔]** Rating actions and traits without globally rating the entire self as a person
> USA: humans can and should evaluate behaviors but should not globally rate themselves as a person. This counters self-downing and ego-based disturbance without promoting inflated or conditional self-esteem.

**C. [ ]** Accepting all one's behaviors without evaluation as clinicians typically apply it
> "Accepting all one's behaviors without evaluation as clinicia…" is not what the item tests — USA: humans can and should evaluate behaviors but should not globally rate themselves as a person.

**D. [ ]** Accepting oneself only after meeting core life goals during a routine session
> "Accepting oneself only after meeting core life goals during …" is not what the item tests — USA: humans can and should evaluate behaviors but should not globally rate themselves as a person.

**Rationale:** USA: humans can and should evaluate behaviors but should not globally rate themselves as a person. This counters self-downing and ego-based disturbance without promoting inflated or conditional self-esteem.

### 15. tpse015 · domain: theories · subdomain: Person-Centered · difficulty: easy

Rogers' three core conditions for therapeutic change are:

**A. [ ]** Empathy, boundaries, and confrontation as clinicians typically apply it
> "Empathy, boundaries, and confrontation as clinicians typical…" is not what the item tests — Rogers' necessary and sufficient conditions: congruence (therapist's genuineness/realness), unconditional positive regard (non-judgmental acceptanc

**B. [ ]** Warmth, structure, and psychoeducation as clinicians typically apply it
> "Warmth, structure, and psychoeducation as clinicians typical…" is not what the item tests — Rogers' necessary and sufficient conditions: congruence (therapist's genuineness/realness), unconditional positive regard (non-judgmental acceptanc

**C. [✔]** Congruence, unconditional positive regard, and empathic understanding
> Rogers' necessary and sufficient conditions: congruence (therapist's genuineness/realness), unconditional positive regard (non-judgmental acceptance), and accurate empathic understanding communicated to the client.

**D. [ ]** Acceptance, genuineness, and directiveness at the level the item tests
> "Acceptance, genuineness, and directiveness at the level the …" is not what the item tests — Rogers' necessary and sufficient conditions: congruence (therapist's genuineness/realness), unconditional positive regard (non-judgmental acceptanc

**Rationale:** Rogers' necessary and sufficient conditions: congruence (therapist's genuineness/realness), unconditional positive regard (non-judgmental acceptance), and accurate empathic understanding communicated to the client.

### 16. tpse016 · domain: theories · subdomain: Person-Centered · difficulty: medium

"Conditions of worth" in person-centered theory refers to:

**A. [ ]** The value clients place on the therapeutic relationship as clinicians typically apply it
> "The value clients place on the therapeutic relationship as c…" is not what the item tests — Conditions of worth are introjected standards (from parents, culture) that say love and worth are conditional on meeting certain criteria.

**B. [ ]** External reinforcers for adaptive behavior at intake as the reference material frames it now
> "External reinforcers for adaptive behavior at intake as the …" is not what the item tests — Conditions of worth are introjected standards (from parents, culture) that say love and worth are conditional on meeting certain criteria.

**C. [ ]** Criteria therapists use to determine client progress as the reference material frames it
> "Criteria therapists use to determine client progress as the …" is not what the item tests — Conditions of worth are introjected standards (from parents, culture) that say love and worth are conditional on meeting certain criteria.

**D. [✔]** Internalized standards that cause clients to deny or distort experience to gain approval
> Conditions of worth are introjected standards (from parents, culture) that say love and worth are conditional on meeting certain criteria. They cause denial or distortion of organismic experience, creating incongruence and vulnerability.

**Rationale:** Conditions of worth are introjected standards (from parents, culture) that say love and worth are conditional on meeting certain criteria. They cause denial or distortion of organismic experience, creating incongruence and vulnerability.

> "The process of moving from incongruence to congruence at int…" is not what the item tests — The actualizing tendency is the foundational motivating force in PCT — an inherent, forward-moving directionality toward growth, complexity, and au

> Incongruence is the gap between organismic experience (what one actually feels/experiences) and the self-concept (how one views oneself). This discrepancy produces psychological tension and defensive functioning.

> "The client's stated and actual goals during counseling" is not what the item tests — Incongruence is the gap between organismic experience (what one actually feels/experiences) and the self-concept (how one views oneself).

> "The client's conscious and unconscious motivations now" is not what the item tests — Incongruence is the gap between organismic experience (what one actually feels/experiences) and the self-concept (how one views oneself).

**Rationale:** Incongruence is the gap between organismic experience (what one actually feels/experiences) and the self-concept (how one views oneself). This discrepancy produces psychological tension and defensive functioning.

> PCT positions the therapist as creating facilitative conditions, not directing change. The relational climate — not techniques — allows the client's inherent actualizing tendency to operate.

> "Assign structured exercises to build coping skills as clinic…" is not what the item tests — PCT positions the therapist as creating facilitative conditions, not directing change.

**B. [ ]** Anxiety, guilt, shame, loneliness at intake

> "Birth, growth, suffering, death in practice" is not what the item tests — Yalom's four ultimate concerns: death (inevitability), freedom (radical responsibility for one's choices), isolation (fundamental aloneness), and meaninglessness (ab

**D. [✔]** Death, freedom, isolation, meaninglessness
> Yalom's four ultimate concerns: death (inevitability), freedom (radical responsibility for one's choices), isolation (fundamental aloneness), and meaninglessness (absence of inherent meaning). Each generates existential anxiety.

### 21. tpse021 · domain: theories · subdomain: Existential · difficulty: hard

"Existential anxiety" is distinguished from neurotic anxiety because:

**A. [✔]** Existential anxiety arises from confronting inescapable realities of human existence
> Existential anxiety is ontological — arising from confronting givens like death, freedom, and meaninglessness.

**B. [ ]** Existential anxiety responds to medication; neurotic anxiety does not in this domain
> "Existential anxiety responds to medication; neurotic anxiety…" is not what the item tests — Existential anxiety is ontological — arising from confronting givens like death, freedom, and meaninglessness.

**C. [ ]** Neurotic anxiety is more severe and disabling by definition during a routine session
> "Neurotic anxiety is more severe and disabling by definition …" is not what the item tests — Existential anxiety is ontological — arising from confronting givens like death, freedom, and meaninglessness.

**D. [ ]** Existential anxiety occurs only in older adults confronting death at the intake stage
> "Existential anxiety occurs only in older adults confronting …" is not what the item tests — Existential anxiety is ontological — arising from confronting givens like death, freedom, and meaninglessness.

**Rationale:** Existential anxiety is ontological — arising from confronting givens like death, freedom, and meaninglessness. It is normal and unavoidable. Neurotic anxiety is a defense against this authentic anxiety rather than a direct confrontation with it.

> "Developing authentic relationships through genuine encounter…" is not what the item tests — Logotherapy (Frankl) holds that the primary human motivation is will to meaning.

> "Reducing anxiety through acceptance of existential givens du…" is not what the item tests — Logotherapy (Frankl) holds that the primary human motivation is will to meaning.

**Rationale:** Logotherapy (Frankl) holds that the primary human motivation is will to meaning. Even in unavoidable suffering, meaning can be found. Techniques include paradoxical intention and dereflection.

> Bad faith = self-deception in which one denies radical freedom by claiming one "had no choice." Clinically, clients in bad faith externalize responsibility for their lives, avoiding the anxiety of authentic choosing.

### 30. tpse030 · domain: theories · subdomain: Gestalt · difficulty: hard

Which Gestalt concept describes the process of turning an impulse back on oneself rather than directing it toward the environment?

**A. [ ]** Projection
> "Projection" is not what the item tests — Retroflection = redirecting toward oneself what one wants to do to others (e.g., self-harm instead of expressing anger).

**B. [✔]** Retroflection
> Retroflection = redirecting toward oneself what one wants to do to others (e.g., self-harm instead of expressing anger).

**C. [ ]** Introjection
> "Introjection" is not what the item tests — Retroflection = redirecting toward oneself what one wants to do to others (e.g., self-harm instead of expressing anger).

**D. [ ]** Deflection
> "Deflection" is not what the item tests — Retroflection = redirecting toward oneself what one wants to do to others (e.g., self-harm instead of expressing anger).

**Rationale:** Retroflection = redirecting toward oneself what one wants to do to others (e.g., self-harm instead of expressing anger). Projection = attributing inner experience to others; deflection = avoiding contact; introjection = swallowing whole without processing.

### 34. tpse034 · domain: theories · subdomain: SFBT · difficulty: hard

> "Situations they have avoided due to the problem at intake" is not what the item tests — Exception questions explore times the problem does not happen or is less present.

> Exception questions explore times the problem does not happen or is less present. These exceptions contain solution information — what was the client doing differently? Exceptions build on existing strengths rather than analyzing deficits.

**C. [ ]** People they consider exceptions to their social difficulties
> "People they consider exceptions to their social difficulties" is not what the item tests — Exception questions explore times the problem does not happen or is less present.

**D. [ ]** Times when they felt their problem was most severe today
> "Times when they felt their problem was most severe today" is not what the item tests — Exception questions explore times the problem does not happen or is less present.

**Rationale:** Exception questions explore times the problem does not happen or is less present. These exceptions contain solution information — what was the client doing differently? Exceptions build on existing strengths rather than analyzing deficits.

> "Cognitive behavioral theory" is not what the item tests — SFBT is grounded in social constructionism — the idea that reality and meaning are constructed through language and social interaction.

**Rationale:** SFBT is grounded in social constructionism — the idea that reality and meaning are constructed through language and social interaction. Problems and solutions are both linguistic constructions, not fixed internal realities.

> "Sharing therapy content with support systems for feedback it…" is not what the item tests — Externalization objectifies the problem through language: "How has Depression been influencing you?" not "How does your depression affect you?" Thi

> "Cultural ceremonies incorporated into the therapeutic proces…" is not what the item tests — Definitional ceremonies use outsider witnesses — people who listen to the client's re-authored narrative, resonate with specific elements, and refl

**B. [ ]** Formal diagnostic rituals conducted at intake as clinicians typically apply it
> "Formal diagnostic rituals conducted at intake as clinicians …" is not what the item tests — Definitional ceremonies use outsider witnesses — people who listen to the client's re-authored narrative, resonate with specific elements, and refl

**C. [✔]** Outsider witnesses who respond to the client's preferred alternative narrative
> Definitional ceremonies use outsider witnesses — people who listen to the client's re-authored narrative, resonate with specific elements, and reflect that resonance back. This 'thickens' the preferred story through community witnessing.

**D. [ ]** Formal termination rituals marking the end of therapy during a routine session
> "Formal termination rituals marking the end of therapy during…" is not what the item tests — Definitional ceremonies use outsider witnesses — people who listen to the client's re-authored narrative, resonate with specific elements, and refl

**Rationale:** Definitional ceremonies use outsider witnesses — people who listen to the client's re-authored narrative, resonate with specific elements, and reflect that resonance back. This 'thickens' the preferred story through community witnessing.

### 40. tpse040 · domain: theories · subdomain: ACT · difficulty: medium

> "Adapt social behavior flexibly to meet situational demands n…" is not what the item tests — Psychological flexibility is ACT's core goal: fully contacting the present moment, accepting thoughts/feelings without defense, defusing from unhel

> "Change negative thoughts to positive ones systematically now…" is not what the item tests — Psychological flexibility is ACT's core goal: fully contacting the present moment, accepting thoughts/feelings without defense, defusing from unhel

> Psychological flexibility is ACT's core goal: fully contacting the present moment, accepting thoughts/feelings without defense, defusing from unhelpful language, connecting with self-as-context, clarifying values, and ta…

### 41. tpse041 · domain: theories · subdomain: ACT · difficulty: hard

"Cognitive defusion" in ACT aims to:

**A. [✔]** Change the relationship with thoughts so they have less behavioral influence
> Defusion does not dispute or change thoughts — it changes the relationship with them: seeing thoughts as words/events in the mind rather than literal facts.

**B. [ ]** Suppress unwanted thoughts through mindful awareness during a routine session
> "Suppress unwanted thoughts through mindful awareness during …" is not what the item tests — Defusion does not dispute or change thoughts — it changes the relationship with them: seeing thoughts as words/events in the mind rather than liter

**C. [ ]** Replace negative thoughts with more accurate alternative thoughts in practice
> "Replace negative thoughts with more accurate alternative tho…" is not what the item tests — Defusion does not dispute or change thoughts — it changes the relationship with them: seeing thoughts as words/events in the mind rather than liter

**D. [ ]** Challenge the validity of unhelpful thoughts as clinicians typically apply it
> "Challenge the validity of unhelpful thoughts as clinicians t…" is not what the item tests — Defusion does not dispute or change thoughts — it changes the relationship with them: seeing thoughts as words/events in the mind rather than liter

**Rationale:** Defusion does not dispute or change thoughts — it changes the relationship with them: seeing thoughts as words/events in the mind rather than literal facts. Techniques (singing thoughts, 'I notice I'm having the thought that...') create psychological distance.

### 42. tpse042 · domain: theories · subdomain: ACT · difficulty: medium

> "Avoiding painful memories through dissociation as used as th…" is not what the item tests — Experiential avoidance = struggling against unwanted internal states (thoughts, feelings, sensations).

**B. [✔]** Efforts to suppress or escape unwanted internal experiences, even when doing so is harmful
> Experiential avoidance = struggling against unwanted internal states (thoughts, feelings, sensations).

**C. [ ]** Avoiding challenging therapeutic exercises in practice as the reference material frames it now
> "Avoiding challenging therapeutic exercises in practice as th…" is not what the item tests — Experiential avoidance = struggling against unwanted internal states (thoughts, feelings, sensations).

**D. [ ]** Behavioral avoidance of feared external situations now as the reference material frames it
> "Behavioral avoidance of feared external situations now as th…" is not what the item tests — Experiential avoidance = struggling against unwanted internal states (thoughts, feelings, sensations).

**Rationale:** Experiential avoidance = struggling against unwanted internal states (thoughts, feelings, sensations). ACT treats it as a primary driver of psychopathology — the attempt to avoid suffering causes more suffering than the experiences themselves.

> "Determined by the therapist in collaboration with the client…" is not what the item tests — Values in ACT are ongoing directions (e.g., "being loving," "being courageous") that can never be permanently completed — unlike goals.

> "Substance Use Disorders in adolescents during counseling" is not what the item tests — Marsha Linehan developed DBT specifically for chronically suicidal individuals with BPD who did not respond to standard CBT.

> Marsha Linehan developed DBT specifically for chronically suicidal individuals with BPD who did not respond to standard CBT. It has since been adapted for eating disorders, substance use, and adolescent populations.

**Rationale:** Marsha Linehan developed DBT specifically for chronically suicidal individuals with BPD who did not respond to standard CBT. It has since been adapted for eating disorders, substance use, and adolescent populations.

### 49. tpse049 · domain: theories · subdomain: Psychodynamic · difficulty: hard

> "Returning to an earlier developmental stage under stress" is not what the item tests — Reaction formation transforms an unacceptable impulse into its opposite (e.g., intense hatred expressed as exaggerated love).

> "Converting anxiety into physical symptoms at intake" is not what the item tests — Reaction formation transforms an unacceptable impulse into its opposite (e.g., intense hatred expressed as exaggerated love).

### 52. tpse052 · domain: pioneers · subdomain: Freud · difficulty: medium

**B. [ ]** Oral, anal, genital, latency, phallic
> "Oral, anal, genital, latency, phallic" is not what the item tests — Freud's psychosexual stages: oral (0–18 mo) → anal (18 mo–3 yr) → phallic/Oedipal (3–6 yr) → latency (6–12 yr) → genital (puberty+).

> "As a student of Freud in Vienna during counseling" is not what the item tests — Frankl (1905–1997) survived Auschwitz, Dachau, and other camps.

> "Encouraging clients to intensify their fear response deliber…" is not what the item tests — Paradoxical intention: client humorously intends to do the very thing they fear (e.g., someone fearing they'll sweat is instructed to 'try to sweat

**B. [✔]** Having clients humorously intend or wish for what they fear, breaking the anticipatory anxiety cycle
> Paradoxical intention: client humorously intends to do the very thing they fear (e.g., someone fearing they'll sweat is instructed to 'try to sweat as much as possible').

**C. [ ]** Setting goals opposite to stated desires to expose ambivalence now as the reference material frames it
> "Setting goals opposite to stated desires to expose ambivalen…" is not what the item tests — Paradoxical intention: client humorously intends to do the very thing they fear (e.g., someone fearing they'll sweat is instructed to 'try to sweat

> "Intentionally creating paradoxical situations to bypass defe…" is not what the item tests — Paradoxical intention: client humorously intends to do the very thing they fear (e.g., someone fearing they'll sweat is instructed to 'try to sweat

**Rationale:** Paradoxical intention: client humorously intends to do the very thing they fear (e.g., someone fearing they'll sweat is instructed to 'try to sweat as much as possible'). This breaks the anticipatory anxiety feedback loop through humor and detachment.

### 68. tpse068 · domain: pioneers · subdomain: Satir · difficulty: hard

**B. [ ]** Aggression, withdrawal, intellectualization, projection

**D. [✔]** Placating, blaming, super-reasonable, irrelevant
