> "Interpret the underlying meaning of the client's communicati…" is not what the item tests — Paraphrasing communicates understanding of content in the therapist's own words, invites correction if inaccurate, and encourages the client to con

**B. [ ]** Summarize everything the client said verbatim during a routine session
> "Summarize everything the client said verbatim during a routi…" is not what the item tests — Paraphrasing communicates understanding of content in the therapist's own words, invites correction if inaccurate, and encourages the client to con

**C. [✔]** Communicate that the client has been accurately heard and understood
> Paraphrasing communicates understanding of content in the therapist's own words, invites correction if inaccurate, and encourages the client to continue and elaborate.

**D. [ ]** Introduce new perspectives on the client's situation during counseling
> "Introduce new perspectives on the client's situation during …" is not what the item tests — Paraphrasing communicates understanding of content in the therapist's own words, invites correction if inaccurate, and encourages the client to con

**Rationale:** Paraphrasing communicates understanding of content in the therapist's own words, invites correction if inaccurate, and encourages the client to continue and elaborate. It does not add interpretation — that is the job of advanced empathy or interpretation.

---

### 84. tpse084 · domain: skills · subdomain: Empathy · difficulty: medium

"Advanced empathy" (vs. basic empathy) involves:

**A. [ ]** Empathizing with more intense emotions like shame and rage today
> "Empathizing with more intense emotions like shame and rage t…" is not what the item tests — Advanced/accurate empathy (Egan, Carkhuff) reflects the 'edge of awareness' — what the client is almost but not quite saying.

**B. [ ]** Using more complex feeling words in reflections during counseling
> "Using more complex feeling words in reflections during couns…" is not what the item tests — Advanced/accurate empathy (Egan, Carkhuff) reflects the 'edge of awareness' — what the client is almost but not quite saying.

**C. [ ]** Maintaining empathic consistency across multiple sessions itself
> "Maintaining empathic consistency across multiple sessions it…" is not what the item tests — Advanced/accurate empathy (Egan, Carkhuff) reflects the 'edge of awareness' — what the client is almost but not quite saying.

**D. [✔]** Reflecting what the client has implied but not yet said directly
> Advanced/accurate empathy (Egan, Carkhuff) reflects the 'edge of awareness' — what the client is almost but not quite saying. It goes beyond the surface to the deeper implied meaning without being a full interpretation.

**Rationale:** Advanced/accurate empathy (Egan, Carkhuff) reflects the 'edge of awareness' — what the client is almost but not quite saying. It goes beyond the surface to the deeper implied meaning without being a full interpretation.

---

### 85. tpse085 · domain: skills · subdomain: Questioning · difficulty: easy

Open-ended questions differ from closed questions primarily because they:

**A. [✔]** Cannot be answered with "yes" or "no" and invite elaboration
> Open-ended questions (who, what, where, when, how) invite narrative and elaboration. Closed questions can be answered with yes/no or a single word and are used for specific information-gathering but limit exploration.

**B. [ ]** Focus on feelings rather than factual content in this domain now
> "Focus on feelings rather than factual content in this domain…" is not what the item tests — Open-ended questions (who, what, where, when, how) invite narrative and elaboration.

**C. [ ]** Are reserved for later phases of the therapeutic process now
> "Are reserved for later phases of the therapeutic process now" is not what the item tests — Open-ended questions (who, what, where, when, how) invite narrative and elaboration.

**D. [ ]** Are typically longer and require more time to formulate here
> "Are typically longer and require more time to formulate here" is not what the item tests — Open-ended questions (who, what, where, when, how) invite narrative and elaboration.

**Rationale:** Open-ended questions (who, what, where, when, how) invite narrative and elaboration. Closed questions can be answered with yes/no or a single word and are used for specific information-gathering but limit exploration.

---

### 86. tpse086 · domain: skills · subdomain: Questioning · difficulty: medium

"Why" questions are generally considered least therapeutically useful because:

**A. [ ]** They close off exploration by requiring a specific answer during counseling
> "They close off exploration by requiring a specific answer du…" is not what the item tests — "Why did you do that?" often triggers defensiveness and rationalization rather than genuine exploration.

**B. [✔]** They prompt defensiveness, require rationalization, and can imply judgment
> "Why did you do that?" often triggers defensiveness and rationalization rather than genuine exploration. "What were you hoping would happen?" or "What was going on for you?" elicits richer, less defensive responses.

**C. [ ]** They are too abstract for most clients to answer meaningfully by counselors
> "They are too abstract for most clients to answer meaningfull…" is not what the item tests — "Why did you do that?" often triggers defensiveness and rationalization rather than genuine exploration.

**D. [ ]** They focus on the past rather than the present moment as usually described
> "They focus on the past rather than the present moment as usu…" is not what the item tests — "Why did you do that?" often triggers defensiveness and rationalization rather than genuine exploration.

**Rationale:** "Why did you do that?" often triggers defensiveness and rationalization rather than genuine exploration. "What were you hoping would happen?" or "What was going on for you?" elicits richer, less defensive responses.

---

### 87. tpse087 · domain: skills · subdomain: Confrontation · difficulty: medium

Therapeutic confrontation is BEST described as:

**A. [ ]** Addressing problems in the therapeutic alliance directly at the intake stage
> "Addressing problems in the therapeutic alliance directly at …" is not what the item tests — Confrontation identifies discrepancies: between what a client says and does, between two statements, or between stated beliefs and nonverbal behavi

**B. [ ]** Directly challenging the client's problematic behaviors or choices at intake
> "Directly challenging the client's problematic behaviors or c…" is not what the item tests — Confrontation identifies discrepancies: between what a client says and does, between two statements, or between stated beliefs and nonverbal behavi

**C. [✔]** Pointing out discrepancies or inconsistencies in the client's communication
> Confrontation identifies discrepancies: between what a client says and does, between two statements, or between stated beliefs and nonverbal behavior.

**D. [ ]** Using forceful language to break through defensive patterns during counseling
> "Using forceful language to break through defensive patterns …" is not what the item tests — Confrontation identifies discrepancies: between what a client says and does, between two statements, or between stated beliefs and nonverbal behavi

**Rationale:** Confrontation identifies discrepancies: between what a client says and does, between two statements, or between stated beliefs and nonverbal behavior. It is offered as a caring observation, not an attack, and requires a solid therapeutic alliance.

---

### 88. tpse088 · domain: skills · subdomain: Confrontation · difficulty: hard

Timing of confrontation is MOST critical because:

**A. [ ]** Early confrontation accelerates client awareness of their problems during counseling
> "Early confrontation accelerates client awareness of their pr…" is not what the item tests — Premature confrontation before a therapeutic alliance is established frequently leads to dropout, increased defensiveness, or rupture.

**B. [ ]** Confrontation should only occur in the termination phase of treatment by counselors
> "Confrontation should only occur in the termination phase of …" is not what the item tests — Premature confrontation before a therapeutic alliance is established frequently leads to dropout, increased defensiveness, or rupture.

**C. [ ]** Clients cannot tolerate confrontation until they demonstrate significant insight now
> "Clients cannot tolerate confrontation until they demonstrate…" is not what the item tests — Premature confrontation before a therapeutic alliance is established frequently leads to dropout, increased defensiveness, or rupture.

**D. [✔]** Confrontation before alliance is established increases dropout and damages rapport
> Premature confrontation before a therapeutic alliance is established frequently leads to dropout, increased defensiveness, or rupture. Clients must experience confrontation as caring rather than attacking — which requires sufficient trust.

**Rationale:** Premature confrontation before a therapeutic alliance is established frequently leads to dropout, increased defensiveness, or rupture. Clients must experience confrontation as caring rather than attacking — which requires sufficient trust.

---

### 89. tpse089 · domain: skills · subdomain: Immediacy · difficulty: medium

"Immediacy" as a counseling skill involves the therapist:

**A. [✔]** Discussing what is happening in the therapeutic relationship in the present moment
> Immediacy (Egan): discussing the here-and-now quality of the therapeutic relationship.

**B. [ ]** Addressing crisis concerns immediately before any other therapeutic content itself
> "Addressing crisis concerns immediately before any other ther…" is not what the item tests — Immediacy (Egan): discussing the here-and-now quality of the therapeutic relationship.

**C. [ ]** Providing prompt behavioral feedback after client role-plays during a routine session
> "Providing prompt behavioral feedback after client role-plays…" is not what the item tests — Immediacy (Egan): discussing the here-and-now quality of the therapeutic relationship.

**D. [ ]** Responding without pause to all client statements as clinicians typically apply it
> "Responding without pause to all client statements as clinici…" is not what the item tests — Immediacy (Egan): discussing the here-and-now quality of the therapeutic relationship.

**Rationale:** Immediacy (Egan): discussing the here-and-now quality of the therapeutic relationship. "Right now, as we're talking about this, I notice..." It brings the relationship itself into the open as a therapeutic tool and addresses parallel processes.

---

### 90. tpse090 · domain: skills · subdomain: Summarizing · difficulty: easy

Summarizing in counseling is MOST useful for:

**A. [ ]** Collecting specific assessment data efficiently now as the reference material frames it
> "Collecting specific assessment data efficiently now as the r…" is not what the item tests — Summarizing ties together multiple aspects of the session, provides transitions between topics, checks accuracy of the therapist's understanding, a

**B. [✔]** Reviewing key themes, marking transitions, and checking for accuracy of understanding
> Summarizing ties together multiple aspects of the session, provides transitions between topics, checks accuracy of the therapist's understanding, and gives the client a sense of coherence and direction.

**C. [ ]** Replacing reflection when sessions become repetitive as clinicians typically apply it
> "Replacing reflection when sessions become repetitive as clin…" is not what the item tests — Summarizing ties together multiple aspects of the session, provides transitions between topics, checks accuracy of the therapist's understanding, a

**D. [ ]** Providing clinical interpretation of unconscious material at the level the item tests
> "Providing clinical interpretation of unconscious material at…" is not what the item tests — Summarizing ties together multiple aspects of the session, provides transitions between topics, checks accuracy of the therapist's understanding, a

**Rationale:** Summarizing ties together multiple aspects of the session, provides transitions between topics, checks accuracy of the therapist's understanding, and gives the client a sense of coherence and direction.

---

### 91. tpse091 · domain: skills · subdomain: SelfDisclosure · difficulty: medium

Appropriate counselor self-disclosure is BEST characterized as:

**A. [ ]** Revealing personal history to build the therapeutic alliance early in treatment in this domain
> "Revealing personal history to build the therapeutic alliance…" is not what the item tests — Appropriate self-disclosure is selective, purposeful, and client-focused.

**B. [ ]** Sharing personal struggles to increase empathic connection as the reference material frames it now
> "Sharing personal struggles to increase empathic connection a…" is not what the item tests — Appropriate self-disclosure is selective, purposeful, and client-focused.

**C. [✔]** Deliberate, purposeful sharing of the therapist's experience in service of the client's growth
> Appropriate self-disclosure is selective, purposeful, and client-focused. Key question: 'Why am I sharing this? For whom?' It should not burden the client or shift focus to the therapist, and should return focus to the client promptly.

**D. [ ]** Avoided entirely to maintain therapeutic neutrality and abstinence at the level the item tests
> "Avoided entirely to maintain therapeutic neutrality and abst…" is not what the item tests — Appropriate self-disclosure is selective, purposeful, and client-focused.

**Rationale:** Appropriate self-disclosure is selective, purposeful, and client-focused. Key question: 'Why am I sharing this? For whom?' It should not burden the client or shift focus to the therapist, and should return focus to the client promptly.

---

### 92. tpse092 · domain: skills · subdomain: GoalSetting · difficulty: medium

Counseling goals are MOST effective when they are:

**A. [ ]** Set by the counselor based on their clinical assessment in practice
> "Set by the counselor based on their clinical assessment in p…" is not what the item tests — SMART goals provide clear direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and maintain client motivation through achievable steps.

**B. [ ]** Focused on symptom elimination as the singular target by counselors
> "Focused on symptom elimination as the singular target by cou…" is not what the item tests — SMART goals provide clear direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and maintain client motivation through achievable steps.

**C. [ ]** Broadly stated to allow flexible interpretation across sessions now
> "Broadly stated to allow flexible interpretation across sessi…" is not what the item tests — SMART goals provide clear direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and maintain client motivation through achievable steps.

**D. [✔]** Specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART)
> SMART goals provide clear direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and maintain client motivation through achievable steps. Vague goals make progress evaluation difficult and can undermine the therapeutic alliance over time.

**Rationale:** SMART goals provide clear direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and maintain client motivation through achievable steps. Vague goals make progress evaluation difficult and can undermine the therapeutic alliance over time.

---

### 93. tpse093 · domain: skills · subdomain: Termination · difficulty: medium

Which statement about termination is MOST clinically accurate?

**A. [✔]** Termination is a therapeutic opportunity to consolidate gains and process separation
> Properly handled termination consolidates gains, reviews progress, processes the experience of ending, and prepares the client for the future.

**B. [ ]** Termination should occur abruptly when clinical goals are met to prevent dependency now
> "Termination should occur abruptly when clinical goals are me…" is not what the item tests — Properly handled termination consolidates gains, reviews progress, processes the experience of ending, and prepares the client for the future.

**C. [ ]** Termination should be avoided or indefinitely deferred with clients who have attachment difficulties
> "Termination should be avoided or indefinitely deferred with …" is not what the item tests — Properly handled termination consolidates gains, reviews progress, processes the experience of ending, and prepares the client for the future.

**D. [ ]** A client's resistance to termination typically indicates insufficient clinical progress
> "A client's resistance to termination typically indicates ins…" is not what the item tests — Properly handled termination consolidates gains, reviews progress, processes the experience of ending, and prepares the client for the future.

**Rationale:** Properly handled termination consolidates gains, reviews progress, processes the experience of ending, and prepares the client for the future. For clients with attachment difficulties, a well-handled ending is a valuable corrective relational experience.

---

### 94. tpse094 · domain: skills · subdomain: Termination · difficulty: hard

A client making good progress suddenly misses sessions as planned termination approaches. This MOST likely reflects:

**A. [ ]** Resistance indicating the need to extend therapy indefinitely by counselors
> "Resistance indicating the need to extend therapy indefinitel…" is not what the item tests — "Flight into health" = sudden apparent improvement or avoidance before termination.

**B. [✔]** Flight into health or termination anxiety requiring direct clinical address
> "Flight into health" = sudden apparent improvement or avoidance before termination.

**C. [ ]** Successful internalization of skills and readiness for independent functioning
> "Successful internalization of skills and readiness for indep…" is not what the item tests — "Flight into health" = sudden apparent improvement or avoidance before termination.

**D. [ ]** External logistical barriers such as transportation or scheduling at intake
> "External logistical barriers such as transportation or sched…" is not what the item tests — "Flight into health" = sudden apparent improvement or avoidance before termination.

**Rationale:** "Flight into health" = sudden apparent improvement or avoidance before termination. May represent anxiety about the ending, avoidance of grief about the loss of the relationship, or fear of coping independently. Warrants direct, curious clinical exploration.

---

### 95. tpse095 · domain: skills · subdomain: Resistance · difficulty: medium

A client consistently arrives late, changes subjects when specific topics arise, and gives vague answers. The counselor should FIRST:

**A. [ ]** Address this as a boundary violation and set firm consequences during counseling
> "Address this as a boundary violation and set firm consequenc…" is not what the item tests — Resistance is typically self-protective and clinically meaningful — indicating areas of anxiety or ambivalence.

**B. [ ]** Terminate treatment for repeated non-compliance as clinicians typically apply it
> "Terminate treatment for repeated non-compliance as clinician…" is not what the item tests — Resistance is typically self-protective and clinically meaningful — indicating areas of anxiety or ambivalence.

**C. [✔]** Explore the pattern with curiosity as potentially meaningful clinical material
> Resistance is typically self-protective and clinically meaningful — indicating areas of anxiety or ambivalence.

**D. [ ]** Ask directly whether the client wants to continue in therapy during counseling
> "Ask directly whether the client wants to continue in therapy…" is not what the item tests — Resistance is typically self-protective and clinically meaningful — indicating areas of anxiety or ambivalence.

**Rationale:** Resistance is typically self-protective and clinically meaningful — indicating areas of anxiety or ambivalence. A curious, non-judgmental exploration ('I notice when we approach X, the topic shifts — I wonder what that might be about') is more productive than consequences.

---

### 96. tpse096 · domain: skills · subdomain: AllianceRupture · difficulty: hard

Research on therapeutic alliance ruptures consistently shows that:

**A. [ ]** Ruptures typically lead to premature termination if not immediately repaired
> "Ruptures typically lead to premature termination if not imme…" is not what the item tests — Rupture and repair research (Safran, Muran): negotiating alliance strains can be among the most therapeutic experiences in treatment — especially f

**B. [ ]** Ruptures occur only with clients who have personality disorder diagnoses now
> "Ruptures occur only with clients who have personality disord…" is not what the item tests — Rupture and repair research (Safran, Muran): negotiating alliance strains can be among the most therapeutic experiences in treatment — especially f

**C. [ ]** The therapist should acknowledge ruptures but not explore them to avoid escalation
> "The therapist should acknowledge ruptures but not explore th…" is not what the item tests — Rupture and repair research (Safran, Muran): negotiating alliance strains can be among the most therapeutic experiences in treatment — especially f

**D. [✔]** Repaired ruptures can actually strengthen the alliance and improve outcomes
> Rupture and repair research (Safran, Muran): negotiating alliance strains can be among the most therapeutic experiences in treatment — especially for clients with relational trauma.

**Rationale:** Rupture and repair research (Safran, Muran): negotiating alliance strains can be among the most therapeutic experiences in treatment — especially for clients with relational trauma. The therapist's capacity to acknowledge, explore, and repair is more predictive of outcomes than avoiding rupture.

---

### 97. tpse097 · domain: skills · subdomain: Countertransference · difficulty: medium

Managing countertransference effectively requires the counselor to PRIMARILY:

**A. [✔]** Develop self-awareness to recognize and use their reactions as clinical information
> Countertransference management: develop self-awareness (personal therapy, supervision) to recognize reactions, understand their source, and use them as data about the client's interpersonal dynamics — not as facts about…

**B. [ ]** Share countertransference reactions with clients to model transparency by counselors
> "Share countertransference reactions with clients to model tr…" is not what the item tests — Countertransference management: develop self-awareness (personal therapy, supervision) to recognize reactions, understand their source, and use the

**C. [ ]** Refer clients who trigger strong emotional reactions as clinicians typically apply it
> "Refer clients who trigger strong emotional reactions as clin…" is not what the item tests — Countertransference management: develop self-awareness (personal therapy, supervision) to recognize reactions, understand their source, and use the

**D. [ ]** Remain emotionally neutral and unaffected by all client material at the intake stage
> "Remain emotionally neutral and unaffected by all client mate…" is not what the item tests — Countertransference management: develop self-awareness (personal therapy, supervision) to recognize reactions, understand their source, and use the

**Rationale:** Countertransference management: develop self-awareness (personal therapy, supervision) to recognize reactions, understand their source, and use them as data about the client's interpersonal dynamics — not as facts about the client, nor material to always suppress or always disclose.

---

### 98. tpse098 · domain: skills · subdomain: CrisisIntervention · difficulty: medium

In crisis intervention, after ensuring immediate safety the NEXT priority is to:

**A. [ ]** Contact family members for collateral information immediately during a routine session
> "Contact family members for collateral information immediatel…" is not what the item tests — After safety: establish connection, listen actively, understand the precipitating crisis from the client's own frame of reference.

**B. [✔]** Establish rapport and understand the precipitating event from the client's perspective
> After safety: establish connection, listen actively, understand the precipitating crisis from the client's own frame of reference. Comprehensive assessment follows but is not the first task — the person must first feel genuinely heard.

**C. [ ]** Complete a comprehensive diagnostic intake assessment as clinicians typically apply it
> "Complete a comprehensive diagnostic intake assessment as cli…" is not what the item tests — After safety: establish connection, listen actively, understand the precipitating crisis from the client's own frame of reference.

**D. [ ]** Provide psychoeducation about the client's presenting disorder during a routine session
> "Provide psychoeducation about the client's presenting disord…" is not what the item tests — After safety: establish connection, listen actively, understand the precipitating crisis from the client's own frame of reference.

**Rationale:** After safety: establish connection, listen actively, understand the precipitating crisis from the client's own frame of reference. Comprehensive assessment follows but is not the first task — the person must first feel genuinely heard.

---

### 99. tpse099 · domain: skills · subdomain: GroupSkills · difficulty: medium

Which of Yalom's therapeutic factors involves group members realizing others share their struggles?

**A. [ ]** Altruism
> "Altruism" is not what the item tests — Universality (Yalom): the powerful relief clients experience when discovering others share their shame, fears, and difficulties — 'I'm not alone in this.' Altruism = giving to others; cohesiveness = s

**B. [ ]** Imparting information
> "Imparting information" is not what the item tests — Universality (Yalom): the powerful relief clients experience when discovering others share their shame, fears, and difficulties — 'I'm not alone in this.' Altruism = giving to others; coh

**C. [✔]** Universality
> Universality (Yalom): the powerful relief clients experience when discovering others share their shame, fears, and difficulties — 'I'm not alone in this.' Altruism = giving to others; cohesiveness = sense of belonging; i…

**D. [ ]** Group cohesiveness
> "Group cohesiveness" is not what the item tests — Universality (Yalom): the powerful relief clients experience when discovering others share their shame, fears, and difficulties — 'I'm not alone in this.' Altruism = giving to others; cohesi

**Rationale:** Universality (Yalom): the powerful relief clients experience when discovering others share their shame, fears, and difficulties — 'I'm not alone in this.' Altruism = giving to others; cohesiveness = sense of belonging; imparting information = psychoeducation.

---

### 100. tpse100 · domain: skills · subdomain: GroupSkills · difficulty: hard

In group counseling, a leader should be MOST clinically concerned about a member who:

**A. [ ]** Frequently and directly challenges the group leader's perspectives now
> "Frequently and directly challenges the group leader's perspe…" is not what the item tests — Prolonged silence with increasing withdrawal may indicate escalating distress, deepening isolation, or suicidal ideation.

**B. [ ]** Shows consistent empathy toward other members' disclosures in practice
> "Shows consistent empathy toward other members' disclosures i…" is not what the item tests — Prolonged silence with increasing withdrawal may indicate escalating distress, deepening isolation, or suicidal ideation.

**C. [ ]** Shares emotional material and cries openly in group at the intake stage
> "Shares emotional material and cries openly in group at the i…" is not what the item tests — Prolonged silence with increasing withdrawal may indicate escalating distress, deepening isolation, or suicidal ideation.

**D. [✔]** Remains silent for several weeks with increasing behavioral withdrawal
> Prolonged silence with increasing withdrawal may indicate escalating distress, deepening isolation, or suicidal ideation.

**Rationale:** Prolonged silence with increasing withdrawal may indicate escalating distress, deepening isolation, or suicidal ideation. A challenging member is typically engaged; a progressively withdrawn member warrants individual clinical attention and direct contact.

---

### 101. tpse101 · domain: skills · subdomain: Psychoeducation · difficulty: medium

Psychoeducation in counseling is MOST appropriately used to:

**A. [✔]** Provide accurate information that reduces stigma and normalizes experience
> Psychoeducation: deliberate teaching of clinical concepts to reduce stigma, increase illness understanding, and support treatment engagement.

**B. [ ]** Replace exploratory work when clients resist personal disclosure at intake
> "Replace exploratory work when clients resist personal disclo…" is not what the item tests — Psychoeducation: deliberate teaching of clinical concepts to reduce stigma, increase illness understanding, and support treatment engagement.

**C. [ ]** Direct clients toward specific treatment decisions the clinician recommends
> "Direct clients toward specific treatment decisions the clini…" is not what the item tests — Psychoeducation: deliberate teaching of clinical concepts to reduce stigma, increase illness understanding, and support treatment engagement.

**D. [ ]** Fill session time when clients are not ready to discuss personal material now
> "Fill session time when clients are not ready to discuss pers…" is not what the item tests — Psychoeducation: deliberate teaching of clinical concepts to reduce stigma, increase illness understanding, and support treatment engagement.

**Rationale:** Psychoeducation: deliberate teaching of clinical concepts to reduce stigma, increase illness understanding, and support treatment engagement. Most effective when tailored to the client's current level and not used as an avoidance of exploratory work.

---

### 102. tpse102 · domain: skills · subdomain: Interpretation · difficulty: hard

A well-timed and accurate clinical interpretation should:

**A. [ ]** Reveal the definitive unconscious meaning behind the client's behavior at the intake stage
> "Reveal the definitive unconscious meaning behind the client'…" is not what the item tests — Effective interpretation: offered tentatively ('I wonder if...'), close to the edge of awareness (not too deep), and invites the client's response

**B. [✔]** Be offered tentatively, close to the client's current awareness, and invite their response
> Effective interpretation: offered tentatively ('I wonder if...'), close to the edge of awareness (not too deep), and invites the client's response rather than presenting as established fact.

**C. [ ]** Clearly explain what the client's behavior means based on clinical theory during counseling
> "Clearly explain what the client's behavior means based on cl…" is not what the item tests — Effective interpretation: offered tentatively ('I wonder if...'), close to the edge of awareness (not too deep), and invites the client's response

**D. [ ]** Be provided only when explicitly requested by the client as the reference material frames it
> "Be provided only when explicitly requested by the client as …" is not what the item tests — Effective interpretation: offered tentatively ('I wonder if...'), close to the edge of awareness (not too deep), and invites the client's response

**Rationale:** Effective interpretation: offered tentatively ('I wonder if...'), close to the edge of awareness (not too deep), and invites the client's response rather than presenting as established fact. Premature or deep interpretations create defensiveness rather than insight.

---

### 103. tpse103 · domain: skills · subdomain: Reframing · difficulty: medium

A counselor reframes a client's "weakness" in crying as "the courage to feel deeply." This technique:

**A. [ ]** Is inherently manipulative and should be avoided in ethical practice in this domain
> "Is inherently manipulative and should be avoided in ethical …" is not what the item tests — Reframing places behavior or experience in a different, often more constructive context without denying the original frame.

**B. [ ]** Should only be used within cognitive-behavioral frameworks during a routine session
> "Should only be used within cognitive-behavioral frameworks d…" is not what the item tests — Reframing places behavior or experience in a different, often more constructive context without denying the original frame.

**C. [✔]** Offers an alternative perspective that opens new ways of relating to the experience
> Reframing places behavior or experience in a different, often more constructive context without denying the original frame. When well-timed and accurate, it expands the client's perspective and reduces shame and self-criticism.

**D. [ ]** Invalidates the client's experience by unilaterally changing its meaning in practice
> "Invalidates the client's experience by unilaterally changing…" is not what the item tests — Reframing places behavior or experience in a different, often more constructive context without denying the original frame.

**Rationale:** Reframing places behavior or experience in a different, often more constructive context without denying the original frame. When well-timed and accurate, it expands the client's perspective and reduces shame and self-criticism.

---

### 104. tpse104 · domain: skills · subdomain: Motivational · difficulty: medium

In Motivational Interviewing, "rolling with resistance" means:

**A. [ ]** Helping clients understand why their resistance is clinically dysfunctional
> "Helping clients understand why their resistance is clinicall…" is not what the item tests — Rolling with resistance: avoid arguing, don't confront resistance directly, don't label it as a problem to overcome.

**B. [ ]** Intentionally scheduling resistance discussions to contain them in practice
> "Intentionally scheduling resistance discussions to contain t…" is not what the item tests — Rolling with resistance: avoid arguing, don't confront resistance directly, don't label it as a problem to overcome.

**C. [ ]** Accepting all client resistance and abandoning change goals during counseling
> "Accepting all client resistance and abandoning change goals …" is not what the item tests — Rolling with resistance: avoid arguing, don't confront resistance directly, don't label it as a problem to overcome.

**D. [✔]** Not arguing or confronting ambivalence; instead reflecting and exploring it
> Rolling with resistance: avoid arguing, don't confront resistance directly, don't label it as a problem to overcome.

**Rationale:** Rolling with resistance: avoid arguing, don't confront resistance directly, don't label it as a problem to overcome. Instead, explore with curiosity, shift focus, or reflect ambivalence. Research confirms direct confrontation in MI reliably increases resistance.

---

### 105. tpse105 · domain: skills · subdomain: Motivational · difficulty: hard

The Stages of Change model identifies which client as being in the "contemplation" stage?

**A. [✔]** A client who acknowledges the problem but is genuinely ambivalent about changing
> Contemplation: client is aware of the problem and considering change but has not committed to action — classic ambivalence.

**B. [ ]** A client who has changed and is working to sustain their gains during counseling
> "A client who has changed and is working to sustain their gai…" is not what the item tests — Contemplation: client is aware of the problem and considering change but has not committed to action — classic ambivalence.

**C. [ ]** A client actively taking concrete steps to change their behavior during counseling
> "A client actively taking concrete steps to change their beha…" is not what the item tests — Contemplation: client is aware of the problem and considering change but has not committed to action — classic ambivalence.

**D. [ ]** A client who does not believe they have a problem as clinicians typically apply it
> "A client who does not believe they have a problem as clinici…" is not what the item tests — Contemplation: client is aware of the problem and considering change but has not committed to action — classic ambivalence.

**Rationale:** Contemplation: client is aware of the problem and considering change but has not committed to action — classic ambivalence. Precontemplation = no awareness or desire to change; preparation = planning; action = actively changing; maintenance = sustaining change.

---

> Biopsychosocial model (Engel, 1977): comprehensive assessment considers biological (genetics, neurobiology, medical status), psychological (cognition, emotion, behavior, personality), and social (relationships, culture…

**C. [ ]** Brain function, psychodynamics, and social learning history
> "Brain function, psychodynamics, and social learning history" is not what the item tests — Biopsychosocial model (Engel, 1977): comprehensive assessment considers biological (genetics, neurobiology, medical status), psychological (cognition

**D. [ ]** Background, present concerns, and social supports
> "Background, present concerns, and social supports" is not what the item tests — Biopsychosocial model (Engel, 1977): comprehensive assessment considers biological (genetics, neurobiology, medical status), psychological (cognition, emotion,

**Rationale:** Biopsychosocial model (Engel, 1977): comprehensive assessment considers biological (genetics, neurobiology, medical status), psychological (cognition, emotion, behavior, personality), and social (relationships, culture, socioeconomic environment) factors.

> "Social history, family of origin, and trauma history in prac…" is not what the item tests — MSE domains: appearance, behavior/motor activity, speech characteristics, mood (subjective) and affect (observed), thought process, thought content

> MSE domains: appearance, behavior/motor activity, speech characteristics, mood (subjective) and affect (observed), thought process, thought content, perceptual disturbances, cognitive functioning, insight, and judgment.

> "Personality traits, defense mechanisms, and coping styles to…" is not what the item tests — MSE domains: appearance, behavior/motor activity, speech characteristics, mood (subjective) and affect (observed), thought process, thought content

The Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS) is specifically designed to assess:

> "Depression severity in outpatient clinical populations now" is not what the item tests — C-SSRS (Posner et al.) standardizes suicidality assessment across: ideation (passive through active with plan and intent), ideation intensity, and beh

> "Risk factors for inpatient psychiatric hospitalization now" is not what the item tests — C-SSRS (Posner et al.) standardizes suicidality assessment across: ideation (passive through active with plan and intent), ideation intensity, and beh

**C. [ ]** Psychiatric symptom burden across multiple diagnostic domains
> "Psychiatric symptom burden across multiple diagnostic domain…" is not what the item tests — C-SSRS (Posner et al.) standardizes suicidality assessment across: ideation (passive through active with plan and intent), ideation intensity, and

> C-SSRS (Posner et al.) standardizes suicidality assessment across: ideation (passive through active with plan and intent), ideation intensity, and behavior (attempts, preparatory acts, interrupted/aborted attempts).

### 109. tpse109 · domain: skills · subdomain: Supervision · difficulty: medium

In clinical supervision, "parallel process" refers to:

**A. [✔]** The supervisee unconsciously replicating client dynamics within the supervisory relationship
> Parallel process: the supervisee mirrors with the supervisor what the client does with the supervisee — a client's helplessness becomes the supervisee's helplessness in supervision.

**B. [ ]** Simultaneously recording and reviewing supervision sessions for training at the intake stage
> "Simultaneously recording and reviewing supervision sessions …" is not what the item tests — Parallel process: the supervisee mirrors with the supervisor what the client does with the supervisee — a client's helplessness becomes the supervi

**C. [ ]** Two supervisors independently providing input on the same clinical case as usually described now
> "Two supervisors independently providing input on the same cl…" is not what the item tests — Parallel process: the supervisee mirrors with the supervisor what the client does with the supervisee — a client's helplessness becomes the supervi

**D. [ ]** Running individual therapy and group therapy concurrently with the same client by counselors
> "Running individual therapy and group therapy concurrently wi…" is not what the item tests — Parallel process: the supervisee mirrors with the supervisor what the client does with the supervisee — a client's helplessness becomes the supervi

**Rationale:** Parallel process: the supervisee mirrors with the supervisor what the client does with the supervisee — a client's helplessness becomes the supervisee's helplessness in supervision. When identified and named, it provides valuable data about the client-counselor dynamic.

**B. [✔]** Consultant
> Bernard's Discrimination Model: supervisor roles = teacher (instructive/didactic), counselor (focuses on supervisee's personal issues affecting clinical work), and consultant (collegial problem-solving).

**C. [ ]** Evaluator
> "Evaluator" is not what the item tests — Bernard's Discrimination Model: supervisor roles = teacher (instructive/didactic), counselor (focuses on supervisee's personal issues affecting clinical work), and consultant (collegial problem-solvi

**D. [ ]** Mentor
> "Mentor" is not what the item tests — Bernard's Discrimination Model: supervisor roles = teacher (instructive/didactic), counselor (focuses on supervisee's personal issues affecting clinical work), and consultant (collegial problem-solving)

**Rationale:** Bernard's Discrimination Model: supervisor roles = teacher (instructive/didactic), counselor (focuses on supervisee's personal issues affecting clinical work), and consultant (collegial problem-solving). The focus areas = intervention skills, conceptualization, and personalization.

### 111. tpse111 · domain: skills · subdomain: CulturalCompetence · difficulty: medium

Cultural humility differs from cultural competence primarily in that cultural humility:

**A. [ ]** Requires acquiring specific factual knowledge about cultural groups in the outpatient setting
> "Requires acquiring specific factual knowledge about cultural…" is not what the item tests — Cultural humility (Tervalon & Murray-García, 1998): an ongoing process of self-reflection, self-critique, and recognizing power imbalances.

**B. [ ]** Is concerned with institutional policy rather than individual clinical practice by counselors
> "Is concerned with institutional policy rather than individua…" is not what the item tests — Cultural humility (Tervalon & Murray-García, 1998): an ongoing process of self-reflection, self-critique, and recognizing power imbalances.

**C. [✔]** Is an ongoing, lifelong process of self-reflection rather than an achieved state of knowledge
> Cultural humility (Tervalon & Murray-García, 1998): an ongoing process of self-reflection, self-critique, and recognizing power imbalances.

**D. [ ]** Focuses on developing technical skills for working with specific populations during counseling
> "Focuses on developing technical skills for working with spec…" is not what the item tests — Cultural humility (Tervalon & Murray-García, 1998): an ongoing process of self-reflection, self-critique, and recognizing power imbalances.

**Rationale:** Cultural humility (Tervalon & Murray-García, 1998): an ongoing process of self-reflection, self-critique, and recognizing power imbalances. 'Competence' implies a state achieved; 'humility' implies continuous learning that acknowledges the limits of one's knowledge.

### 112. tpse112 · domain: skills · subdomain: CulturalCompetence · difficulty: hard

Microaggressions in the counseling context are clinically harmful because:

**A. [ ]** They are typically consciously intended to demean and invalidate clients during counseling
> "They are typically consciously intended to demean and invali…" is not what the item tests — Microaggressions (Sue et al.): brief, commonplace messages that communicate negative, demeaning slights to members of marginalized groups.

**B. [ ]** They are too minor in individual occurrence to meaningfully affect therapeutic alliance now
> "They are too minor in individual occurrence to meaningfully …" is not what the item tests — Microaggressions (Sue et al.): brief, commonplace messages that communicate negative, demeaning slights to members of marginalized groups.

**C. [ ]** They only significantly affect clients from visible racial minority groups during counseling
> "They only significantly affect clients from visible racial m…" is not what the item tests — Microaggressions (Sue et al.): brief, commonplace messages that communicate negative, demeaning slights to members of marginalized groups.

**D. [✔]** They cumulatively communicate invalidation of marginalized identities regardless of intent
> Microaggressions (Sue et al.): brief, commonplace messages that communicate negative, demeaning slights to members of marginalized groups.

**Rationale:** Microaggressions (Sue et al.): brief, commonplace messages that communicate negative, demeaning slights to members of marginalized groups. Their harm operates through cumulative effect, regardless of intent. Research shows they damage the therapeutic alliance and treatment outcomes.

> "Individual, group, and societal at the intake stage" is not what the item tests — ACA Advocacy Competencies (Lewis et al., 2003): Client/Student Level (empowerment and advocacy on behalf of), School/Community Level (community collaboration

**C. [ ]** Internal, external, and systemic during counseling
> "Internal, external, and systemic during counseling" is not what the item tests — ACA Advocacy Competencies (Lewis et al., 2003): Client/Student Level (empowerment and advocacy on behalf of), School/Community Level (community collaboration

> "Personal, professional, and political by counselors" is not what the item tests — ACA Advocacy Competencies (Lewis et al., 2003): Client/Student Level (empowerment and advocacy on behalf of), School/Community Level (community collaboration

SOAP note format stands for:

> "Symptoms, Observations, Assessment, Plan" is not what the item tests — SOAP: Subjective (what client reports — symptoms, statements), Objective (observable data — MSE, behavioral observations, affect), Assessment (clinical impression, prog

**B. [ ]** Summary, Outcomes, Analysis, Prognosis now
> "Summary, Outcomes, Analysis, Prognosis now" is not what the item tests — SOAP: Subjective (what client reports — symptoms, statements), Objective (observable data — MSE, behavioral observations, affect), Assessment (clinical impression, pr

**C. [✔]** Subjective, Objective, Assessment, Plan
> SOAP: Subjective (what client reports — symptoms, statements), Objective (observable data — MSE, behavioral observations, affect), Assessment (clinical impression, progress toward goals, risk level), Plan (interventions…

> "Session, Observations, Approach, Progress" is not what the item tests — SOAP: Subjective (what client reports — symptoms, statements), Objective (observable data — MSE, behavioral observations, affect), Assessment (clinical impression, pro

**Rationale:** SOAP: Subjective (what client reports — symptoms, statements), Objective (observable data — MSE, behavioral observations, affect), Assessment (clinical impression, progress toward goals, risk level), Plan (interventions, next steps, homework). ### 116. tpse116 · domain: skills · subdomain: Documentation · difficulty: hard

Clinical records should be written with the understanding that they:

**A. [ ]** Are private documents unlikely to ever be reviewed by outside parties during counseling
> "Are private documents unlikely to ever be reviewed by outsid…" is not what the item tests — Clinical records can be accessed by: clients (right to records), courts (subpoena), insurance companies (utilization review), licensing boards (com

**B. [ ]** Should omit negative client statements to avoid potential harm if records are accessed now
> "Should omit negative client statements to avoid potential ha…" is not what the item tests — Clinical records can be accessed by: clients (right to records), courts (subpoena), insurance companies (utilization review), licensing boards (com

**C. [ ]** Must capture every detail of every session to constitute adequate documentation as used
> "Must capture every detail of every session to constitute ade…" is not what the item tests — Clinical records can be accessed by: clients (right to records), courts (subpoena), insurance companies (utilization review), licensing boards (com

**D. [✔]** May be subpoenaed, accessed by clients, and reviewed by supervisors or licensing boards
> Clinical records can be accessed by: clients (right to records), courts (subpoena), insurance companies (utilization review), licensing boards (complaint investigations), and supervisors.

**Rationale:** Clinical records can be accessed by: clients (right to records), courts (subpoena), insurance companies (utilization review), licensing boards (complaint investigations), and supervisors. Write as if any appropriate third party might read it — clinically relevant and legally defensible.

### 117. tpse117 · domain: skills · subdomain: Referral · difficulty: medium

When referring a client to another provider, the counselor's ethical responsibility includes:

**A. [✔]** Providing a warm handoff, continuing until new provider is engaged, and documenting the referral
> Ethical referral: ensure client safety during transition (warm handoff where possible), continue providing at minimum crisis support until new services begin, document referral rationale and outcome.

**B. [ ]** Providing a referral list and requiring the client to manage their own transition in this domain
> "Providing a referral list and requiring the client to manage…" is not what the item tests — Ethical referral: ensure client safety during transition (warm handoff where possible), continue providing at minimum crisis support until new serv

**C. [ ]** Ending the current therapeutic relationship immediately upon making the referral during counseling
> "Ending the current therapeutic relationship immediately upon…" is not what the item tests — Ethical referral: ensure client safety during transition (warm handoff where possible), continue providing at minimum crisis support until new serv

**D. [ ]** Maintaining simultaneous roles with both providers until transition is fully complete in practice
> "Maintaining simultaneous roles with both providers until tra…" is not what the item tests — Ethical referral: ensure client safety during transition (warm handoff where possible), continue providing at minimum crisis support until new serv

**Rationale:** Ethical referral: ensure client safety during transition (warm handoff where possible), continue providing at minimum crisis support until new services begin, document referral rationale and outcome. Abrupt termination without transition support constitutes abandonment — an ethical violation.

> "Cognitive restructuring accomplished through present-focused…" is not what the item tests — Mindfulness (Kabat-Zinn): 'paying attention in a particular way: on purpose, in the present moment, and non-judgmentally.' It is not merely relaxat

**B. [✔]** Deliberate, non-judgmental attention to present-moment experience
> Mindfulness (Kabat-Zinn): 'paying attention in a particular way: on purpose, in the present moment, and non-judgmentally.' It is not merely relaxation (though relaxation may result) and is distinguished from cognitive re…

**C. [ ]** A relaxation technique primarily used for anxiety reduction today
> "A relaxation technique primarily used for anxiety reduction …" is not what the item tests — Mindfulness (Kabat-Zinn): 'paying attention in a particular way: on purpose, in the present moment, and non-judgmentally.' It is not merely relaxat

**D. [ ]** A Buddhist spiritual practice adapted for secular therapy contexts
> "A Buddhist spiritual practice adapted for secular therapy co…" is not what the item tests — Mindfulness (Kabat-Zinn): 'paying attention in a particular way: on purpose, in the present moment, and non-judgmentally.' It is not merely relaxat

### 119. tpse119 · domain: skills · subdomain: Telehealth · difficulty: medium

When providing telehealth counseling, which consideration is UNIQUE to this delivery format?

**A. [ ]** Documenting session content and clinical impressions at the intake stage
> "Documenting session content and clinical impressions at the …" is not what the item tests — Telehealth requires all standard ethical practices PLUS: verifying client's physical location for emergencies (cannot physically intervene), confir

**B. [ ]** Maintaining confidentiality of clinical communications during counseling
> "Maintaining confidentiality of clinical communications durin…" is not what the item tests — Telehealth requires all standard ethical practices PLUS: verifying client's physical location for emergencies (cannot physically intervene), confir

**C. [✔]** Having a verified emergency protocol for the client's physical location
> Telehealth requires all standard ethical practices PLUS: verifying client's physical location for emergencies (cannot physically intervene), confirming who may be present nearby, ensuring technology security (HIPAA-compl…

**D. [ ]** Obtaining informed consent before beginning services at the intake stage
> "Obtaining informed consent before beginning services at the …" is not what the item tests — Telehealth requires all standard ethical practices PLUS: verifying client's physical location for emergencies (cannot physically intervene), confir

**Rationale:** Telehealth requires all standard ethical practices PLUS: verifying client's physical location for emergencies (cannot physically intervene), confirming who may be present nearby, ensuring technology security (HIPAA-compliant platform), and cross-jurisdictional licensing compliance. Emergency planning for remote location is uniquely critical.

### 120. tpse120 · domain: skills · subdomain: EvidenceBased · difficulty: medium

Evidence-based practice (EBP) in counseling integrates:

**A. [ ]** Manualized protocols followed without deviation to ensure fidelity itself
> "Manualized protocols followed without deviation to ensure fi…" is not what the item tests — EBP (APA, 2006) = integration of three elements: best available research evidence + clinical expertise (informed judgment based on experience) + cl

**B. [ ]** Research evidence only, applied uniformly regardless of client context now
> "Research evidence only, applied uniformly regardless of clie…" is not what the item tests — EBP (APA, 2006) = integration of three elements: best available research evidence + clinical expertise (informed judgment based on experience) + cl

**C. [ ]** Empirically supported treatments applied through standardized protocols now
> "Empirically supported treatments applied through standardize…" is not what the item tests — EBP (APA, 2006) = integration of three elements: best available research evidence + clinical expertise (informed judgment based on experience) + cl

**D. [✔]** Best research evidence, clinical expertise, and client preferences/values
> EBP (APA, 2006) = integration of three elements: best available research evidence + clinical expertise (informed judgment based on experience) + client values, preferences, and cultural context.

**Rationale:** EBP (APA, 2006) = integration of three elements: best available research evidence + clinical expertise (informed judgment based on experience) + client values, preferences, and cultural context. All three are required — research without clinical judgment or client fit is not EBP.

### 121. tpse121 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Confidentiality · difficulty: easy

Confidentiality in counseling is best defined as:

**A. [✔]** The ethical and legal protection of client-shared information with clearly specified limits
> Confidentiality is an ethical duty with legal backing, but not absolute.

**B. [ ]** A promise to keep all therapy records permanently sealed as the reference material frames it
> "A promise to keep all therapy records permanently sealed as …" is not what the item tests — Confidentiality is an ethical duty with legal backing, but not absolute.

**C. [ ]** A legal privilege that prevents any disclosure in court as the reference material frames it
> "A legal privilege that prevents any disclosure in court as t…" is not what the item tests — Confidentiality is an ethical duty with legal backing, but not absolute.

**D. [ ]** An absolute promise rarely to share any client information under any circumstances at intake
> "An absolute promise rarely to share any client information u…" is not what the item tests — Confidentiality is an ethical duty with legal backing, but not absolute.

**Rationale:** Confidentiality is an ethical duty with legal backing, but not absolute. Standard limits: danger to self/others, mandatory reporting obligations, court orders, insurance utilization review, supervision, and consultation. 'Privilege' is the client's separate legal right in court contexts.

### 122. tpse122 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Confidentiality · difficulty: medium

A counselor receives a call from a client's employer asking about the client's attendance and treatment progress. The MOST ethical response is:

**A. [ ]** Confirm the client is in treatment but share no clinical details by counselors
> "Confirm the client is in treatment but share no clinical det…" is not what the item tests — Without a signed release, counselors should not confirm or deny whether someone is their client (HIPAA minimum necessary standard).

**B. [✔]** Decline to confirm or deny the client is in treatment without a signed release
> Without a signed release, counselors should not confirm or deny whether someone is their client (HIPAA minimum necessary standard).

**C. [ ]** Refer the employer directly to the client for information as usually described
> "Refer the employer directly to the client for information as…" is not what the item tests — Without a signed release, counselors should not confirm or deny whether someone is their client (HIPAA minimum necessary standard).

**D. [ ]** Provide general information since employers have a legitimate occupational interest
> "Provide general information since employers have a legitimat…" is not what the item tests — Without a signed release, counselors should not confirm or deny whether someone is their client (HIPAA minimum necessary standard).

**Rationale:** Without a signed release, counselors should not confirm or deny whether someone is their client (HIPAA minimum necessary standard). Confirming treatment status alone constitutes a confidentiality breach. The caller should be directed to obtain consent from the client.

### 123. tpse123 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Confidentiality · difficulty: medium

Confidentiality in group counseling is ethically unique because:

**A. [ ]** Information shared in groups is rarely legally protected as confidential during counseling
> "Information shared in groups is rarely legally protected as …" is not what the item tests — In group counseling, the leader can commit to their own confidentiality but cannot compel or legally guarantee group members will maintain it.

**B. [ ]** Confidentiality is optional in group settings under ACA standards during a routine session
> "Confidentiality is optional in group settings under ACA stan…" is not what the item tests — In group counseling, the leader can commit to their own confidentiality but cannot compel or legally guarantee group members will maintain it.

**C. [✔]** The group leader cannot guarantee that other group members will maintain confidentiality
> In group counseling, the leader can commit to their own confidentiality but cannot compel or legally guarantee group members will maintain it.

**D. [ ]** HIPAA protections extend equally to all group member disclosures during a routine session
> "HIPAA protections extend equally to all group member disclos…" is not what the item tests — In group counseling, the leader can commit to their own confidentiality but cannot compel or legally guarantee group members will maintain it.

**Rationale:** In group counseling, the leader can commit to their own confidentiality but cannot compel or legally guarantee group members will maintain it. This limitation must be clearly disclosed in informed consent. A member breach is an ethical issue to address in group but cannot be prevented by legal authority.

### 124. tpse124 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Confidentiality · difficulty: hard

A deceased client's adult child requests access to their parent's therapy records. The counselor should FIRST:

**A. [ ]** Release records since the client is deceased and confidentiality obligations have ended during counseling
> "Release records since the client is deceased and confidentia…" is not what the item tests — Confidentiality survives death in most jurisdictions, but laws vary considerably.

**B. [ ]** Release records only if the requesting child was also a client today as the reference material frames it
> "Release records only if the requesting child was also a clie…" is not what the item tests — Confidentiality survives death in most jurisdictions, but laws vary considerably.

**C. [ ]** Refuse all requests generally since the client can no longer provide consent at the level the item tests
> "Refuse all requests generally since the client can no longer…" is not what the item tests — Confidentiality survives death in most jurisdictions, but laws vary considerably.

**D. [✔]** Consult state law, as confidentiality obligations to deceased clients vary significantly by jurisdiction
> Confidentiality survives death in most jurisdictions, but laws vary considerably.

**Rationale:** Confidentiality survives death in most jurisdictions, but laws vary considerably. Some states allow executor/estate administrator access; others protect records indefinitely. Consult state statute and legal counsel. Clinical judgment about potential harm to the child from the content is also ethically relevant.

### 125. tpse125 · domain: ethics · subdomain: InformedConsent · difficulty: easy

Informed consent for counseling must include all of the following EXCEPT:

**A. [✔]** The counselor's personal values and religious beliefs
> Required informed consent elements: nature of counseling, limits of confidentiality, risks/benefits of treatment, fees/billing, crisis procedures, and client rights.

**B. [ ]** Fees, billing procedures, and payment policies itself
> "Fees, billing procedures, and payment policies itself" is not what the item tests — Required informed consent elements: nature of counseling, limits of confidentiality, risks/benefits of treatment, fees/billing, crisis procedures, and clie

**C. [ ]** The nature and specific limits of confidentiality now
> "The nature and specific limits of confidentiality now" is not what the item tests — Required informed consent elements: nature of counseling, limits of confidentiality, risks/benefits of treatment, fees/billing, crisis procedures, and clie

**D. [ ]** The client's rights and responsibilities in therapy
> "The client's rights and responsibilities in the therapeutic …" is not what the item tests — Required informed consent elements: nature of counseling, limits of confidentiality, risks/benefits of treatment, fees/billing, crisis procedures,

**Rationale:** Required informed consent elements: nature of counseling, limits of confidentiality, risks/benefits of treatment, fees/billing, crisis procedures, and client rights. Personal values are not required disclosure elements — they would be disclosed only when relevant to service limitations (e.g., value-based referral).

---

### 126. tpse126 · domain: ethics · subdomain: InformedConsent · difficulty: medium

When counseling minors, informed consent is MOST ethically addressed by:

**A. [ ]** Obtaining consent only from the minor if services are sought voluntarily at intake
> "Obtaining consent only from the minor if services are sought…" is not what the item tests — Parents/guardians provide legal consent; minors provide assent (agreement to participate in services).

**B. [✔]** Obtaining parental consent and the minor's assent when developmentally appropriate
> Parents/guardians provide legal consent; minors provide assent (agreement to participate in services).

**C. [ ]** Consent is not ethically required for minors since parents have full legal authority
> "Consent is not ethically required for minors since parents h…" is not what the item tests — Parents/guardians provide legal consent; minors provide assent (agreement to participate in services).

**D. [ ]** Obtaining consent only from the parent or legal guardian in the outpatient setting
> "Obtaining consent only from the parent or legal guardian in …" is not what the item tests — Parents/guardians provide legal consent; minors provide assent (agreement to participate in services).

**Rationale:** Parents/guardians provide legal consent; minors provide assent (agreement to participate in services). Assent is ethically important even when not legally required — particularly for older adolescents. Some states allow minors to independently consent to specific services (mental health, substance use, reproductive health).

---

### 127. tpse127 · domain: ethics · subdomain: InformedConsent · difficulty: hard

A client with schizophrenia agreed to treatment when stable but is now refusing medication during a psychotic episode. The MOST accurate ethical framing is:

**A. [ ]** Beneficence requires treating against the expressed will of any client with psychosis in practice
> "Beneficence requires treating against the expressed will of …" is not what the item tests — Capacity is decision-specific and moment-specific — it must be re-assessed.

**B. [ ]** Autonomy typically overrides beneficence in competent adults now as clinicians typically apply it now
> "Autonomy typically overrides beneficence in competent adults…" is not what the item tests — Capacity is decision-specific and moment-specific — it must be re-assessed.

**C. [✔]** Decision-making capacity must be assessed; the refusal may be involuntary if capacity is impaired
> Capacity is decision-specific and moment-specific — it must be re-assessed.

**D. [ ]** Prior consent remains permanently binding regardless of current mental state as usually described
> "Prior consent remains permanently binding regardless of curr…" is not what the item tests — Capacity is decision-specific and moment-specific — it must be re-assessed.

**Rationale:** Capacity is decision-specific and moment-specific — it must be re-assessed. When psychotic, capacity to meaningfully consent or refuse may be impaired. Psychiatric Advance Directives (PADs) address this by allowing competent individuals to make instructions for times when they are not competent.

---

### 128. tpse128 · domain: ethics · subdomain: DualRelationships · difficulty: easy

The ACA Code prohibits sexual relationships with current clients primarily because:

**A. [ ]** Society and professional bodies regard such relationships as broadly immoral at the intake stage
> "Society and professional bodies regard such relationships as…" is not what the item tests — The therapeutic relationship carries an inherent power differential that compromises the client's ability to give fully free and genuine consent.

**B. [ ]** They create scheduling and documentation complications that compromise care as usually described now
> "They create scheduling and documentation complications that …" is not what the item tests — The therapeutic relationship carries an inherent power differential that compromises the client's ability to give fully free and genuine consent.

**C. [ ]** Such relationships are typically unwanted by clients who are experiencing them during counseling
> "Such relationships are typically unwanted by clients who are…" is not what the item tests — The therapeutic relationship carries an inherent power differential that compromises the client's ability to give fully free and genuine consent.

**D. [✔]** The inherent power differential in the therapeutic relationship makes genuine consent impossible
> The therapeutic relationship carries an inherent power differential that compromises the client's ability to give fully free and genuine consent.

**Rationale:** The therapeutic relationship carries an inherent power differential that compromises the client's ability to give fully free and genuine consent. The prohibition is fundamentally about protecting clients from exploitation — regardless of whether the client appears to want the relationship.

---

### 129. tpse129 · domain: ethics · subdomain: DualRelationships · difficulty: medium

The ACA Code of Ethics addresses sexual relationships with FORMER clients by:

**A. [✔]** Requiring a minimum of 5 years and documentation that no exploitation occurred
> ACA Code A.5.b: counselors do not engage in sexual/romantic relationships with former clients, their romantic partners, or family members for 5 years following last professional contact — and even then only in the most u…

**B. [ ]** Permanently prohibiting them under all circumstances in the outpatient setting
> "Permanently prohibiting them under all circumstances in the …" is not what the item tests — ACA Code A.5.b: counselors do not engage in sexual/romantic relationships with former clients, their romantic partners, or family members for 5 yea

**C. [ ]** Permitting them after 6 months following the last professional contact as used
> "Permitting them after 6 months following the last profession…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code A.5.b: counselors do not engage in sexual/romantic relationships with former clients, their romantic partners, or family members for 5 yea

**D. [ ]** Permitting them after 2 years with written supervisor approval during counseling
> "Permitting them after 2 years with written supervisor approv…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code A.5.b: counselors do not engage in sexual/romantic relationships with former clients, their romantic partners, or family members for 5 yea

**Rationale:** ACA Code A.5.b: counselors do not engage in sexual/romantic relationships with former clients, their romantic partners, or family members for 5 years following last professional contact — and even then only in the most unusual circumstances. The counselor bears the burden of demonstrating no exploitation.

### 130. tpse130 · domain: ethics · subdomain: DualRelationships · difficulty: hard

A school counselor is asked to provide individual therapy for a student they already see for career guidance. This represents:

**A. [ ]** An absolute ethical violation that must be generally refused as clinicians typically apply it
> "An absolute ethical violation that must be generally refused…" is not what the item tests — Dual roles are not automatically prohibited but require assessment of potential harm.

**B. [✔]** A potentially problematic dual role requiring careful ethical assessment before proceeding
> Dual roles are not automatically prohibited but require assessment of potential harm.

**C. [ ]** A straightforward extension of existing services requiring no special consideration itself
> "A straightforward extension of existing services requiring n…" is not what the item tests — Dual roles are not automatically prohibited but require assessment of potential harm.

**D. [ ]** An appropriate use of established rapport for efficient and integrated service delivery now
> "An appropriate use of established rapport for efficient and …" is not what the item tests — Dual roles are not automatically prohibited but require assessment of potential harm.

**Rationale:** Dual roles are not automatically prohibited but require assessment of potential harm. This situation carries real risks: role confusion, compromised objectivity, and impact on the career guidance relationship. The counselor should assess harm potential and consider referral for the individual therapy component.

### 131. tpse131 · domain: ethics · subdomain: MandatoryReporting · difficulty: easy

Mandatory reporting laws require counselors to report child abuse based on:

**A. [ ]** Only direct disclosures made by the child in session during counseling
> "Only direct disclosures made by the child in session during …" is not what the item tests — Mandatory reporters are not investigators — they report reasonable suspicion.

**B. [ ]** Only confirmed incidents, not mere suspicion during a routine session
> "Only confirmed incidents, not mere suspicion during a routin…" is not what the item tests — Mandatory reporters are not investigators — they report reasonable suspicion.

**C. [✔]** Reasonable suspicion of abuse or neglect — certainty is not required
> Mandatory reporters are not investigators — they report reasonable suspicion.

**D. [ ]** Abuse only if the counselor personally witnessed it occurring itself
> "Abuse only if the counselor personally witnessed it occurrin…" is not what the item tests — Mandatory reporters are not investigators — they report reasonable suspicion.

**Rationale:** Mandatory reporters are not investigators — they report reasonable suspicion. The investigation is the responsibility of Child Protective Services. Waiting for certainty before reporting constitutes a failure to report and creates legal and ethical liability.

---

### 132. tpse132 · domain: ethics · subdomain: MandatoryReporting · difficulty: medium

When a client discloses abuse they experienced as a child (now an adult), with no current minor at risk, the counselor is:

**A. [ ]** Required to report the historical perpetrator to law enforcement during a routine session
> "Required to report the historical perpetrator to law enforce…" is not what the item tests — Mandatory reporting is triggered by present or ongoing risk to a current child.

**B. [ ]** Required to notify the survivor's current family members as clinicians typically apply it
> "Required to notify the survivor's current family members as …" is not what the item tests — Mandatory reporting is triggered by present or ongoing risk to a current child.

**C. [ ]** Required to report to CPS regardless of whether any child is currently at risk in practice
> "Required to report to CPS regardless of whether any child is…" is not what the item tests — Mandatory reporting is triggered by present or ongoing risk to a current child.

**D. [✔]** Generally not required to report absent current risk to a child; should consult state law
> Mandatory reporting is triggered by present or ongoing risk to a current child.

**Rationale:** Mandatory reporting is triggered by present or ongoing risk to a current child. A client disclosing their own past victimization generally does not trigger reporting unless there is reason to believe the historical perpetrator currently has access to children. Consult state law for jurisdiction-specific guidance.

---

### 133. tpse133 · domain: ethics · subdomain: MandatoryReporting · difficulty: hard

A client works in elder care and the counselor suspects they may be financially exploiting elderly patients. The reporting obligation depends on:

**A. [✔]** State law — unlike child abuse reporting, elder abuse mandatory reporting statutes vary significantly
> Elder abuse mandatory reporting statutes vary significantly by state — not all states designate counselors as mandatory reporters for elder abuse (unlike child abuse, where all states do).

**B. [ ]** HIPAA regulations governing disclosure of PHI at the intake stage as the reference material frames it
> "HIPAA regulations governing disclosure of PHI at the intake …" is not what the item tests — Elder abuse mandatory reporting statutes vary significantly by state — not all states designate counselors as mandatory reporters for elder abuse (

**C. [ ]** Whether the exploitation involves physical harm in addition to financial as clinicians typically apply it
> "Whether the exploitation involves physical harm in addition …" is not what the item tests — Elder abuse mandatory reporting statutes vary significantly by state — not all states designate counselors as mandatory reporters for elder abuse (

**D. [ ]** Whether the client explicitly admits to the exploitation in session as the reference material frames it
> "Whether the client explicitly admits to the exploitation in …" is not what the item tests — Elder abuse mandatory reporting statutes vary significantly by state — not all states designate counselors as mandatory reporters for elder abuse (

**Rationale:** Elder abuse mandatory reporting statutes vary significantly by state — not all states designate counselors as mandatory reporters for elder abuse (unlike child abuse, where all states do). Check state-specific law. ACA ethics also call for protecting vulnerable individuals, informing ethical considerations even when a specific mandate is absent.
