### 134. tpse134 · domain: ethics · subdomain: DutyToWarn · difficulty: medium

The Tarasoff case (1974/1976) established the duty to:

**A. [ ]** Hospitalize any client who makes any threat of violence as clinicians typically apply it
> "Hospitalize any client who makes any threat of violence as c…" is not what the item tests — Tarasoff established the 'duty to protect' — therapists must take reasonable steps to protect identifiable third parties from serious, credible thr

**B. [✔]** Take reasonable steps to protect identifiable third parties from credible client threats
> Tarasoff established the 'duty to protect' — therapists must take reasonable steps to protect identifiable third parties from serious, credible threats.

**C. [ ]** Ensure clients with violent histories are prohibited from owning firearms in this domain
> "Ensure clients with violent histories are prohibited from ow…" is not what the item tests — Tarasoff established the 'duty to protect' — therapists must take reasonable steps to protect identifiable third parties from serious, credible thr

**D. [ ]** Report all violent thoughts to law enforcement immediately as clinicians typically apply it
> "Report all violent thoughts to law enforcement immediately a…" is not what the item tests — Tarasoff established the 'duty to protect' — therapists must take reasonable steps to protect identifiable third parties from serious, credible thr

**Rationale:** Tarasoff established the 'duty to protect' — therapists must take reasonable steps to protect identifiable third parties from serious, credible threats. Methods vary by state: warning the victim directly, notifying law enforcement, hospitalizing the client, or a combination. 'Duty to warn' vs. 'duty to protect' varies by jurisdiction.

---

### 135. tpse135 · domain: ethics · subdomain: DutyToWarn · difficulty: hard

The Tarasoff duty is clinically triggered when:

**A. [ ]** The client is angry and has a documented history of prior violence by counselors
> "The client is angry and has a documented history of prior vi…" is not what the item tests — Tarasoff duty requires: (1) a specific, serious, credible threat (2) against an identifiable potential victim.

**B. [ ]** Any homicidal ideation is reported to the counselor by the client in this domain
> "Any homicidal ideation is reported to the counselor by the c…" is not what the item tests — Tarasoff duty requires: (1) a specific, serious, credible threat (2) against an identifiable potential victim.

**C. [✔]** There is a serious, credible threat directed at an identifiable potential victim
> Tarasoff duty requires: (1) a specific, serious, credible threat (2) against an identifiable potential victim.

**D. [ ]** The client owns or has access to a firearm now as the reference material frames it
> "The client owns or has access to a firearm now as the refere…" is not what the item tests — Tarasoff duty requires: (1) a specific, serious, credible threat (2) against an identifiable potential victim.

**Rationale:** Tarasoff duty requires: (1) a specific, serious, credible threat (2) against an identifiable potential victim. Vague homicidal ideation without a specific target does not trigger the duty. Anger or history alone is insufficient — the threat must be concrete and the victim reasonably identifiable.

### 136. tpse136 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Competence · difficulty: medium

A counselor is asked to use EMDR with a trauma survivor but has received no EMDR training. The ethical course of action is:

**A. [ ]** Research EMDR online and in textbooks before beginning as clinicians typically apply it
> "Research EMDR online and in textbooks before beginning as cl…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.2.a: counselors practice only within the boundaries of their competence.

**B. [ ]** Proceed if the client provides informed consent to the learning process in practice
> "Proceed if the client provides informed consent to the learn…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.2.a: counselors practice only within the boundaries of their competence.

**C. [ ]** Proceed with EMDR if adequate supervisory oversight is available at the intake stage
> "Proceed with EMDR if adequate supervisory oversight is avail…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.2.a: counselors practice only within the boundaries of their competence.

**D. [✔]** Decline EMDR specifically; treat trauma within current competence or refer for EMDR
> ACA Code C.2.a: counselors practice only within the boundaries of their competence.

**Rationale:** ACA Code C.2.a: counselors practice only within the boundaries of their competence. EMDR requires specific supervised training. The ethical response: serve the client using modalities within competence, refer specifically for EMDR, and pursue appropriate training to expand competence over time.

### 137. tpse137 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Competence · difficulty: hard

A counselor with deeply held religious beliefs that homosexuality is sinful is asked to counsel LGBTQ+ clients on relationship satisfaction. They should:

**A. [✔]** Refer to a counselor who can provide genuinely affirming, competent services without value imposition
> ACA Code A.11.b: counselors may not refer based solely on personal values conflicts.

**B. [ ]** Provide services while maintaining strict value neutrality in session as clinicians typically apply it
> "Provide services while maintaining strict value neutrality i…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code A.11.b: counselors may not refer based solely on personal values conflicts.

**C. [ ]** Provide services while sharing their value difference in the spirit of therapeutic transparency today
> "Provide services while sharing their value difference in the…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code A.11.b: counselors may not refer based solely on personal values conflicts.

**D. [ ]** Attempt to facilitate change in the client's sexual orientation as a therapeutic approach in practice
> "Attempt to facilitate change in the client's sexual orientat…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code A.11.b: counselors may not refer based solely on personal values conflicts.

**Rationale:** ACA Code A.11.b: counselors may not refer based solely on personal values conflicts. However, if the counselor cannot provide genuinely LGBTQ+-affirming care and the client's goal is relationship satisfaction (not orientation change), referral to an affirming provider is indicated. Conversion/change-effort practices are prohibited by ACA and CACREP standards.

### 138. tpse138 · domain: ethics · subdomain: EthicalDecisionMaking · difficulty: medium

The first step in most ethical decision-making models is to:

**A. [ ]** Generate all possible courses of action available by counselors
> "Generate all possible courses of action available by counsel…" is not what the item tests — Most ethical decision-making models (Forester-Miller & Davis, Corey et al.) begin with recognizing that a potential ethical issue exists.

**B. [✔]** Identify and recognize that an ethical issue or dilemma exists
> Most ethical decision-making models (Forester-Miller & Davis, Corey et al.) begin with recognizing that a potential ethical issue exists.

**C. [ ]** Consult immediately with a supervisor or trusted colleague now
> "Consult immediately with a supervisor or trusted colleague n…" is not what the item tests — Most ethical decision-making models (Forester-Miller & Davis, Corey et al.) begin with recognizing that a potential ethical issue exists.

**D. [ ]** Review the relevant sections of the ACA Code of Ethics as used
> "Review the relevant sections of the ACA Code of Ethics as us…" is not what the item tests — Most ethical decision-making models (Forester-Miller & Davis, Corey et al.) begin with recognizing that a potential ethical issue exists.

**Rationale:** Most ethical decision-making models (Forester-Miller & Davis, Corey et al.) begin with recognizing that a potential ethical issue exists. One cannot navigate a dilemma one hasn't first identified. Subsequent steps: identify relevant codes, generate options, consider consequences, act, and evaluate the outcome.

### 139. tpse139 · domain: ethics · subdomain: EthicalDecisionMaking · difficulty: hard

When legal requirements and ethical principles are in direct conflict, counselors should FIRST:

**A. [ ]** Typically follow ethical principles regardless of legal consequences as usually described
> "Typically follow ethical principles regardless of legal cons…" is not what the item tests — When ethics and law conflict (ACA B.1.d, H.1.b), counselors should: inform clients of the situation, consult with supervisors and legal counsel, se

**B. [ ]** Follow client preferences when ethics and law conflict as the reference material frames it
> "Follow client preferences when ethics and law conflict as th…" is not what the item tests — When ethics and law conflict (ACA B.1.d, H.1.b), counselors should: inform clients of the situation, consult with supervisors and legal counsel, se

**C. [✔]** Consult legal and ethics resources to find the most ethical path within legal constraints
> When ethics and law conflict (ACA B.1.d, H.1.b), counselors should: inform clients of the situation, consult with supervisors and legal counsel, seek to act as ethically as possible within legal requirements, and document the process.

**D. [ ]** Typically follow legal requirements as they take absolute precedence as usually described
> "Typically follow legal requirements as they take absolute pr…" is not what the item tests — When ethics and law conflict (ACA B.1.d, H.1.b), counselors should: inform clients of the situation, consult with supervisors and legal counsel, se

**Rationale:** When ethics and law conflict (ACA B.1.d, H.1.b), counselors should: inform clients of the situation, consult with supervisors and legal counsel, seek to act as ethically as possible within legal requirements, and document the process. Neither law nor ethics always unambiguously trumps the other — navigating the tension requires careful deliberation.

### 140. tpse140 · domain: ethics · subdomain: PrinciplesOfEthics · difficulty: medium

Kitchener's five moral principles of counseling ethics are autonomy, nonmaleficence, beneficence, justice, and:

**A. [ ]** Competence
> "Competence" is not what the item tests — Kitchener (1984) identified five moral principles: autonomy (self-determination), nonmaleficence (do no harm), beneficence (promote wellbeing), justice (fairness/equal treatment), and fidelity (hono

**B. [ ]** Veracity
> "Veracity" is not what the item tests — Kitchener (1984) identified five moral principles: autonomy (self-determination), nonmaleficence (do no harm), beneficence (promote wellbeing), justice (fairness/equal treatment), and fidelity (honori

**C. [ ]** Transparency
> "Transparency" is not what the item tests — Kitchener (1984) identified five moral principles: autonomy (self-determination), nonmaleficence (do no harm), beneficence (promote wellbeing), justice (fairness/equal treatment), and fidelity (ho

**D. [✔]** Fidelity
> Kitchener (1984) identified five moral principles: autonomy (self-determination), nonmaleficence (do no harm), beneficence (promote wellbeing), justice (fairness/equal treatment), and fidelity (honoring commitments, bein…

**Rationale:** Kitchener (1984) identified five moral principles: autonomy (self-determination), nonmaleficence (do no harm), beneficence (promote wellbeing), justice (fairness/equal treatment), and fidelity (honoring commitments, being trustworthy/loyal). Veracity is sometimes added as a sixth principle.

### 141. tpse141 · domain: ethics · subdomain: PrinciplesOfEthics · difficulty: hard

A trauma survivor tells their counselor, "I've decided to stop all my medications without telling my psychiatrist." Applying the principle of autonomy requires:

**A. [✔]** Respecting the right to decide while ensuring the client has full, accurate information about risks
> Autonomy requires informed decision-making, not just the absence of coercion.

**B. [ ]** Respecting the decision entirely without comment or further exploration at the level the item tests
> "Respecting the decision entirely without comment or further …" is not what the item tests — Autonomy requires informed decision-making, not just the absence of coercion.

**C. [ ]** Notifying the prescribing psychiatrist directly without the client's consent during a routine session
> "Notifying the prescribing psychiatrist directly without the …" is not what the item tests — Autonomy requires informed decision-making, not just the absence of coercion.

**D. [ ]** Terminating services for medically non-compliant behavior today as the reference material frames it
> "Terminating services for medically non-compliant behavior to…" is not what the item tests — Autonomy requires informed decision-making, not just the absence of coercion.

**Rationale:** Autonomy requires informed decision-making, not just the absence of coercion. The ethical response: ensure the client has complete, accurate information about medication cessation risks (beneficence), explore the decision therapeutically, and ultimately respect their choice as a competent adult — while documenting the clinical discussion.

### 142. tpse142 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Technology · difficulty: medium

Using a personal social media account to describe a "frustrating client session" without naming the client is:

**A. [ ]** Only problematic if current professional colleagues see the post as clinicians typically apply it
> "Only problematic if current professional colleagues see the …" is not what the item tests — ACA Code H.6.a–b: counselors maintain confidentiality on all social media platforms.

**B. [✔]** An ethical concern even without names — clients may be identifiable and the act is unprofessional
> ACA Code H.6.a–b: counselors maintain confidentiality on all social media platforms.

**C. [ ]** Acceptable as a legitimate self-care and venting tool for clinicians as clinicians typically apply it
> "Acceptable as a legitimate self-care and venting tool for cl…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code H.6.a–b: counselors maintain confidentiality on all social media platforms.

**D. [ ]** Acceptable since no personally identifying information is shared as clinicians typically apply it
> "Acceptable since no personally identifying information is sh…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code H.6.a–b: counselors maintain confidentiality on all social media platforms.

**Rationale:** ACA Code H.6.a–b: counselors maintain confidentiality on all social media platforms. Even without explicit identifying details, the act undermines professional standards, may inadvertently enable identification through context clues, and constitutes a violation of the dignity of the therapeutic relationship.

---

### 143. tpse143 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Technology · difficulty: hard

A counselor discovers a client has posted detailed descriptions of their therapy sessions on a public social media account. The counselor should FIRST:

**A. [ ]** Take no action — clients have the right to share their own therapeutic experiences during counseling now
> "Take no action — clients have the right to share their own t…" is not what the item tests — Clients own their own therapy experiences and may share them.

**B. [ ]** Terminate services for privacy violations that compromise the therapeutic relationship by counselors
> "Terminate services for privacy violations that compromise th…" is not what the item tests — Clients own their own therapy experiences and may share them.

**C. [✔]** Discuss the posts with the client in session to understand the meaning and any clinical implications
> Clients own their own therapy experiences and may share them.

**D. [ ]** Request that the client remove the posts immediately as a condition of continued therapy in practice
> "Request that the client remove the posts immediately as a co…" is not what the item tests — Clients own their own therapy experiences and may share them.

**Rationale:** Clients own their own therapy experiences and may share them. If the counselor discovered the posts through active social media searching, that raises separate ethical concerns. If discovered incidentally, bringing it to session therapeutically — what does posting mean for this client? any safety concerns? — is appropriate. This is clinical material.

### 144. tpse144 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Supervision · difficulty: medium

An intern discovers their supervisor is billing insurance for services the intern provided as if the supervisor rendered them directly. The intern should:

**A. [ ]** Report immediately and exclusively to the state licensing board at the level the item tests
> "Report immediately and exclusively to the state licensing bo…" is not what the item tests — This constitutes insurance fraud — an ethical violation (ACA C.6.b) and potentially criminal act.

**B. [ ]** Confront the supervisor directly and privately before taking any other steps by counselors
> "Confront the supervisor directly and privately before taking…" is not what the item tests — This constitutes insurance fraud — an ethical violation (ACA C.6.b) and potentially criminal act.

**C. [ ]** Do nothing — billing is entirely the supervisor's professional responsibility by counselors
> "Do nothing — billing is entirely the supervisor's profession…" is not what the item tests — This constitutes insurance fraud — an ethical violation (ACA C.6.b) and potentially criminal act.

**D. [✔]** Follow the agency's complaint procedure and consult with another supervisor or ethics body
> This constitutes insurance fraud — an ethical violation (ACA C.6.b) and potentially criminal act.

**Rationale:** This constitutes insurance fraud — an ethical violation (ACA C.6.b) and potentially criminal act. The intern should document the situation, consult with a trusted supervisor or ethics resource, and use agency reporting procedures. Licensing board reporting may follow, but internal escalation is typically the recommended first step.

### 145. tpse145 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Supervision · difficulty: hard

A supervisor discovers their supervisee has been providing counseling services outside the scope of their supervision agreement. The supervisor's primary obligation is to:

**A. [✔]** Client welfare first — then address the supervisee's ethical violation directly
> Supervisors' primary obligation is to client welfare — supervision exists fundamentally to protect clients (ACA F.1.a).

**B. [ ]** The supervisee first — to support their professional learning and development now
> "The supervisee first — to support their professional learnin…" is not what the item tests — Supervisors' primary obligation is to client welfare — supervision exists fundamentally to protect clients (ACA F.1.a).

**C. [ ]** The supervisee and all affected clients equally and simultaneously by counselors
> "The supervisee and all affected clients equally and simultan…" is not what the item tests — Supervisors' primary obligation is to client welfare — supervision exists fundamentally to protect clients (ACA F.1.a).

**D. [ ]** The agency first — to document the situation and protect the organization from liability
> "The agency first — to document the situation and protect the…" is not what the item tests — Supervisors' primary obligation is to client welfare — supervision exists fundamentally to protect clients (ACA F.1.a).

**Rationale:** Supervisors' primary obligation is to client welfare — supervision exists fundamentally to protect clients (ACA F.1.a). The supervisor must address immediate client safety and wellbeing first, then address the supervisee's conduct through appropriate channels. Institutional loyalties do not override client protection.

### 146. tpse146 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Abandonment · difficulty: medium

Therapeutic abandonment is defined as:

**A. [ ]** Any termination of counseling that the client did not initiate themselves during a routine session
> "Any termination of counseling that the client did not initia…" is not what the item tests — Abandonment: unilaterally ending professional services without reasonable notice, adequate referral, or transition planning — leaving a client with

**B. [✔]** Unilateral termination by the counselor without adequate notice, transition support, or referral
> Abandonment: unilaterally ending professional services without reasonable notice, adequate referral, or transition planning — leaving a client without needed care.

**C. [ ]** Ending therapy with clients who have a history of non-payment as the reference material frames it
> "Ending therapy with clients who have a history of non-paymen…" is not what the item tests — Abandonment: unilaterally ending professional services without reasonable notice, adequate referral, or transition planning — leaving a client with

**D. [ ]** Reducing session frequency without explicit client agreement as the reference material frames it
> "Reducing session frequency without explicit client agreement…" is not what the item tests — Abandonment: unilaterally ending professional services without reasonable notice, adequate referral, or transition planning — leaving a client with

**Rationale:** Abandonment: unilaterally ending professional services without reasonable notice, adequate referral, or transition planning — leaving a client without needed care. Clinically indicated termination with proper process and documentation is NOT abandonment, even if the client disagrees.

### 147. tpse147 · domain: ethics · subdomain: FeesPractice · difficulty: medium

Bartering (exchanging goods or services for counseling) is:

**A. [ ]** Prohibited in urban settings but ethically permissible in rural communities as clinicians typically apply it
> "Prohibited in urban settings but ethically permissible in ru…" is not what the item tests — ACA A.10.e: counselors may barter only if the relationship is not exploitative, if the client requests it, and if such arrangements are an accepted

**B. [ ]** Generally prohibited by the ACA Code of Ethics in all circumstances now as the reference material frames it
> "Generally prohibited by the ACA Code of Ethics in all circum…" is not what the item tests — ACA A.10.e: counselors may barter only if the relationship is not exploitative, if the client requests it, and if such arrangements are an accepted

**C. [✔]** Permissible only when it is not exploitative, is in the client's best interest, and is clearly documented
> ACA A.10.e: counselors may barter only if the relationship is not exploitative, if the client requests it, and if such arrangements are an accepted local community practice.

**D. [ ]** Automatically acceptable if the client proposes the arrangement today as the reference material frames it
> "Automatically acceptable if the client proposes the arrangem…" is not what the item tests — ACA A.10.e: counselors may barter only if the relationship is not exploitative, if the client requests it, and if such arrangements are an accepted

**Rationale:** ACA A.10.e: counselors may barter only if the relationship is not exploitative, if the client requests it, and if such arrangements are an accepted local community practice. Documentation and ongoing monitoring of the arrangement's impact on the therapeutic relationship are required.

### 148. tpse148 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Research · difficulty: medium

The principle of "beneficence" in research ethics (Belmont Report) requires:

**A. [ ]** Ensuring all research participants receive monetary compensation for their time
> "Ensuring all research participants receive monetary compensa…" is not what the item tests — Beneficence in research (Belmont Report): maximize benefits and minimize harms to participants.

**B. [ ]** Obtaining institutional review board (IRB) approval before any data collection
> "Obtaining institutional review board (IRB) approval before a…" is not what the item tests — Beneficence in research (Belmont Report): maximize benefits and minimize harms to participants.

**C. [ ]** Including diverse and representative populations in all research samples now
> "Including diverse and representative populations in all rese…" is not what the item tests — Beneficence in research (Belmont Report): maximize benefits and minimize harms to participants.

**D. [✔]** Maximizing possible benefits to participants while minimizing possible harms
> Beneficence in research (Belmont Report): maximize benefits and minimize harms to participants.

**Rationale:** Beneficence in research (Belmont Report): maximize benefits and minimize harms to participants. Distinguished from nonmaleficence (do no harm). IRB approval is a procedural safeguard; diverse samples = justice; compensation = a standard practice, not a named ethical principle.

### 149. tpse149 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Impairment · difficulty: medium

A counselor observes that a colleague appears intoxicated while actively working with clients. The MOST ethical FIRST step is to:

**A. [✔]** Ensure immediate client safety; then address the colleague directly or through supervisory channels
> Client safety is the immediate non-negotiable priority.

**B. [ ]** Continue observing over several more incidents to confirm the pattern before acting during counseling
> "Continue observing over several more incidents to confirm th…" is not what the item tests — Client safety is the immediate non-negotiable priority.

**C. [ ]** Consult other colleagues first to verify the observation before taking any action during counseling
> "Consult other colleagues first to verify the observation bef…" is not what the item tests — Client safety is the immediate non-negotiable priority.

**D. [ ]** Report immediately and exclusively to the state licensing board as the reference material frames it
> "Report immediately and exclusively to the state licensing bo…" is not what the item tests — Client safety is the immediate non-negotiable priority.

**Rationale:** Client safety is the immediate non-negotiable priority. If impairment is clear and clients are at risk, intervening immediately is ethically required. ACA C.2.g: counselors assist impaired colleagues in seeking help. Reporting to the licensing board may follow, but the first obligation is ensuring client welfare.

### 150. tpse150 · domain: ethics · subdomain: Diversity · difficulty: hard

A counselor's moral objection to a client's legal lifestyle (e.g., consensual non-monogamy) allows them, per ACA Code, to:

**A. [ ]** Decline all services to clients who engage in this legal lifestyle as the reference material frames it
> "Decline all services to clients who engage in this legal lif…" is not what the item tests — ACA A.11.b (2014 revision): counselors cannot refuse services based solely on personal values when this constitutes discrimination against protecte

**B. [✔]** Refer only when the referral is clinically appropriate — not merely because of the values difference
> ACA A.11.b (2014 revision): counselors cannot refuse services based solely on personal values when this constitutes discrimination against protected characteristics or legally-protected lifestyles.

**C. [ ]** Incorporate their values into the client's treatment goals where ethical principles are involved now
> "Incorporate their values into the client's treatment goals w…" is not what the item tests — ACA A.11.b (2014 revision): counselors cannot refuse services based solely on personal values when this constitutes discrimination against protecte

**D. [ ]** Explore how the behavior aligns with the counselor's values as a therapeutic intervention in practice
> "Explore how the behavior aligns with the counselor's values …" is not what the item tests — ACA A.11.b (2014 revision): counselors cannot refuse services based solely on personal values when this constitutes discrimination against protecte

**Rationale:** ACA A.11.b (2014 revision): counselors cannot refuse services based solely on personal values when this constitutes discrimination against protected characteristics or legally-protected lifestyles. They may refer when there is a genuine, documentable therapeutic limitation — not simply because of a value conflict. The 2014 revision explicitly addresses this scenario.

### 152. csi002 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: Microskills · difficulty: medium

A counselor says, "So your boss criticized you in front of the team, and you've been replaying it ever since." This is BEST classified as:

**A. [ ]** Reflection of feeling
> "Reflection of feeling" is not what the item tests — A paraphrase restates the cognitive/factual content of the client's message in the counselor's own words.

**B. [ ]** Confrontation
> "Confrontation" is not what the item tests — A paraphrase restates the cognitive/factual content of the client's message in the counselor's own words.

**C. [ ]** Interpretation
> "Interpretation" is not what the item tests — A paraphrase restates the cognitive/factual content of the client's message in the counselor's own words.

**D. [✔]** Paraphrase
> A paraphrase restates the cognitive/factual content of the client's message in the counselor's own words. No feeling word is added, and no interpretation is offered — distinguishing it from reflection or interpretation.

**Rationale:** A paraphrase restates the cognitive/factual content of the client's message in the counselor's own words. No feeling word is added, and no interpretation is offered — distinguishing it from reflection or interpretation.

> "Subtracts from what the client expressed as used as the refe…" is not what the item tests — Carkhuff's Level 3 is the minimally facilitative baseline: the response is interchangeable with the client's expression.

**Rationale:** Carkhuff's Level 3 is the minimally facilitative baseline: the response is interchangeable with the client's expression. Levels 1–2 subtract; Levels 4–5 add depth beyond what was expressed, moving toward advanced empathy.

> Behavioral experiments are hypothesis-testing exercises: the client predicts what will happen based on a belief, then conducts an experiment to gather evidence.

**Rationale:** Wolpe's systematic desensitization: (1) teach deep relaxation (reciprocal inhibition), (2) construct a fear hierarchy from least to most anxious, (3) pair each step with relaxation, moving up only when the lower step produces no anxiety.

> "Classical conditioning through stimulus pairing in this doma…" is not what the item tests — Token economies use secondary (conditioned) reinforcers (tokens/points) that are exchanged for primary or preferred backup reinforcers.

> "Extinction of maladaptive behavior through reinforcement wit…" is not what the item tests — Token economies use secondary (conditioned) reinforcers (tokens/points) that are exchanged for primary or preferred backup reinforcers.

**D. [ ]** Observational learning through modeled behavior in this domain as the reference material frames it
> "Observational learning through modeled behavior in this doma…" is not what the item tests — Token economies use secondary (conditioned) reinforcers (tokens/points) that are exchanged for primary or preferred backup reinforcers.

**Rationale:** Token economies use secondary (conditioned) reinforcers (tokens/points) that are exchanged for primary or preferred backup reinforcers. They work through positive reinforcement, shaping target behaviors via operant conditioning.

**B. [✔]** Reinforcing a behavior that physically cannot occur simultaneously with the problem behavior
> DRI: reinforce a behavior that is physically incompatible with the target behavior (e.g., keeping hands on the desk is incompatible with hitting).

**C. [ ]** Withholding reinforcement to extinguish the problem behavior directly during a routine session
> "Withholding reinforcement to extinguish the problem behavior…" is not what the item tests — DRI: reinforce a behavior that is physically incompatible with the target behavior (e.g., keeping hands on the desk is incompatible with hitting).

**D. [ ]** Reinforcing any behavior that reduces the target problem behavior at the level the item tests
> "Reinforcing any behavior that reduces the target problem beh…" is not what the item tests — DRI: reinforce a behavior that is physically incompatible with the target behavior (e.g., keeping hands on the desk is incompatible with hitting).

**Rationale:** DRI: reinforce a behavior that is physically incompatible with the target behavior (e.g., keeping hands on the desk is incompatible with hitting). DRO reinforces absence; DRA reinforces any alternative behavior that is not necessarily incompatible.

> "Phase 2 — Preparation" is not what the item tests — EMDR Phase 4 (Desensitization): client holds the target memory, negative cognition, emotion, and body sensation while engaging in bilateral stimulation.

> "Phase 7 — Closure" is not what the item tests — EMDR Phase 4 (Desensitization): client holds the target memory, negative cognition, emotion, and body sensation while engaging in bilateral stimulation.

**C. [ ]** Precontemplation — to demonstrate they have a problem
> "Precontemplation — to demonstrate they have a problem" is not what the item tests — Decisional balance (exploring pros and cons of change and of staying the same) is most useful in contemplation, where ambivalence is central.

**D. [ ]** Action — to reinforce commitment to current change efforts
> "Action — to reinforce commitment to current change efforts" is not what the item tests — Decisional balance (exploring pros and cons of change and of staying the same) is most useful in contemplation, where ambivalence is central.

**Rationale:** Decisional balance (exploring pros and cons of change and of staying the same) is most useful in contemplation, where ambivalence is central. In precontemplation, it can backfire by reinforcing sustain talk; in action/maintenance, the client has already committed and doesn't need to re-examine the decision.

### 170. csi020 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: SFBTInterventions · difficulty: medium

In SFBT, "compliments" delivered as part of the consultation break message serve to:

**A. [ ]** Build rapport early in the therapeutic relationship at the level the item tests
> "Build rapport early in the therapeutic relationship at the l…" is not what the item tests — SFBT compliments identify and affirm what the client is already doing that relates to the solution.

**B. [✔]** Validate the client's strengths and frame exceptions as evidence of capability
> SFBT compliments identify and affirm what the client is already doing that relates to the solution.

**C. [ ]** Motivate clients through praise for stated intentions during a routine session
> "Motivate clients through praise for stated intentions during…" is not what the item tests — SFBT compliments identify and affirm what the client is already doing that relates to the solution.

**D. [ ]** Provide positive reinforcement for session attendance during a routine session
> "Provide positive reinforcement for session attendance during…" is not what the item tests — SFBT compliments identify and affirm what the client is already doing that relates to the solution.

**Rationale:** SFBT compliments identify and affirm what the client is already doing that relates to the solution. They are not generic praise — they link observed strengths to the client's goals, priming the "task" (homework) that follows in the consultation message.

> "Identify where in their body they feel the problem most inte…" is not what the item tests — Mapping influence is a two-phase externalization process: (1) map how the problem influences the person's life and relationships, and (2) map how t

> Mapping influence is a two-phase externalization process: (1) map how the problem influences the person's life and relationships, and (2) map how the person influences the problem. Together, they expose agency and prepare for re-authoring.

**D. [ ]** Draw a diagram of their social support network as usually described
> "Draw a diagram of their social support network as usually de…" is not what the item tests — Mapping influence is a two-phase externalization process: (1) map how the problem influences the person's life and relationships, and (2) map how t

**Rationale:** Mapping influence is a two-phase externalization process: (1) map how the problem influences the person's life and relationships, and (2) map how the person influences the problem. Together, they expose agency and prepare for re-authoring.

> "Practicing assertiveness skills in a safe environment before…" is not what the item tests — The two-chair (Gestalt): client alternates between two chairs, speaking from each of two conflicting internal parts (e.g., the self-critical part v

**B. [ ]** Desensitizing clients to interpersonal conflict by rehearsing difficult conversations
> "Desensitizing clients to interpersonal conflict by rehearsin…" is not what the item tests — The two-chair (Gestalt): client alternates between two chairs, speaking from each of two conflicting internal parts (e.g., the self-critical part v

**C. [ ]** Allowing the client to experience how others perceive their behavior in practice
> "Allowing the client to experience how others perceive their …" is not what the item tests — The two-chair (Gestalt): client alternates between two chairs, speaking from each of two conflicting internal parts (e.g., the self-critical part v

> The two-chair (Gestalt): client alternates between two chairs, speaking from each of two conflicting internal parts (e.g., the self-critical part vs.

**Rationale:** The two-chair (Gestalt): client alternates between two chairs, speaking from each of two conflicting internal parts (e.g., the self-critical part vs. the wounded part). The dialogue promotes contact, awareness, and eventual integration of the split.

**B. [ ]** Perform the problem behavior deliberately to increase voluntary control over it
> "Perform the problem behavior deliberately to increase volunt…" is not what the item tests — The exaggeration experiment: client is asked to amplify a small nonverbal cue (a tapping foot, a half-smile, a clenched fist) to heighten awareness

**C. [ ]** Exaggerate positive self-statements to counteract negative thinking at intake
> "Exaggerate positive self-statements to counteract negative t…" is not what the item tests — The exaggeration experiment: client is asked to amplify a small nonverbal cue (a tapping foot, a half-smile, a clenched fist) to heighten awareness

**D. [ ]** Catastrophize about a feared outcome to reveal its irrationality by counselors
> "Catastrophize about a feared outcome to reveal its irrationa…" is not what the item tests — The exaggeration experiment: client is asked to amplify a small nonverbal cue (a tapping foot, a half-smile, a clenched fist) to heighten awareness

**Rationale:** The exaggeration experiment: client is asked to amplify a small nonverbal cue (a tapping foot, a half-smile, a clenched fist) to heighten awareness of the emotion or message it carries. This brings body-held experience into conscious awareness.

> Bibliotherapy uses carefully selected reading materials — self-help books, memoirs, workbooks, or fiction — as therapeutic adjuncts. It provides psychoeducation, reduces isolation, and often prompts material for exploration in session.

**C. [ ]** Replace evidence-based treatment for clients with subclinical presentations during a routine session
> "Replace evidence-based treatment for clients with subclinica…" is not what the item tests — Bibliotherapy uses carefully selected reading materials — self-help books, memoirs, workbooks, or fiction — as therapeutic adjuncts.

**D. [ ]** Assign memoir reading to clients who struggle with verbal expression as clinicians typically apply it
> "Assign memoir reading to clients who struggle with verbal ex…" is not what the item tests — Bibliotherapy uses carefully selected reading materials — self-help books, memoirs, workbooks, or fiction — as therapeutic adjuncts.

**Rationale:** Bibliotherapy uses carefully selected reading materials — self-help books, memoirs, workbooks, or fiction — as therapeutic adjuncts. It provides psychoeducation, reduces isolation, and often prompts material for exploration in session.

> "Use expressive arts only with children, not adults during a …" is not what the item tests — ACA C.2.a (competence): basic expressive arts activities (drawing, journaling, metaphor-based exercises) may fall within a counselor's scope with a

**Rationale:** ACA C.2.a (competence): basic expressive arts activities (drawing, journaling, metaphor-based exercises) may fall within a counselor's scope with appropriate training. Advanced or specialized art therapy applications require credentialed training. Scope of practice, not categorical prohibition, governs use.

### 179. csi029 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: GroupInterventions · difficulty: medium

A group member discloses suicidal ideation mid-session. The group leader should FIRST:

**A. [ ]** End the group session for all members immediately in practice as the reference material frames it
> "End the group session for all members immediately in practic…" is not what the item tests — The leader's duty to protect client safety supersedes group process.

**B. [ ]** Refer the matter to the co-leader and continue the group now as the reference material frames it
> "Refer the matter to the co-leader and continue the group now…" is not what the item tests — The leader's duty to protect client safety supersedes group process.

**C. [✔]** Conduct a brief safety assessment with the member and address safety before continuing the group
> The leader's duty to protect client safety supersedes group process.

**D. [ ]** Ask the group to support the member through the disclosure now as the reference material frames it
> "Ask the group to support the member through the disclosure n…" is not what the item tests — The leader's duty to protect client safety supersedes group process.

**Rationale:** The leader's duty to protect client safety supersedes group process. A brief in-session assessment determines urgency. The leader may step out briefly with the member or pause the group, ensuring safety before considering whether and how to continue. The group's experience of the event also becomes therapeutic material.

### 180. csi030 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: GroupInterventions · difficulty: hard

Research consistently shows that grouping antisocial or conduct-disordered adolescents together in group therapy:

**A. [ ]** Is effective only when combined with psychoeducation now as the reference material frames it
> "Is effective only when combined with psychoeducation now as …" is not what the item tests — Deviancy training (Dishion et al.): peer reinforcement of deviant talk and behavior in homogeneous antisocial youth groups can increase problem beh

**B. [ ]** Is the most efficient way to reach this population here as the reference material frames it
> "Is the most efficient way to reach this population here as t…" is not what the item tests — Deviancy training (Dishion et al.): peer reinforcement of deviant talk and behavior in homogeneous antisocial youth groups can increase problem beh

**C. [ ]** Is equivalent in effectiveness to individual therapy now as the reference material frames it
> "Is equivalent in effectiveness to individual therapy now as …" is not what the item tests — Deviancy training (Dishion et al.): peer reinforcement of deviant talk and behavior in homogeneous antisocial youth groups can increase problem beh

**D. [✔]** Can worsen outcomes through deviancy training and peer reinforcement of antisocial behavior
> Deviancy training (Dishion et al.): peer reinforcement of deviant talk and behavior in homogeneous antisocial youth groups can increase problem behaviors.

**Rationale:** Deviancy training (Dishion et al.): peer reinforcement of deviant talk and behavior in homogeneous antisocial youth groups can increase problem behaviors. Heterogeneous groups (mixing prosocial and antisocial youth) or individual/family approaches are preferable.

> Joining: the therapist temporarily adopts the family's language, pace, and relational style to establish trust and entry into the system before attempting restructuring interventions.

**B. [ ]** Joining the identified patient in validating their perspective against the family as used
> "Joining the identified patient in validating their perspecti…" is not what the item tests — Joining: the therapist temporarily adopts the family's language, pace, and relational style to establish trust and entry into the system before att

> "Forming a coalition with the parental subsystem to enforce h…" is not what the item tests — Joining: the therapist temporarily adopts the family's language, pace, and relational style to establish trust and entry into the system before att

**Rationale:** Joining: the therapist temporarily adopts the family's language, pace, and relational style to establish trust and entry into the system before attempting restructuring interventions. Without joining, structural interventions are met with resistance.

> Systemic reframing repositions symptoms as serving a relational or protective function in the family system (e.g., "Your son's acting out may be his way of keeping you and your wife focused together").

> "Is used only when the family's narrative is factually inaccu…" is not what the item tests — Systemic reframing repositions symptoms as serving a relational or protective function in the family system (e.g., "Your son's acting out may be hi

**Rationale:** Systemic reframing repositions symptoms as serving a relational or protective function in the family system (e.g., "Your son's acting out may be his way of keeping you and your wife focused together"). It shifts from individual pathology to relational meaning.

> "Mapping DSM diagnoses across family members as used as the r…" is not what the item tests — Genograms (Bowen/McGoldrick) are multigenerational family maps showing structure, relationships (enmeshment, cut-off, conflict), significant events

> Genograms (Bowen/McGoldrick) are multigenerational family maps showing structure, relationships (enmeshment, cut-off, conflict), significant events, and patterns (substance use, mental illness, abuse) across at least three generations.

**D. [ ]** Providing a legal record of family structure for custody evaluations at the intake stage

Gottman's "Four Horsemen" — predictors of relationship dissolution — are:

**A. [ ]** Anger, silence, criticism, jealousy by counselors
> "Anger, silence, criticism, jealousy by counselors" is not what the item tests — Gottman's Four Horsemen: Criticism (attacking character), Contempt (superiority/disgust — strongest predictor of divorce), Defensiveness (counter-complaint or

**B. [ ]** Criticism, resentment, disengagement, hostility now
> "Criticism, resentment, disengagement, hostility now" is not what the item tests — Gottman's Four Horsemen: Criticism (attacking character), Contempt (superiority/disgust — strongest predictor of divorce), Defensiveness (counter-complaint o

**C. [ ]** Contempt, avoidance, withdrawal, passive aggression
> "Contempt, avoidance, withdrawal, passive aggression" is not what the item tests — Gottman's Four Horsemen: Criticism (attacking character), Contempt (superiority/disgust — strongest predictor of divorce), Defensiveness (counter-complaint o

> "Behavioral contracts that reward positive couple interaction…" is not what the item tests — EFT (Johnson, 1985): grounded in attachment theory, EFT identifies negative interaction cycles (pursuer-withdrawer), accesses the primary attachmen

> "Teaching communication skills and conflict resolution strate…" is not what the item tests — EFT (Johnson, 1985): grounded in attachment theory, EFT identifies negative interaction cycles (pursuer-withdrawer), accesses the primary attachmen

**D. [ ]** Cognitive restructuring of negative attributions about the partner as the reference material frames it
> "Cognitive restructuring of negative attributions about the p…" is not what the item tests — EFT (Johnson, 1985): grounded in attachment theory, EFT identifies negative interaction cycles (pursuer-withdrawer), accesses the primary attachmen

**Rationale:** EFT (Johnson, 1985): grounded in attachment theory, EFT identifies negative interaction cycles (pursuer-withdrawer), accesses the primary attachment emotions beneath the cycle (fear, longing, shame), and restructures interactions around vulnerable, authentic expression to create secure bonding events.

**B. [✔]** The client's perspective on the presenting concern
> The six-step model: (1) Define the problem from the client's perspective, (2) Ensure safety, (3) Provide support, (4) Examine alternatives, (5) Make plans, (6) Obtain commitment.

**C. [ ]** The counselor's clinical assessment and diagnostic formulation
> "The counselor's clinical assessment and diagnostic formulati…" is not what the item tests — The six-step model: (1) Define the problem from the client's perspective, (2) Ensure safety, (3) Provide support, (4) Examine alternatives, (5) Mak

**D. [ ]** The client's family's description of the events at intake
> "The client's family's description of events" is not what the item tests — The six-step model: (1) Define the problem from the client's perspective, (2) Ensure safety, (3) Provide support, (4) Examine alternatives, (5) Make plans, (6) Obtai

### 187. csi037 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: CrisisInterventions · difficulty: hard

Means restriction counseling in suicide intervention is evidence-based because:

**A. [ ]** It is required by HIPAA when a client discloses suicidal ideation at intake as the reference material frames it
> "It is required by HIPAA when a client discloses suicidal ide…" is not what the item tests — Means restriction works because suicidal crises are typically acute and time-limited.

**B. [ ]** It provides the client with a sense of control over their environment today as the reference material frames it
> "It provides the client with a sense of control over their en…" is not what the item tests — Means restriction works because suicidal crises are typically acute and time-limited.

**C. [✔]** Suicidal crises are time-limited and reducing access to lethal means during the crisis period reduces mortality
> Means restriction works because suicidal crises are typically acute and time-limited.

**D. [ ]** It removes the underlying desire to die as the reference material frames it now as the reference material frames it
> "It removes the underlying desire to die as the reference mat…" is not what the item tests — Means restriction works because suicidal crises are typically acute and time-limited.

**Rationale:** Means restriction works because suicidal crises are typically acute and time-limited. Most people who survive an attempt do not go on to die by suicide. Reducing access to highly lethal means (firearms, medications) during the crisis window significantly reduces mortality — even without changing the underlying wish to die.

### 188. csi038 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: CrisisInterventions · difficulty: medium

**A. [ ]** No-suicide contracts are preferred for clients with prior attempts (as commonly taught)
> "No-suicide contracts are preferred for clients with prior at…" is not what the item tests — Safety plans (Stanley & Brown): collaboratively identify warning signs, internal coping strategies, social contacts, professional resources, and me

**B. [ ]** Safety plans are used only in inpatient settings (as the reference material frames it)
> "Safety plans are used only in inpatient settings as the refe…" is not what the item tests — Safety plans (Stanley & Brown): collaboratively identify warning signs, internal coping strategies, social contacts, professional resources, and me

**C. [ ]** Safety plans are legally binding; no-suicide contracts are not (during routine sessions)
> "Safety plans are legally binding; no-suicide contracts are n…" is not what the item tests — Safety plans (Stanley & Brown): collaboratively identify warning signs, internal coping strategies, social contacts, professional resources, and me

**D. [✔]** Safety plans are collaboratively developed guides; no-suicide contracts are not evidence-based
> Safety plans (Stanley & Brown): collaboratively identify warning signs, internal coping strategies, social contacts, professional resources, and means restriction.

**Rationale:** Safety plans (Stanley & Brown): collaboratively identify warning signs, internal coping strategies, social contacts, professional resources, and means restriction. Research supports their effectiveness. No-suicide contracts lack evidence, may reduce the client's sense of agency, and create liability concerns if clients feel they signed under pressure.

**B. [ ]** Conducting formal diagnostic assessments with standardized tools as clinicians typically apply it

**C. [ ]** Facilitating emotional debriefing for all exposed individuals as clinicians typically apply it
> "Facilitating emotional debriefing for all exposed individual…" is not what the item tests — PFA (NCTSN/NCPTSD) focuses on: ensuring safety, providing comfort, stabilizing distressed individuals, gathering information, offering practical as

**D. [ ]** Administering PTSD screening scales to all affected survivors as clinicians typically apply it
> "Administering PTSD screening scales to all affected survivor…" is not what the item tests — PFA (NCTSN/NCPTSD) focuses on: ensuring safety, providing comfort, stabilizing distressed individuals, gathering information, offering practical as

**Rationale:** PFA (NCTSN/NCPTSD) focuses on: ensuring safety, providing comfort, stabilizing distressed individuals, gathering information, offering practical assistance, connecting with supports, and providing information about coping. It does NOT involve formal diagnosis or routine debriefing.

> PMR (Jacobson): client alternately tenses and releases major muscle groups, learning to recognize and produce the contrast between tension and relaxation. This teaches voluntary control of physiological arousal through the somatic pathway.

**C. [ ]** Engaging in aerobic activity to metabolize stress hormones during a routine session
> "Engaging in aerobic activity to metabolize stress hormones d…" is not what the item tests — PMR (Jacobson): client alternately tenses and releases major muscle groups, learning to recognize and produce the contrast between tension and rela

> "Slowing breath rate to counteract the physiological arousal …" is not what the item tests — PMR (Jacobson): client alternately tenses and releases major muscle groups, learning to recognize and produce the contrast between tension and rela

**Rationale:** PMR (Jacobson): client alternately tenses and releases major muscle groups, learning to recognize and produce the contrast between tension and relaxation. This teaches voluntary control of physiological arousal through the somatic pathway.

### 191. csi041 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: RelaxationInterventions · difficulty: medium

Diaphragmatic breathing (belly breathing) reduces anxiety primarily through:

**A. [ ]** Reducing muscle tension through rhythmic physical movement as clinicians typically apply it
> "Reducing muscle tension through rhythmic physical movement a…" is not what the item tests — Slow diaphragmatic breathing activates the parasympathetic nervous system via the vagus nerve (stimulating the relaxation response), counteracting

**B. [ ]** Distracting the client from anxious thoughts through attention to breathing by counselors
> "Distracting the client from anxious thoughts through attenti…" is not what the item tests — Slow diaphragmatic breathing activates the parasympathetic nervous system via the vagus nerve (stimulating the relaxation response), counteracting

**C. [✔]** Activating the parasympathetic nervous system, counteracting the fight-or-flight response
> Slow diaphragmatic breathing activates the parasympathetic nervous system via the vagus nerve (stimulating the relaxation response), counteracting sympathetic activation.

**D. [ ]** Increasing blood oxygen to counteract hyperventilation-induced dizziness during counseling
> "Increasing blood oxygen to counteract hyperventilation-induc…" is not what the item tests — Slow diaphragmatic breathing activates the parasympathetic nervous system via the vagus nerve (stimulating the relaxation response), counteracting

**Rationale:** Slow diaphragmatic breathing activates the parasympathetic nervous system via the vagus nerve (stimulating the relaxation response), counteracting sympathetic activation. This is why it is effective for acute anxiety, panic, and PTSD hyperarousal.

> Effective homework: collaboratively developed (not imposed), logically connected to in-session work, specific and achievable, and always reviewed in the following session.

> "Complex enough to challenge the client between sessions toda…" is not what the item tests — Effective homework: collaboratively developed (not imposed), logically connected to in-session work, specific and achievable, and always reviewed i

### 196. csi046 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: MindfulnessInterventions · difficulty: medium

> "Mindful breathing as a foundation for distress tolerance at …" is not what the item tests — "Leaves on a stream": client imagines placing each thought on a leaf and watching it float downstream, not grasping or pushing any thought away.

**B. [ ]** Values clarification through contemplative practice as clinicians typically apply it
> "Values clarification through contemplative practice as clini…" is not what the item tests — "Leaves on a stream": client imagines placing each thought on a leaf and watching it float downstream, not grasping or pushing any thought away.

> "Grounding during dissociative episodes at intake as the refe…" is not what the item tests — "Leaves on a stream": client imagines placing each thought on a leaf and watching it float downstream, not grasping or pushing any thought away.

**D. [✔]** Cognitive defusion — observing thoughts as passing mental events without attachment
> "Leaves on a stream": client imagines placing each thought on a leaf and watching it float downstream, not grasping or pushing any thought away.

**Rationale:** "Leaves on a stream": client imagines placing each thought on a leaf and watching it float downstream, not grasping or pushing any thought away. This trains defusion — the ability to observe thoughts as events without being swept along by them.

### 197. csi047 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: DeescalationInterventions · difficulty: medium

When a client in session becomes verbally aggressive and escalating, the counselor's FIRST priority is to:

**A. [✔]** Ensure physical safety of all parties while maintaining a calm, non-threatening presence
> De-escalation: prioritize safety — counselor's own and the client's.

**B. [ ]** Set a firm limit and remind the client of the behavioral agreement during a routine session
> "Set a firm limit and remind the client of the behavioral agr…" is not what the item tests — De-escalation: prioritize safety — counselor's own and the client's.

**C. [ ]** Terminate the session immediately and ask the client to leave at the level the item tests
> "Terminate the session immediately and ask the client to leav…" is not what the item tests — De-escalation: prioritize safety — counselor's own and the client's.

**D. [ ]** Contact security or law enforcement preemptively now as the reference material frames it
> "Contact security or law enforcement preemptively now as the …" is not what the item tests — De-escalation: prioritize safety — counselor's own and the client's.

**Rationale:** De-escalation: prioritize safety — counselor's own and the client's. Maintain a calm voice, non-threatening posture, personal space, and reduced stimulation. Avoid power struggles. Limit-setting and termination may follow, but safety comes first. Security is contacted only if risk of harm is imminent.

> Peer support specialists with lived experience of mental health or substance use challenges provide unique recovery-oriented support: hope through shared experience, practical navigation of systems, and engagement of pop…

> "Provide a less expensive alternative to licensed clinical se…" is not what the item tests — Peer support specialists with lived experience of mental health or substance use challenges provide unique recovery-oriented support: hope through

**D. [ ]** Reduce clinician caseloads by taking on lower acuity clients as clinicians typically apply it
> "Reduce clinician caseloads by taking on lower acuity clients…" is not what the item tests — Peer support specialists with lived experience of mental health or substance use challenges provide unique recovery-oriented support: hope through

**Rationale:** Peer support specialists with lived experience of mental health or substance use challenges provide unique recovery-oriented support: hope through shared experience, practical navigation of systems, and engagement of populations that distrust traditional clinical services. They complement — not replace — clinical services.

### 200. csi050 · domain: skills_interventions · subdomain: TerminationInterventions · difficulty: hard

A client who has achieved all treatment goals but is distressed about ending therapy says, "You're the only person who has ever really listened to me." The counselor's BEST response:

**A. [ ]** Remind the client that therapy is a professional, not personal, relationship in practice
> "Remind the client that therapy is a professional, not person…" is not what the item tests — The statement is clinically rich and emotionally valid — the relationship has been meaningful and real.

**B. [ ]** Interpret this as unresolved abandonment issues requiring more treatment in this domain
> "Interpret this as unresolved abandonment issues requiring mo…" is not what the item tests — The statement is clinically rich and emotionally valid — the relationship has been meaningful and real.

> "Offer to extend therapy given the depth of the relationship …" is not what the item tests — The statement is clinically rich and emotionally valid — the relationship has been meaningful and real.

**D. [✔]** Validate the meaning of the relationship while exploring and building external supports
> The statement is clinically rich and emotionally valid — the relationship has been meaningful and real.

**Rationale:** The statement is clinically rich and emotionally valid — the relationship has been meaningful and real. Effective response: validate genuinely, explore what made this relationship different, use it to identify what the client can seek in other relationships, and strengthen the support network for post-termination.

### 201. ppe001 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: ACACode · difficulty: easy

The ACA Code of Ethics is BEST understood as:

**A. [✔]** A professional standard establishing aspirational ideals and enforceable conduct standards
> The ACA Code of Ethics combines aspirational principles (how counselors should strive to act) with enforceable mandatory standards (what they must or must not do).

**B. [ ]** A legal document with the force of state law in all jurisdictions during a routine session
> "A legal document with the force of state law in all jurisdic…" is not what the item tests — The ACA Code of Ethics combines aspirational principles (how counselors should strive to act) with enforceable mandatory standards (what they must

**C. [ ]** A billing and documentation compliance guide for third-party payers during a routine session
> "A billing and documentation compliance guide for third-party…" is not what the item tests — The ACA Code of Ethics combines aspirational principles (how counselors should strive to act) with enforceable mandatory standards (what they must

**D. [ ]** A set of recommendations that are entirely voluntary for licensed counselors by counselors
> "A set of recommendations that are entirely voluntary for lic…" is not what the item tests — The ACA Code of Ethics combines aspirational principles (how counselors should strive to act) with enforceable mandatory standards (what they must

**Rationale:** The ACA Code of Ethics combines aspirational principles (how counselors should strive to act) with enforceable mandatory standards (what they must or must not do). It is not law, but licensing boards and ACA may sanction violations, and it is used as a standard of care in legal proceedings.

### 202. ppe002 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Confidentiality · difficulty: medium

Privileged communication differs from confidentiality in that privilege:

**A. [ ]** Applies to all mental health communications regardless of setting as usually described
> "Applies to all mental health communications regardless of se…" is not what the item tests — Confidentiality is the counselor's ethical duty.

**B. [✔]** Is a legal right held by the client that protects disclosures from being used in court
> Confidentiality is the counselor's ethical duty.

**C. [ ]** Can be waived by the counselor in the interest of the client in the outpatient setting
> "Can be waived by the counselor in the interest of the client…" is not what the item tests — Confidentiality is the counselor's ethical duty.

**D. [ ]** Is an ethical obligation while confidentiality is a legal right during a routine session
> "Is an ethical obligation while confidentiality is a legal ri…" is not what the item tests — Confidentiality is the counselor's ethical duty.

**Rationale:** Confidentiality is the counselor's ethical duty. Privilege is the client's legal right to prevent testimony about therapeutic communications in legal proceedings. Privilege is waived by the CLIENT, not the counselor. It varies by state and relationship type.

### 203. ppe003 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Records · difficulty: medium

Under HIPAA, when a client requests a copy of their mental health records, the covered entity must respond within:

**A. [ ]** 24 hours for urgent requests during a routine session
> "24 hours for urgent requests during a routine session" is not what the item tests — HIPAA Access Rule (45 CFR §164.524): covered entities must provide access to requested records within 30 days.

**B. [ ]** 60 days under all circumstances at the intake stage
> "60 days under all circumstances at the intake stage" is not what the item tests — HIPAA Access Rule (45 CFR §164.524): covered entities must provide access to requested records within 30 days.

**C. [✔]** 30 days, with a possible one-time 30-day extension
> HIPAA Access Rule (45 CFR §164.524): covered entities must provide access to requested records within 30 days.

**D. [ ]** 10 business days as an absolute standard at intake
> "10 business days as an absolute standard at intake" is not what the item tests — HIPAA Access Rule (45 CFR §164.524): covered entities must provide access to requested records within 30 days.

**Rationale:** HIPAA Access Rule (45 CFR §164.524): covered entities must provide access to requested records within 30 days. A one-time 30-day extension is permitted if written notice with reason is provided. Counselors may not withhold records solely to collect unpaid balances.

### 204. ppe004 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Records · difficulty: hard

A counselor's "psychotherapy notes" (process notes) receive a higher level of HIPAA protection than standard records because:

**A. [ ]** They can only be released with court order, rarely with client consent (in item-tests usage)
> "They can only be released with court order, rarely with clie…" is not what the item tests — HIPAA defines psychotherapy notes as notes recorded in any medium by a healthcare provider who is a mental health professional documenting or analy

**B. [ ]** They are not subject to any disclosure under any circumstances (as typically described here)
> "They are not subject to any disclosure under any circumstanc…" is not what the item tests — HIPAA defines psychotherapy notes as notes recorded in any medium by a healthcare provider who is a mental health professional documenting or analy

**C. [ ]** They are protected by attorney-client privilege as the reference typically frames it now
> "They are protected by attorney-client privilege as the refer…" is not what the item tests — HIPAA defines psychotherapy notes as notes recorded in any medium by a healthcare provider who is a mental health professional documenting or analy

**D. [✔]** They require separate authorization and are excluded from the standard TPO disclosure exception
> HIPAA defines psychotherapy notes as notes recorded in any medium by a healthcare provider who is a mental health professional documenting or analyzing the contents of a conversation, separated from the rest of the record.

**Rationale:** HIPAA defines psychotherapy notes as notes recorded in any medium by a healthcare provider who is a mental health professional documenting or analyzing the contents of a conversation, separated from the rest of the record. They require specific authorization beyond standard TPO disclosures and cannot be released without client consent except in very limited circumstances.

### 205. ppe005 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Supervision · difficulty: medium

Vicarious liability in clinical supervision means:

**A. [✔]** Supervisors may be held legally responsible for harm caused by supervisees under their oversight
> Vicarious liability (respondeat superior): supervisors can be held responsible for harm caused by supervisees under their supervision because they have a duty to ensure supervisee competence and conduct.

**B. [ ]** Supervisees share equal liability with their supervisors for all clinical decisions by counselors
> "Supervisees share equal liability with their supervisors for…" is not what the item tests — Vicarious liability (respondeat superior): supervisors can be held responsible for harm caused by supervisees under their supervision because they

**C. [ ]** Liability is transferred entirely to the supervisee once they obtain independent licensure today
> "Liability is transferred entirely to the supervisee once the…" is not what the item tests — Vicarious liability (respondeat superior): supervisors can be held responsible for harm caused by supervisees under their supervision because they

**D. [ ]** The supervisor is legally liable for any clinical outcome regardless of supervisee conduct today
> "The supervisor is legally liable for any clinical outcome re…" is not what the item tests — Vicarious liability (respondeat superior): supervisors can be held responsible for harm caused by supervisees under their supervision because they

**Rationale:** Vicarious liability (respondeat superior): supervisors can be held responsible for harm caused by supervisees under their supervision because they have a duty to ensure supervisee competence and conduct. This underscores the supervisor's ethical obligation to monitor, correct, and protect clients.

### 206. ppe006 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Consultation · difficulty: medium

When a counselor consults with a colleague about a clinical case, they are ethically obligated to:

**A. [ ]** Obtain written informed consent from the client before any consultation in this domain
> "Obtain written informed consent from the client before any c…" is not what the item tests — Consultation falls under treatment operations (generally permissible under HIPAA) but ethically requires the minimum necessary information.

**B. [✔]** Disclose only the minimum information necessary to accomplish the consultation purpose
> Consultation falls under treatment operations (generally permissible under HIPAA) but ethically requires the minimum necessary information.

**C. [ ]** Avoid any identifying information about the client in all consultations in this domain
> "Avoid any identifying information about the client in all co…" is not what the item tests — Consultation falls under treatment operations (generally permissible under HIPAA) but ethically requires the minimum necessary information.

**D. [ ]** Document only consultations where information was shared, not purely conceptual discussions
> "Document only consultations where information was shared, no…" is not what the item tests — Consultation falls under treatment operations (generally permissible under HIPAA) but ethically requires the minimum necessary information.

**Rationale:** Consultation falls under treatment operations (generally permissible under HIPAA) but ethically requires the minimum necessary information. While complete de-identification is ideal, in practice the minimum necessary standard governs — share only what the consultant needs to be helpful, and document all consultations.

**C. [✔]** Encouraging counselors to contribute to society by offering pro bono or reduced-fee services
> ACA Code A.10.d: counselors are encouraged to contribute to society through pro bono or reduced-fee work. This is aspirational, not a mandatory specific percentage. It reflects the profession's commitment to access and social justice.

**D. [ ]** Specifying that pro bono clients must receive the same services as paying clients in practice
> "Specifying that pro bono clients must receive the same servi…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code A.10.d: counselors are encouraged to contribute to society through pro bono or reduced-fee work.

**Rationale:** ACA Code A.10.d: counselors are encouraged to contribute to society through pro bono or reduced-fee work. This is aspirational, not a mandatory specific percentage. It reflects the profession's commitment to access and social justice.

### 208. ppe008 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: DiagnosisEthics · difficulty: hard

Assigning a diagnosis primarily to secure third-party reimbursement when the diagnosis does not accurately reflect the client's condition is:

**A. [ ]** Permissible if it is done collaboratively with the client
> "Permissible if done collaboratively with the client" is not what the item tests — ACA Code E.5.c: counselors take special care to provide proper diagnosis of mental disorders and do not assign inaccurate diagnoses for billing purposes.

**B. [ ]** Acceptable if the counselor thinks criteria will eventually be met
> "Acceptable if the counselor believes the client will eventua…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code E.5.c: counselors take special care to provide proper diagnosis of mental disorders and do not assign inaccurate diagnoses for billing pur

**C. [ ]** Permitted if the diagnosis is V-code adjusted in the record
> "Permitted if the diagnosis is V-code adjusted in the record" is not what the item tests — ACA Code E.5.c: counselors take special care to provide proper diagnosis of mental disorders and do not assign inaccurate diagnoses for billing purpo

**D. [✔]** An ethical violation that constitutes insurance fraud
> ACA Code E.5.c: counselors take special care to provide proper diagnosis of mental disorders and do not assign inaccurate diagnoses for billing purposes.

**Rationale:** ACA Code E.5.c: counselors take special care to provide proper diagnosis of mental disorders and do not assign inaccurate diagnoses for billing purposes. This constitutes insurance fraud (a criminal act) and an ethical violation, regardless of client consent.

### 209. ppe009 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Technology · difficulty: medium

When storing electronic client records, a counselor's primary HIPAA obligation is to:

**A. [✔]** Implement reasonable physical, technical, and administrative safeguards to protect PHI
> HIPAA Security Rule requires "reasonable and appropriate" safeguards — physical (locked storage), technical (encryption, access controls), and administrative (policies, training).

**B. [ ]** Conduct annual third-party security audits of all systems as clinicians typically apply it
> "Conduct annual third-party security audits of all systems as…" is not what the item tests — HIPAA Security Rule requires "reasonable and appropriate" safeguards — physical (locked storage), technical (encryption, access controls), and admi

**C. [ ]** Use only government-approved encryption software now as the reference material frames it
> "Use only government-approved encryption software now as the …" is not what the item tests — HIPAA Security Rule requires "reasonable and appropriate" safeguards — physical (locked storage), technical (encryption, access controls), and admi

**D. [ ]** Store records exclusively on local hardware with no cloud storage as usually described
> "Store records exclusively on local hardware with no cloud st…" is not what the item tests — HIPAA Security Rule requires "reasonable and appropriate" safeguards — physical (locked storage), technical (encryption, access controls), and admi

**Rationale:** HIPAA Security Rule requires "reasonable and appropriate" safeguards — physical (locked storage), technical (encryption, access controls), and administrative (policies, training). The standard is reasonable safeguards, not specific technology mandates. Cloud storage is permissible with a Business Associate Agreement.

### 210. ppe010 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Advertising · difficulty: medium

A counselor uses client testimonials on their practice website. According to ACA ethics, this:

**A. [ ]** Is acceptable only for testimonials from former clients today as the reference material frames it
> "Is acceptable only for testimonials from former clients toda…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.3.b: counselors do not use testimonials from current clients or others who, because of their particular circumstances, are vulnerable to

**B. [✔]** Is prohibited because the therapeutic relationship creates undue influence over client statements
> ACA Code C.3.b: counselors do not use testimonials from current clients or others who, because of their particular circumstances, are vulnerable to undue influence.

**C. [ ]** Is acceptable if the client provides written consent at intake as the reference material frames it
> "Is acceptable if the client provides written consent at inta…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.3.b: counselors do not use testimonials from current clients or others who, because of their particular circumstances, are vulnerable to

**D. [ ]** Is required to have proper SEO tagging to identify it as promotional as clinicians typically apply it
> "Is required to have proper SEO tagging to identify it as pro…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.3.b: counselors do not use testimonials from current clients or others who, because of their particular circumstances, are vulnerable to

**Rationale:** ACA Code C.3.b: counselors do not use testimonials from current clients or others who, because of their particular circumstances, are vulnerable to undue influence. The power differential in the therapeutic relationship makes genuine freely-given consent for promotional use ethically suspect.
