### 211. ppe011 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Competence · difficulty: medium

A counselor who has practiced a specific evidence-based approach for years without pursuing updated training may be at risk of:

**A. [ ]** Violating supervision requirements only, not competence standards itself as the reference material frames it
> "Violating supervision requirements only, not competence stan…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.2.f: counselors recognize when their skills and knowledge are outdated and take steps to address this.

**B. [ ]** Nothing, as established training is sufficient for the duration of licensure as clinicians typically apply it
> "Nothing, as established training is sufficient for the durat…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.2.f: counselors recognize when their skills and knowledge are outdated and take steps to address this.

**C. [✔]** Practicing with outdated methods that no longer reflect current standards, violating the competence standard
> ACA Code C.2.f: counselors recognize when their skills and knowledge are outdated and take steps to address this.

**D. [ ]** Redundant practice, which is ethically neutral in the outpatient setting as the reference material frames it
> "Redundant practice, which is ethically neutral in the outpat…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.2.f: counselors recognize when their skills and knowledge are outdated and take steps to address this.

**Rationale:** ACA Code C.2.f: counselors recognize when their skills and knowledge are outdated and take steps to address this. Competence requires ongoing professional development, not just initial training. Evidence-based practices evolve; continuing education is an ethical obligation.

### 212. ppe012 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Impairment · difficulty: hard

A counselor experiencing burnout to the degree that it is affecting their clinical judgment is ETHICALLY required to:

**A. [ ]** Seek supervision, which substitutes for any other action when impaired now as clinicians typically apply it
> "Seek supervision, which substitutes for any other action whe…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.2.g: when counselors are impaired (by burnout, personal crises, substance use, etc.) to the point of affecting practice, they are ethica

**B. [ ]** Continue working and address burnout through self-care strategies only now as clinicians typically apply it
> "Continue working and address burnout through self-care strat…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.2.g: when counselors are impaired (by burnout, personal crises, substance use, etc.) to the point of affecting practice, they are ethica

**C. [ ]** Disclose their impairment to all current clients immediately in practice as the reference material frames it
> "Disclose their impairment to all current clients immediately…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code C.2.g: when counselors are impaired (by burnout, personal crises, substance use, etc.) to the point of affecting practice, they are ethica

**D. [✔]** Limit, suspend, or terminate their professional responsibilities to the extent necessary to protect clients
> ACA Code C.2.g: when counselors are impaired (by burnout, personal crises, substance use, etc.) to the point of affecting practice, they are ethically obligated to limit, suspend, or terminate professional responsibiliti…

**Rationale:** ACA Code C.2.g: when counselors are impaired (by burnout, personal crises, substance use, etc.) to the point of affecting practice, they are ethically obligated to limit, suspend, or terminate professional responsibilities to protect clients. Self-care alone is insufficient when clients are at risk.

### 213. ppe013 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: FERPA · difficulty: hard

In a school setting, student counseling records that are maintained separately from the general education record and used only by treatment personnel are governed primarily by:

**A. [✔]** HIPAA — they are health records exempt from FERPA's education record definition
> FERPA excludes "sole possession records" maintained by a treatment provider and not shared with others from its education record definition.

**B. [ ]** FERPA — they are education records at intake as the reference material frames it
> "FERPA — they are education records at intake as the referenc…" is not what the item tests — FERPA excludes "sole possession records" maintained by a treatment provider and not shared with others from its education record definition.

**C. [ ]** Neither FERPA nor HIPAA — school counseling records are unregulated in practice
> "Neither FERPA nor HIPAA — school counseling records are unre…" is not what the item tests — FERPA excludes "sole possession records" maintained by a treatment provider and not shared with others from its education record definition.

**D. [ ]** State law only — federal law does not apply to school counseling in this domain
> "State law only — federal law does not apply to school counse…" is not what the item tests — FERPA excludes "sole possession records" maintained by a treatment provider and not shared with others from its education record definition.

**Rationale:** FERPA excludes "sole possession records" maintained by a treatment provider and not shared with others from its education record definition. Separately maintained treatment records in schools may fall under HIPAA if the school is a covered entity, or be governed by state law. The intersection of FERPA and HIPAA in schools is complex; legal consultation is often warranted.

### 214. ppe014 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: Documentation · difficulty: medium

Late entries in clinical records (documentation completed after the session) are:

**A. [ ]** Prohibited under HIPAA regardless of circumstances now as the reference material frames it
> "Prohibited under HIPAA regardless of circumstances now as th…" is not what the item tests — Late entries are clinically and legally common.

**B. [✔]** Acceptable if clearly dated with the actual documentation date and the date of the session
> Late entries are clinically and legally common.

**C. [ ]** Typically considered fraudulent if not completed within 24 hours in the outpatient setting
> "Typically considered fraudulent if not completed within 24 h…" is not what the item tests — Late entries are clinically and legally common.

**D. [ ]** Acceptable only if the client provides retroactive consent as clinicians typically apply it
> "Acceptable only if the client provides retroactive consent a…" is not what the item tests — Late entries are clinically and legally common.

**Rationale:** Late entries are clinically and legally common. Ethical documentation requires accurately recording BOTH the date of the session and the date the note was written. Backdating (recording a false date) is fraudulent. Timely documentation is a professional standard; late but accurately dated notes are preferable to no notes.

### 215. ppe015 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: ClientRights · difficulty: medium

A competent adult client has the right to refuse treatment. Respecting this right, even when the counselor disagrees with the decision, reflects the principle of:

**A. [ ]** Fidelity
> "Fidelity" is not what the item tests — Autonomy: the ethical principle of respecting clients' rights to self-determination — including the right to refuse treatment, discontinue therapy, or make decisions the counselor disagrees with (prov

**B. [ ]** Justice
> "Justice" is not what the item tests — Autonomy: the ethical principle of respecting clients' rights to self-determination — including the right to refuse treatment, discontinue therapy, or make decisions the counselor disagrees with (provi

**C. [✔]** Autonomy
> Autonomy: the ethical principle of respecting clients' rights to self-determination — including the right to refuse treatment, discontinue therapy, or make decisions the counselor disagrees with (provided the client has…

**D. [ ]** Nonmaleficence
> "Nonmaleficence" is not what the item tests — Autonomy: the ethical principle of respecting clients' rights to self-determination — including the right to refuse treatment, discontinue therapy, or make decisions the counselor disagrees with

**Rationale:** Autonomy: the ethical principle of respecting clients' rights to self-determination — including the right to refuse treatment, discontinue therapy, or make decisions the counselor disagrees with (provided the client has decision-making capacity and the refusal does not create imminent danger to others).

### 216. ppe016 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: SuicideDocumentation · difficulty: hard

When documenting a clinical session in which suicide risk was assessed, the MOST important element to include is:

**A. [ ]** A copy of the no-suicide contract signed by the client (as the reference frames it now)
> "A copy of the no-suicide contract signed by the client as th…" is not what the item tests — Documentation of suicide risk assessment should capture the clinical reasoning process: identified risk factors (static and dynamic), protective fa

**B. [ ]** Contact information for the nearest inpatient psychiatric unit (at the item-tests level)
> "Contact information for the nearest inpatient psychiatric un…" is not what the item tests — Documentation of suicide risk assessment should capture the clinical reasoning process: identified risk factors (static and dynamic), protective fa

**C. [ ]** A statement that the client denied any suicidal ideation (as the reference material typically frames it)
> "A statement that the client denied suicidal ideation as the …" is not what the item tests — Documentation of suicide risk assessment should capture the clinical reasoning process: identified risk factors (static and dynamic), protective fa

**D. [✔]** The clinical reasoning — risk and protective factors, findings, and the rationale for the decision
> Documentation of suicide risk assessment should capture the clinical reasoning process: identified risk factors (static and dynamic), protective factors, assessment method/tools used, clinical determination of risk level…

**Rationale:** Documentation of suicide risk assessment should capture the clinical reasoning process: identified risk factors (static and dynamic), protective factors, assessment method/tools used, clinical determination of risk level, and the specific clinical actions taken and why. This demonstrates professional standard of care and provides a defensible record.

### 217. ppe017 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: MulticulturalEthics · difficulty: medium

The ACA's Multicultural and Social Justice Counseling Competencies (MSJCC) position multicultural competence as:

**A. [✔]** A foundational ethical responsibility integrated throughout all competency domains
> The MSJCC (Ratts et al., 2016) frames multicultural and social justice competence as foundational — not optional or specialty.

**B. [ ]** An elective specialty area counselors may choose to develop during a routine session
> "An elective specialty area counselors may choose to develop …" is not what the item tests — The MSJCC (Ratts et al., 2016) frames multicultural and social justice competence as foundational — not optional or specialty.

**C. [ ]** Required only for counselors serving explicitly diverse populations in this domain
> "Required only for counselors serving explicitly diverse popu…" is not what the item tests — The MSJCC (Ratts et al., 2016) frames multicultural and social justice competence as foundational — not optional or specialty.

**D. [ ]** A separate certification program requiring additional supervised hours in practice
> "A separate certification program requiring additional superv…" is not what the item tests — The MSJCC (Ratts et al., 2016) frames multicultural and social justice competence as foundational — not optional or specialty.

**Rationale:** The MSJCC (Ratts et al., 2016) frames multicultural and social justice competence as foundational — not optional or specialty. All counselors are expected to develop awareness, knowledge, skills, and action across cultural and power dimensions regardless of their client population.

### 218. ppe018 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: AssessmentEthics · difficulty: medium

Test security in psychological assessment refers to the counselor's obligation to:

**A. [ ]** Conduct all assessments in a secure, private setting at the level the item tests
> "Conduct all assessments in a secure, private setting at the …" is not what the item tests — ACA Code E.10: counselors maintain the integrity and security of tests and other assessment techniques by protecting test content from unauthorized

**B. [✔]** Store assessments in a locked cabinet accessible only by licensed professionals
> ACA Code E.10: counselors maintain the integrity and security of tests and other assessment techniques by protecting test content from unauthorized disclosure.

**C. [ ]** Ensure test results are encrypted in electronic health records during counseling
> "Ensure test results are encrypted in electronic health recor…" is not what the item tests — ACA Code E.10: counselors maintain the integrity and security of tests and other assessment techniques by protecting test content from unauthorized

**D. [ ]** Only use assessments with no known security vulnerabilities at the intake stage
> "Only use assessments with no known security vulnerabilities …" is not what the item tests — ACA Code E.10: counselors maintain the integrity and security of tests and other assessment techniques by protecting test content from unauthorized

**Rationale:** ACA Code E.10: counselors maintain the integrity and security of tests and other assessment techniques by protecting test content from unauthorized disclosure. This protects assessment validity — if items are publicly known, normative comparisons become invalid. This includes secure storage, preventing item exposure, and not reproducing copyrighted materials.

### 219. ppe019 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: LicensingBoards · difficulty: hard

When a counselor receives a formal complaint from a licensing board, they should FIRST:

**A. [ ]** Notify all current clients of the complaint to maintain transparency by counselors
> "Notify all current clients of the complaint to maintain tran…" is not what the item tests — A licensing board complaint is a legal proceeding with career and licensure implications.

**B. [ ]** Submit a detailed written response to the board within 48 hours at the intake stage
> "Submit a detailed written response to the board within 48 ho…" is not what the item tests — A licensing board complaint is a legal proceeding with career and licensure implications.

**C. [✔]** Consult a licensed attorney familiar with professional licensing before responding
> A licensing board complaint is a legal proceeding with career and licensure implications.

**D. [ ]** Immediately contact the complainant to address their concerns directly in practice
> "Immediately contact the complainant to address their concern…" is not what the item tests — A licensing board complaint is a legal proceeding with career and licensure implications.

**Rationale:** A licensing board complaint is a legal proceeding with career and licensure implications. The counselor should consult an attorney specializing in professional licensing before making any response. Contacting the complainant directly may violate board orders and worsen the situation. Clients should generally not be notified unless required.

### 220. ppe020 · domain: professional_ethics · subdomain: WellnessEthics · difficulty: medium

The ACA Taskforce on Counselor Wellness and Impairment suggests that counselor self-care is an ethical issue primarily because:

**A. [ ]** Wellness modeling increases client motivation for behavior change as the reference material frames it
> "Wellness modeling increases client motivation for behavior c…" is not what the item tests — Self-care is reframed as an ethical obligation (not just a personal benefit) because a counselor's impairment directly compromises client care.

**B. [ ]** Counselors who burn out increase costs to the healthcare system as the reference material frames it
> "Counselors who burn out increase costs to the healthcare sys…" is not what the item tests — Self-care is reframed as an ethical obligation (not just a personal benefit) because a counselor's impairment directly compromises client care.

**C. [ ]** The ACA Code requires counselors to engage in specific wellness activities during a routine session
> "The ACA Code requires counselors to engage in specific welln…" is not what the item tests — Self-care is reframed as an ethical obligation (not just a personal benefit) because a counselor's impairment directly compromises client care.

**D. [✔]** Impaired counselors cannot provide competent care, and client welfare depends on counselor wellness
> Self-care is reframed as an ethical obligation (not just a personal benefit) because a counselor's impairment directly compromises client care.

**Rationale:** Self-care is reframed as an ethical obligation (not just a personal benefit) because a counselor's impairment directly compromises client care. ACA Code C.2.g and C.2.e address impairment: counselors whose personal problems impair competence must address this, limiting practice if necessary.

**B. [ ]** Orient the client to agency rules, fees, and paperwork in practice as the reference material frames it
> "Orient the client to agency rules, fees, and paperwork in pr…" is not what the item tests — The intake serves multiple purposes but its primary clinical function is comprehensive information-gathering to understand the client's presenting

**C. [ ]** Determine whether the client is appropriate for the agency's services as clinicians typically apply it
> "Determine whether the client is appropriate for the agency's…" is not what the item tests — The intake serves multiple purposes but its primary clinical function is comprehensive information-gathering to understand the client's presenting

**D. [ ]** Establish a formal diagnosis for insurance billing during counseling as the reference material frames it
> "Establish a formal diagnosis for insurance billing during co…" is not what the item tests — The intake serves multiple purposes but its primary clinical function is comprehensive information-gathering to understand the client's presenting

**Rationale:** The intake serves multiple purposes but its primary clinical function is comprehensive information-gathering to understand the client's presenting concerns, history, strengths, and context — enabling an accurate formulation and appropriate treatment planning.

**B. [✔]** Record the client's own words describing their primary reason for seeking help
> The chief complaint is best documented in the client's own words (often in quotation marks), capturing their subjective experience and primary concern before clinical formulation.

**C. [ ]** Note only the DSM-diagnosable components of the client's stated concern itself
> "Note only the DSM-diagnosable components of the client's sta…" is not what the item tests — The chief complaint is best documented in the client's own words (often in quotation marks), capturing their subjective experience and primary conc

**Rationale:** The chief complaint is best documented in the client's own words (often in quotation marks), capturing their subjective experience and primary concern before clinical formulation. This preserves the client's voice and provides a baseline for tracking progress.

### 223. int003 · domain: intake · subdomain: PresentingProblem · difficulty: medium

**A. [ ]** Previous diagnoses and hospitalizations only itself as the reference material frames it
> "Previous diagnoses and hospitalizations only itself as the r…" is not what the item tests — HPI: characterizes the current episode — when it started (onset), how long it has lasted (duration), how intense it is (severity), whether it is st

**B. [ ]** Family history of the same presenting concern today as the reference material frames it
> "Family history of the same presenting concern today as the r…" is not what the item tests — HPI: characterizes the current episode — when it started (onset), how long it has lasted (duration), how intense it is (severity), whether it is st

**C. [✔]** The onset, duration, severity, course, and precipitating factors of the current concern
> HPI: characterizes the current episode — when it started (onset), how long it has lasted (duration), how intense it is (severity), whether it is stable/worsening/episodic (course), and what may have triggered or preceded it (precipitants).

**D. [ ]** The client's entire psychiatric history from childhood to present during a routine session
> "The client's entire psychiatric history from childhood to pr…" is not what the item tests — HPI: characterizes the current episode — when it started (onset), how long it has lasted (duration), how intense it is (severity), whether it is st

**Rationale:** HPI: characterizes the current episode — when it started (onset), how long it has lasted (duration), how intense it is (severity), whether it is stable/worsening/episodic (course), and what may have triggered or preceded it (precipitants). This is distinct from full psychiatric history.

### 224. int004 · domain: intake · subdomain: PsychiatricHistory · difficulty: medium

When gathering psychiatric history at intake, "previous treatment response" is clinically important because:

**A. [ ]** It is required by most third-party payers before authorizing services at the intake stage
> "It is required by most third-party payers before authorizing…" is not what the item tests — Prior treatment response is one of the most clinically informative elements of psychiatric history: what modalities were tried?

**B. [ ]** It establishes whether the client qualifies for intensive services during a routine session
> "It establishes whether the client qualifies for intensive se…" is not what the item tests — Prior treatment response is one of the most clinically informative elements of psychiatric history: what modalities were tried?

**C. [ ]** It determines which diagnoses the client has had confirmed as clinicians typically apply it
> "It determines which diagnoses the client has had confirmed a…" is not what the item tests — Prior treatment response is one of the most clinically informative elements of psychiatric history: what modalities were tried?

**D. [✔]** It informs which approaches have worked or not worked, guiding current treatment planning
> Prior treatment response is one of the most clinically informative elements of psychiatric history: what modalities were tried?

**Rationale:** Prior treatment response is one of the most clinically informative elements of psychiatric history: what modalities were tried? what were the outcomes? what did the client find helpful or harmful? This avoids repeating ineffective approaches and builds on what works.

### 225. int005 · domain: intake · subdomain: MedicalHistory · difficulty: medium

Obtaining a medical history at intake is clinically important primarily because:

**A. [✔]** Medical conditions can cause, contribute to, or be exacerbated by psychiatric symptoms
> Many medical conditions (thyroid disorders, neurological conditions, autoimmune diseases, medications) produce or mimic psychiatric symptoms.

**B. [ ]** Insurance requires medical clearance before mental health services can begin at intake
> "Insurance requires medical clearance before mental health se…" is not what the item tests — Many medical conditions (thyroid disorders, neurological conditions, autoimmune diseases, medications) produce or mimic psychiatric symptoms.

**C. [ ]** Counselors must rule out medical causes before providing any counseling service itself
> "Counselors must rule out medical causes before providing any…" is not what the item tests — Many medical conditions (thyroid disorders, neurological conditions, autoimmune diseases, medications) produce or mimic psychiatric symptoms.

**D. [ ]** Counselors are required by law to coordinate with medical providers at the intake stage
> "Counselors are required by law to coordinate with medical pr…" is not what the item tests — Many medical conditions (thyroid disorders, neurological conditions, autoimmune diseases, medications) produce or mimic psychiatric symptoms.

**B. [✔]** It establishes the client's biological predisposition and may inform differential diagnosis and risk
> Family psychiatric history informs: (1) genetic/biological vulnerability (e.g., family history of bipolar disorder increases risk), (2) differential diagnosis (e.g., family history of schizophrenia informs evaluation of…

> "It identifies which family members should be included in tre…" is not what the item tests — Family psychiatric history informs: (1) genetic/biological vulnerability (e.g., family history of bipolar disorder increases risk), (2) differentia

> "It determines which diagnoses to exclude during a routine se…" is not what the item tests — Family psychiatric history informs: (1) genetic/biological vulnerability (e.g., family history of bipolar disorder increases risk), (2) differentia

### 228. int008 · domain: intake · subdomain: TraumaScreening · difficulty: medium

**A. [ ]** All psychiatric conditions have a trauma component by counselors as the reference material frames it
> "All psychiatric conditions have a trauma component by counse…" is not what the item tests — Trauma-informed care recognizes that trauma is highly prevalent (ACE studies), often undisclosed without direct inquiry, and influences multiple pr

**B. [ ]** Trauma history determines whether a client can be seen in an outpatient setting as usually described
> "Trauma history determines whether a client can be seen in an…" is not what the item tests — Trauma-informed care recognizes that trauma is highly prevalent (ACE studies), often undisclosed without direct inquiry, and influences multiple pr

> "Trauma history is required for PTSD diagnosis at the intake …" is not what the item tests — Trauma-informed care recognizes that trauma is highly prevalent (ACE studies), often undisclosed without direct inquiry, and influences multiple pr

> Trauma-informed care recognizes that trauma is highly prevalent (ACE studies), often undisclosed without direct inquiry, and influences multiple presenting concerns.

### 229. int009 · domain: intake · subdomain: SubstanceUseHistory · difficulty: medium

When screening for substance use at intake, the CAGE questionnaire screens for:

**A. [✔]** Alcohol use disorder using four questions about cutting down, annoyance, guilt, and eye-openers
> CAGE: "Have you ever felt you should Cut down?

**B. [ ]** Cannabis, alcohol, gambling, and electronic device use here as the reference material frames it
> "Cannabis, alcohol, gambling, and electronic device use here …" is not what the item tests — CAGE: "Have you ever felt you should Cut down?

**C. [ ]** Cocaine, alcohol, gambling, and eating disorders in practice as the reference material frames it
> "Cocaine, alcohol, gambling, and eating disorders in practice…" is not what the item tests — CAGE: "Have you ever felt you should Cut down?

**D. [ ]** Any substance use through a comprehensive drug history here as the reference material frames it
> "Any substance use through a comprehensive drug history here …" is not what the item tests — CAGE: "Have you ever felt you should Cut down?

**Rationale:** CAGE: "Have you ever felt you should Cut down? Have people Annoyed you by criticizing your drinking? Have you ever felt Guilty about your drinking? Have you ever had an Eye-opener (drink first thing in morning)?" Two or more "yes" answers indicate probable alcohol use disorder. CAGE screens specifically for alcohol; AUDIT is broader.

> "The client is under 18 years of age only as the reference ma…" is not what the item tests — Developmental history — milestones, early attachment patterns, school functioning, early trauma — informs understanding of personality development,

**C. [✔]** Legal involvement often intersects with mental health, substance use, and trauma — and may affect treatment
> Legal history provides context for understanding substance use patterns, trauma exposure (victimization or perpetration), systemic stressors, and current legal contingencies (court-mandated treatment, probation, custody).

**D. [ ]** Insurers require legal history disclosure before authorizing treatment as clinicians typically apply it now
> "Insurers require legal history disclosure before authorizing…" is not what the item tests — Legal history provides context for understanding substance use patterns, trauma exposure (victimization or perpetration), systemic stressors, and c

**Rationale:** Legal history provides context for understanding substance use patterns, trauma exposure (victimization or perpetration), systemic stressors, and current legal contingencies (court-mandated treatment, probation, custody). It informs realistic treatment planning and coordination with legal systems.

**B. [ ]** Including it only in assessments for clients who appear religiously observant in this domain
> "Including it only in assessments for clients who appear reli…" is not what the item tests — Religion and spirituality are dimensions of diversity and frequently serve as significant coping resources, sources of meaning, and potential stres

> "Referring all spiritual concerns to chaplains or clergy with…" is not what the item tests — Religion and spirituality are dimensions of diversity and frequently serve as significant coping resources, sources of meaning, and potential stres

> History of suicidal behavior — attempts, hospitalizations, self-harm — is among the strongest known predictors of future suicidal behavior.

**Rationale:** History of suicidal behavior — attempts, hospitalizations, self-harm — is among the strongest known predictors of future suicidal behavior. Comprehensive intake must include this history to properly stratify risk and inform treatment intensity and safety planning from the outset.

> "Contact the referring court to request motivational enhancem…" is not what the item tests — With involuntary clients, acknowledging the mandated context honestly and non-defensively, validating their position, and exploring any personal st

**B. [ ]** Reschedule until the client is ready to engage voluntarily during a routine session as the reference material frames it
> "Reschedule until the client is ready to engage voluntarily d…" is not what the item tests — With involuntary clients, acknowledging the mandated context honestly and non-defensively, validating their position, and exploring any personal st

> With involuntary clients, acknowledging the mandated context honestly and non-defensively, validating their position, and exploring any personal stake they might have ("Is there anything about your situation you'd want t…

> "Remind the client of the legal consequences of non-participa…" is not what the item tests — With involuntary clients, acknowledging the mandated context honestly and non-defensively, validating their position, and exploring any personal st

**Rationale:** With involuntary clients, acknowledging the mandated context honestly and non-defensively, validating their position, and exploring any personal stake they might have ("Is there anything about your situation you'd want to change, apart from what the court requires?") is more likely to build engagement than authority-based approaches.

> "A one-time signed document completed before the first sessio…" is not what the item tests — Informed consent is an ongoing process, not a one-time event.

> "A legal formality primarily protecting the counselor from li…" is not what the item tests — Informed consent is an ongoing process, not a one-time event.

Exceptions exist for safety emergencies. Verbal consent alone is insufficient under HIPAA.

> PHQ-9 (Patient Health Questionnaire-9): a 9-item validated measure of depression severity based on DSM criteria.

> "Psychosis risk in primary care settings now" is not what the item tests — PHQ-9 (Patient Health Questionnaire-9): a 9-item validated measure of depression severity based on DSM criteria.

> "Generalized anxiety disorder severity now" is not what the item tests — PHQ-9 (Patient Health Questionnaire-9): a 9-item validated measure of depression severity based on DSM criteria.

> "PTSD symptom burden in trauma survivors now" is not what the item tests — PHQ-9 (Patient Health Questionnaire-9): a 9-item validated measure of depression severity based on DSM criteria.

**Rationale:** PHQ-9 (Patient Health Questionnaire-9): a 9-item validated measure of depression severity based on DSM criteria. Scores range from 0–27; cutoffs indicate mild, moderate, moderately severe, and severe depression. Also used to track treatment response. Widely used in primary care and mental health settings.

> "A clinician-rated scale for assessing personality disorder s…" is not what the item tests — MMPI-2: an objective (self-report, empirically keyed) measure with clinical scales (depression, schizophrenia, psychopathic deviate, etc.), validit

> "A projective measure assessing unconscious personality dynam…" is not what the item tests — MMPI-2: an objective (self-report, empirically keyed) measure with clinical scales (depression, schizophrenia, psychopathic deviate, etc.), validit

**Rationale:** MMPI-2: an objective (self-report, empirically keyed) measure with clinical scales (depression, schizophrenia, psychopathic deviate, etc.), validity scales (L, F, K — detecting exaggeration, defensiveness), and content/supplementary scales. > MCMI-IV: theoretically based on Millon's evolutionary-biosocial personality theory and closely aligned with DSM-5 personality disorder criteria.

> "Is shorter and used for screening, while MMPI-2 is used for …" is not what the item tests — MCMI-IV: theoretically based on Millon's evolutionary-biosocial personality theory and closely aligned with DSM-5 personality disorder criteria.

**D. [ ]** Is a projective measure rather than an objective self-report at intake as the reference material frames it
> "Is a projective measure rather than an objective self-report…" is not what the item tests — MCMI-IV: theoretically based on Millon's evolutionary-biosocial personality theory and closely aligned with DSM-5 personality disorder criteria.

**Rationale:** MCMI-IV: theoretically based on Millon's evolutionary-biosocial personality theory and closely aligned with DSM-5 personality disorder criteria. Designed specifically for clinical populations. MMPI-2 was empirically derived without a single guiding personality theory. Both are objective self-report instruments.

> "Clients project their responses onto a screen during adminis…" is not what the item tests — Projective measures present ambiguous stimuli to elicit responses that are thought to reveal aspects of the individual's perceptual and cognitive o

**B. [ ]** It was developed by projection onto a psychodynamic theoretical framework as clinicians typically apply it
> "It was developed by projection onto a psychodynamic theoreti…" is not what the item tests — Projective measures present ambiguous stimuli to elicit responses that are thought to reveal aspects of the individual's perceptual and cognitive o

> Projective measures present ambiguous stimuli to elicit responses that are thought to reveal aspects of the individual's perceptual and cognitive organization, emotional processing, and interpersonal representations.

> "It directly measures unconscious conflicts through symbolic …" is not what the item tests — Projective measures present ambiguous stimuli to elicit responses that are thought to reveal aspects of the individual's perceptual and cognitive o

**Rationale:** Projective measures present ambiguous stimuli to elicit responses that are thought to reveal aspects of the individual's perceptual and cognitive organization, emotional processing, and interpersonal representations. The Rorschach is scored via standardized systems (e.g., Exner's Comprehensive System, R-PAS) to reduce subjectivity.

> "Neurological integrity through pattern analysis as clinician…" is not what the item tests — Wechsler scales yield: Full Scale IQ (global cognitive ability) and Index scores — Verbal Comprehension, Visual Spatial (or Perceptual Reasoning),

**B. [ ]** A single general intelligence quotient (g factor) only during a routine session as clinicians typically apply it
> "A single general intelligence quotient (g factor) only durin…" is not what the item tests — Wechsler scales yield: Full Scale IQ (global cognitive ability) and Index scores — Verbal Comprehension, Visual Spatial (or Perceptual Reasoning),

This profile approach identifies cognitive strengths and weaknesses more clinically useful than a single IQ score.

### 249. asx009 · domain: assessment · subdomain: Psychometrics · difficulty: medium

"Reliability" in psychological assessment refers to:

**A. [✔]** The consistency of measurement across time, raters, or items
> Reliability = consistency of measurement.

**B. [ ]** Whether an assessment has been validated in the population being tested
> "Whether an assessment has been validated in the population b…" is not what the item tests — Reliability = consistency of measurement.

**C. [ ]** The accuracy of the assessment's norms for the local population
> "The accuracy of the assessment's norms for the local populat…" is not what the item tests — Reliability = consistency of measurement.

**D. [ ]** Whether an assessment measures what it is intended to measure
> "Whether an assessment measures what it is intended to measur…" is not what the item tests — Reliability = consistency of measurement.

**Rationale:** Reliability = consistency of measurement. Types: test-retest (consistent over time), internal consistency (items correlate with each other), inter-rater (different raters agree). Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity — a measure can be reliably measuring the wrong thing.

### 250. asx010 · domain: assessment · subdomain: Psychometrics · difficulty: medium

"Construct validity" refers to whether an assessment:

**A. [ ]** Produces consistent results when administered to the same person twice
> "Produces consistent results when administered to the same pe…" is not what the item tests — Construct validity: evidence that the assessment measures the theoretical construct it claims to measure (e.g., does the depression scale actually

**B. [✔]** Measures the theoretical construct it is intended to measure
> Construct validity: evidence that the assessment measures the theoretical construct it claims to measure (e.g., does the depression scale actually measure depression, not general distress?).

**C. [ ]** Adequately represents all aspects of the content domain being measured
> "Adequately represents all aspects of the content domain bein…" is not what the item tests — Construct validity: evidence that the assessment measures the theoretical construct it claims to measure (e.g., does the depression scale actually

**D. [ ]** Predicts an important future outcome (e.g., treatment response)
> "Predicts an important future outcome (e.g., treatment respon…" is not what the item tests — Construct validity: evidence that the assessment measures the theoretical construct it claims to measure (e.g., does the depression scale actually

**Rationale:** Construct validity: evidence that the assessment measures the theoretical construct it claims to measure (e.g., does the depression scale actually measure depression, not general distress?). Assessed through convergent validity (correlates with other depression measures) and discriminant validity (does not correlate with unrelated constructs).

### 251. asx011 · domain: assessment · subdomain: Psychometrics · difficulty: hard

A test with high sensitivity but low specificity means:

**A. [ ]** It correctly identifies true negatives but misses many true positives as used
> "It correctly identifies true negatives but misses many true …" is not what the item tests — Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate).

**B. [ ]** It produces consistent results but does not measure the target construct here
> "It produces consistent results but does not measure the targ…" is not what the item tests — Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate).

**C. [✔]** It correctly identifies true positives but also produces many false positives
> Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate).

**D. [ ]** It is appropriate for high-stakes diagnostic decisions during a routine session
> "It is appropriate for high-stakes diagnostic decisions durin…" is not what the item tests — Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate).

**Rationale:** Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate). High sensitivity = few false negatives. Specificity = ability to correctly identify those WITHOUT the condition. Low specificity = many false positives. High sensitivity/low specificity = good for screening (don't miss cases) but produces overidentification.

### 252. asx012 · domain: assessment · subdomain: Psychometrics · difficulty: medium

Norm-referenced assessment interprets a client's score by:

**A. [ ]** Comparing it to a predefined standard of mastery or criterion in practice
> "Comparing it to a predefined standard of mastery or criterio…" is not what the item tests — Norm-referenced assessment locates a score in relation to a standardization sample (e.g., "above the 85th percentile for adults aged 30–40").

**B. [ ]** Evaluating it against the client's own previous performance by counselors
> "Evaluating it against the client's own previous performance …" is not what the item tests — Norm-referenced assessment locates a score in relation to a standardization sample (e.g., "above the 85th percentile for adults aged 30–40").

**C. [ ]** Using clinical cutoffs developed through expert consensus in this domain
> "Using clinical cutoffs developed through expert consensus in…" is not what the item tests — Norm-referenced assessment locates a score in relation to a standardization sample (e.g., "above the 85th percentile for adults aged 30–40").

**D. [✔]** Comparing it to the performance of a relevant normative comparison group
> Norm-referenced assessment locates a score in relation to a standardization sample (e.g., "above the 85th percentile for adults aged 30–40").

**Rationale:** Norm-referenced assessment locates a score in relation to a standardization sample (e.g., "above the 85th percentile for adults aged 30–40"). Criterion-referenced assessment evaluates performance against an absolute standard (e.g., "met the cutoff for clinical depression"). Most standardized personality and cognitive tests are norm-referenced.

### 253. asx013 · domain: assessment · subdomain: RiskAssessment · difficulty: medium

Static risk factors in suicide risk assessment are significant because:

**A. [✔]** They are immutable historical factors that establish baseline risk but cannot be reduced
> Static risk factors (prior attempts, history of trauma, demographic factors) are fixed and establish baseline risk.

**B. [ ]** They only apply to clients with prior hospitalization histories during a routine session
> "They only apply to clients with prior hospitalization histor…" is not what the item tests — Static risk factors (prior attempts, history of trauma, demographic factors) are fixed and establish baseline risk.

**C. [ ]** They are more important than dynamic factors in predicting imminent suicide by counselors
> "They are more important than dynamic factors in predicting i…" is not what the item tests — Static risk factors (prior attempts, history of trauma, demographic factors) are fixed and establish baseline risk.

**D. [ ]** They change with treatment and indicate areas for intervention in the outpatient setting
> "They change with treatment and indicate areas for interventi…" is not what the item tests — Static risk factors (prior attempts, history of trauma, demographic factors) are fixed and establish baseline risk.

**Rationale:** Static risk factors (prior attempts, history of trauma, demographic factors) are fixed and establish baseline risk. Dynamic risk factors (current hopelessness, substance use, access to means, social isolation) are modifiable and more predictive of imminent risk. Both inform the comprehensive risk assessment, but dynamic factors are the targets of intervention.

### 254. asx014 · domain: assessment · subdomain: RiskAssessment · difficulty: hard

"Hopelessness" is particularly significant in suicide risk assessment because:

**A. [ ]** It indicates the client lacks the protective factors needed for outpatient safety at the intake stage
> "It indicates the client lacks the protective factors needed …" is not what the item tests — Beck's hopelessness research (Beck Hopelessness Scale): hopelessness — the belief that the future holds nothing positive — is a stronger predictor

**B. [✔]** Research consistently identifies it as a stronger predictor of suicide than depression severity alone
> Beck's hopelessness research (Beck Hopelessness Scale): hopelessness — the belief that the future holds nothing positive — is a stronger predictor of suicidal intent and completion than depression severity.

**C. [ ]** Clients who express hopelessness are typically in immediate danger as the reference material frames it
> "Clients who express hopelessness are typically in immediate …" is not what the item tests — Beck's hopelessness research (Beck Hopelessness Scale): hopelessness — the belief that the future holds nothing positive — is a stronger predictor

**D. [ ]** It is the primary symptom of Major Depressive Disorder in practice as the reference material frames it
> "It is the primary symptom of Major Depressive Disorder in pr…" is not what the item tests — Beck's hopelessness research (Beck Hopelessness Scale): hopelessness — the belief that the future holds nothing positive — is a stronger predictor

**Rationale:** Beck's hopelessness research (Beck Hopelessness Scale): hopelessness — the belief that the future holds nothing positive — is a stronger predictor of suicidal intent and completion than depression severity. A client can be depressed without hopelessness (more treatable) or hopeless without severe depression (highest risk).

### 255. asx015 · domain: assessment · subdomain: AssessmentBias · difficulty: hard

Cultural bias in standardized assessment most commonly occurs when:

**A. [ ]** Counselors administer assessments without proper training as usually described
> "Counselors administer assessments without proper training as…" is not what the item tests — A normative sample that underrepresents or excludes specific cultural groups leads to scores being interpreted against a reference group that does

**B. [ ]** The client is unfamiliar with standardized testing formats at the intake stage
> "The client is unfamiliar with standardized testing formats a…" is not what the item tests — A normative sample that underrepresents or excludes specific cultural groups leads to scores being interpreted against a reference group that does

**C. [✔]** The normative sample does not adequately represent the client's cultural group
> A normative sample that underrepresents or excludes specific cultural groups leads to scores being interpreted against a reference group that does not reflect the client's experience — inflating apparent pathology.

**D. [ ]** Assessments are translated without back-translation validation during counseling
> "Assessments are translated without back-translation validati…" is not what the item tests — A normative sample that underrepresents or excludes specific cultural groups leads to scores being interpreted against a reference group that does

**Rationale:** A normative sample that underrepresents or excludes specific cultural groups leads to scores being interpreted against a reference group that does not reflect the client's experience — inflating apparent pathology. Item bias (culturally unfamiliar content), construct bias (culture-specific constructs), and method bias (test-taking familiarity) also contribute.

**C. [ ]** Clients have a legal right to know their scores under HIPAA as the reference material frames it
> "Clients have a legal right to know their scores under HIPAA …" is not what the item tests — Therapeutic Assessment (Finn): systematically sharing assessment results with clients in an empathic, collaborative manner — asking the client's ow

**D. [✔]** Collaborative interpretation of results can itself produce therapeutic change and reduce shame
> Therapeutic Assessment (Finn): systematically sharing assessment results with clients in an empathic, collaborative manner — asking the client's own questions — can produce significant therapeutic benefits: increased sel…

**Rationale:** Therapeutic Assessment (Finn): systematically sharing assessment results with clients in an empathic, collaborative manner — asking the client's own questions — can produce significant therapeutic benefits: increased self-understanding, reduced stigma, improved treatment engagement, and even symptom reduction.

**B. [ ]** A guarantee of assessment confidentiality under HIPAA as clinicians typically apply it
> "A guarantee of assessment confidentiality under HIPAA as cli…" is not what the item tests — Assessment informed consent (ACA E.3.a): clients must understand why they are being assessed, how results will be used, who will receive results (i

**C. [ ]** A complete list of all assessment instruments and their psychometric properties today
> "A complete list of all assessment instruments and their psyc…" is not what the item tests — Assessment informed consent (ACA E.3.a): clients must understand why they are being assessed, how results will be used, who will receive results (i

**D. [ ]** The counselor's credentials and training in the specific assessment being used itself
> "The counselor's credentials and training in the specific ass…" is not what the item tests — Assessment informed consent (ACA E.3.a): clients must understand why they are being assessed, how results will be used, who will receive results (i

**Rationale:** Assessment informed consent (ACA E.3.a): clients must understand why they are being assessed, how results will be used, who will receive results (including referring parties), and their right to ask questions. This ensures autonomous decision-making about participation in the assessment process.

> "Are administered by computer rather than a clinician as the …" is not what the item tests — Structured and semi-structured clinical interviews (SCID-5, MINI) systematically cover DSM criteria with standardized questions, reducing clinician

**B. [✔]** Use standardized questions following a prescribed order to reduce diagnostic variability
> Structured and semi-structured clinical interviews (SCID-5, MINI) systematically cover DSM criteria with standardized questions, reducing clinician bias and improving diagnostic reliability and agreement.

**C. [ ]** Are considered less clinically rich but more legally defensible during a routine session
> "Are considered less clinically rich but more legally defensi…" is not what the item tests — Structured and semi-structured clinical interviews (SCID-5, MINI) systematically cover DSM criteria with standardized questions, reducing clinician

**D. [ ]** Cannot be used to assess complex presentations with multiple comorbidities by counselors
> "Cannot be used to assess complex presentations with multiple…" is not what the item tests — Structured and semi-structured clinical interviews (SCID-5, MINI) systematically cover DSM criteria with standardized questions, reducing clinician

**Rationale:** Structured and semi-structured clinical interviews (SCID-5, MINI) systematically cover DSM criteria with standardized questions, reducing clinician bias and improving diagnostic reliability and agreement. They sacrifice some clinical flexibility but significantly improve diagnostic consistency — essential for research and high-stakes clinical decisions.

> "The frequency and intensity of a target behavior across sett…" is not what the item tests — FBA (ABC analysis): Antecedents (what precedes/triggers the behavior), Behavior (specific observable definition), Consequences (what follows the be

**B. [ ]** Neurological and cognitive factors underlying behavioral dysregulation itself
> "Neurological and cognitive factors underlying behavioral dys…" is not what the item tests — FBA (ABC analysis): Antecedents (what precedes/triggers the behavior), Behavior (specific observable definition), Consequences (what follows the be

**C. [✔]** The antecedents, behavior, and consequences that maintain the target behavior
> FBA (ABC analysis): Antecedents (what precedes/triggers the behavior), Behavior (specific observable definition), Consequences (what follows the behavior and maintains it).

**D. [ ]** Whether the behavior meets criteria for a behavioral disorder diagnosis today

### 260. asx020 · domain: assessment · subdomain: ScreeningVsDiagnosis · difficulty: medium

A screening tool with a positive result should be followed by:

**A. [ ]** Immediate psychoeducation about the screened-for condition during counseling
> "Immediate psychoeducation about the screened-for condition d…" is not what the item tests — Screening tools identify individuals who may warrant further evaluation — they are not diagnostic instruments.

**B. [ ]** Assignment of the corresponding diagnostic code for billing purposes today
> "Assignment of the corresponding diagnostic code for billing …" is not what the item tests — Screening tools identify individuals who may warrant further evaluation — they are not diagnostic instruments.

**C. [ ]** Referral to psychiatry for medication evaluation in the outpatient setting
> "Referral to psychiatry for medication evaluation in the outp…" is not what the item tests — Screening tools identify individuals who may warrant further evaluation — they are not diagnostic instruments.

**D. [✔]** A comprehensive clinical assessment to confirm or disconfirm the diagnosis
> Screening tools identify individuals who may warrant further evaluation — they are not diagnostic instruments.

**Rationale:** Screening tools identify individuals who may warrant further evaluation — they are not diagnostic instruments. A positive screen indicates that a full clinical assessment (diagnostic interview, history, collateral information) should follow to confirm, rule out, or characterize the presentation before any diagnosis is assigned.

### 261. dx001 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: DXProcess · difficulty: easy

A "provisional" DSM-5 diagnosis is used when:

**A. [✔]** There is a strong presumption the full criteria will be met but information is still being gathered
> DSM-5 provisional specifier: used when the clinician has reason to believe the full criteria will be met but insufficient information exists to confirm (e.g., a client presenting in crisis before a full history is obtained).

**B. [ ]** The counselor is not fully trained to confirm the diagnosis now as the reference material frames it
> "The counselor is not fully trained to confirm the diagnosis …" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 provisional specifier: used when the clinician has reason to believe the full criteria will be met but insufficient information exists to con

**C. [ ]** Symptoms are subclinical and do not fully meet diagnostic criteria as clinicians typically apply it
> "Symptoms are subclinical and do not fully meet diagnostic cr…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 provisional specifier: used when the clinician has reason to believe the full criteria will be met but insufficient information exists to con

**D. [ ]** The client has not consented to receiving a formal diagnosis now as the reference material frames it
> "The client has not consented to receiving a formal diagnosis…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 provisional specifier: used when the clinician has reason to believe the full criteria will be met but insufficient information exists to con

**Rationale:** DSM-5 provisional specifier: used when the clinician has reason to believe the full criteria will be met but insufficient information exists to confirm (e.g., a client presenting in crisis before a full history is obtained). It is a working diagnosis pending further assessment.

### 262. dx002 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: DXProcess · difficulty: medium

The DSM-5 requires that diagnostic criteria include a "clinically significant" requirement. This means symptoms must:

**A. [ ]** Be rated as severe by a validated assessment tool now as the reference material frames it
> "Be rated as severe by a validated assessment tool now as the…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 clinical significance: symptoms must cause clinically significant distress OR functional impairment (social, occupational, academic).

**B. [✔]** Cause distress OR functional impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas
> DSM-5 clinical significance: symptoms must cause clinically significant distress OR functional impairment (social, occupational, academic).

**C. [ ]** Be present for at least six months to qualify as clinically significant during counseling
> "Be present for at least six months to qualify as clinically …" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 clinical significance: symptoms must cause clinically significant distress OR functional impairment (social, occupational, academic).

**D. [ ]** Represent a change from the client's lifelong baseline functioning during a routine session
> "Represent a change from the client's lifelong baseline funct…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 clinical significance: symptoms must cause clinically significant distress OR functional impairment (social, occupational, academic).

**Rationale:** DSM-5 clinical significance: symptoms must cause clinically significant distress OR functional impairment (social, occupational, academic). This criterion distinguishes disorder from normative variation. Symptoms can be intense but not clinically significant if they cause no distress or impairment.

### 263. dx003 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: DifferentialDiagnosis · difficulty: medium

The key feature distinguishing Persistent Depressive Disorder (PDD/Dysthymia) from Major Depressive Disorder (MDD) is:

**A. [ ]** MDD can include psychotic features; PDD cannot in practice as the reference material frames it
> "MDD can include psychotic features; PDD cannot in practice a…" is not what the item tests — PDD (DSM-5 F34.1): depressed mood for most of the day, more days than not, for at least 2 years (1 year for children/adolescents), with at least 2

**B. [ ]** PDD involves more severe symptoms than MDD in this domain as the reference material frames it
> "PDD involves more severe symptoms than MDD in this domain as…" is not what the item tests — PDD (DSM-5 F34.1): depressed mood for most of the day, more days than not, for at least 2 years (1 year for children/adolescents), with at least 2

**C. [✔]** PDD requires depressed mood present most of the day, more days than not, for at least 2 years
> PDD (DSM-5 F34.1): depressed mood for most of the day, more days than not, for at least 2 years (1 year for children/adolescents), with at least 2 additional symptoms.

**D. [ ]** PDD occurs only in adults; MDD can occur across the lifespan as clinicians typically apply it
> "PDD occurs only in adults; MDD can occur across the lifespan…" is not what the item tests — PDD (DSM-5 F34.1): depressed mood for most of the day, more days than not, for at least 2 years (1 year for children/adolescents), with at least 2

**Rationale:** PDD (DSM-5 F34.1): depressed mood for most of the day, more days than not, for at least 2 years (1 year for children/adolescents), with at least 2 additional symptoms. MDD involves more discrete episodes with 5+ symptoms for 2 weeks. Both can co-occur ("double depression").

### 264. dx004 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: DifferentialDiagnosis · difficulty: hard

The key diagnostic feature distinguishing PTSD from Acute Stress Disorder (ASD) is:

**A. [ ]** ASD responds only to medication; PTSD responds to psychotherapy during a routine session
> "ASD responds only to medication; PTSD responds to psychother…" is not what the item tests — Timing is the primary distinguishing criterion: ASD (F43.0) is diagnosed 3 days to 1 month post-trauma.

**B. [ ]** PTSD includes dissociative symptoms; ASD does not now as clinicians typically apply it
> "PTSD includes dissociative symptoms; ASD does not now as cli…" is not what the item tests — Timing is the primary distinguishing criterion: ASD (F43.0) is diagnosed 3 days to 1 month post-trauma.

**C. [ ]** ASD requires a greater number of traumatic events now as clinicians typically apply it
> "ASD requires a greater number of traumatic events now as cli…" is not what the item tests — Timing is the primary distinguishing criterion: ASD (F43.0) is diagnosed 3 days to 1 month post-trauma.

**D. [✔]** ASD is diagnosed within 3 days to 1 month post-trauma; PTSD is diagnosed after 1 month
> Timing is the primary distinguishing criterion: ASD (F43.0) is diagnosed 3 days to 1 month post-trauma.

**Rationale:** Timing is the primary distinguishing criterion: ASD (F43.0) is diagnosed 3 days to 1 month post-trauma. PTSD (F43.10) is diagnosed when symptoms persist beyond 1 month. Both require the same Criterion A trauma exposure. ASD actually requires more dissociative symptoms than PTSD.

### 265. dx005 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: DifferentialDiagnosis · difficulty: hard

Differentiating Borderline Personality Disorder (BPD) from Bipolar II Disorder is MOST reliably done by examining:

**A. [✔]** The temporal relationship between mood instability and interpersonal events vs. spontaneous episode onset
> Key distinction: BPD mood instability is reactive — triggered by interpersonal events, shifting within hours to days.

**B. [ ]** Whether mood episodes respond to mood stabilizing medication at intake as the reference material frames it
> "Whether mood episodes respond to mood stabilizing medication…" is not what the item tests — Key distinction: BPD mood instability is reactive — triggered by interpersonal events, shifting within hours to days.

**C. [ ]** Age of onset — BPD presents in adolescence; Bipolar II in early adulthood as clinicians typically apply it
> "Age of onset — BPD presents in adolescence; Bipolar II in ea…" is not what the item tests — Key distinction: BPD mood instability is reactive — triggered by interpersonal events, shifting within hours to days.

**D. [ ]** The presence of self-harm behavior, which occurs only in BPD at intake as the reference material frames it
> "The presence of self-harm behavior, which occurs only in BPD…" is not what the item tests — Key distinction: BPD mood instability is reactive — triggered by interpersonal events, shifting within hours to days.

**Rationale:** Key distinction: BPD mood instability is reactive — triggered by interpersonal events, shifting within hours to days. Bipolar II episodes are more spontaneous, sustained (days to weeks), and episodic, often without clear interpersonal precipitants. Identity disturbance, fear of abandonment, and chronic emptiness are more specific to BPD.

### 266. dx006 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: DifferentialDiagnosis · difficulty: medium

**A. [ ]** OCD typically presents with depression; GAD does not during any routine visit
> "OCD typically presents with depression; GAD does not during …" is not what the item tests — OCD: ego-dystonic intrusive thoughts (obsessions) + compulsive rituals driven by the need to neutralize anxiety.

**B. [✔]** OCD features discrete obsessions with compulsive rituals; GAD involves pervasive diffuse worry
> OCD: ego-dystonic intrusive thoughts (obsessions) + compulsive rituals driven by the need to neutralize anxiety.

**C. [ ]** GAD responds to CBT; OCD does not (as the reference material typically frames it in item usage)
> "GAD responds to CBT; OCD does not as the reference material …" is not what the item tests — OCD: ego-dystonic intrusive thoughts (obsessions) + compulsive rituals driven by the need to neutralize anxiety.

**D. [ ]** OCD involves more severe anxiety than GAD, as the reference material frames it here now
> "OCD involves more severe anxiety than GAD as the reference m…" is not what the item tests — OCD: ego-dystonic intrusive thoughts (obsessions) + compulsive rituals driven by the need to neutralize anxiety.

**Rationale:** OCD: ego-dystonic intrusive thoughts (obsessions) + compulsive rituals driven by the need to neutralize anxiety. GAD: pervasive, chronic, largely ego-syntonic worry across multiple life domains without discrete obsessions or compulsive rituals. OCD content is often perceived as repugnant or foreign to the self.

### 267. dx007 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: DifferentialDiagnosis · difficulty: medium

To distinguish a substance-induced depressive disorder from a primary depressive disorder, the clinician should determine:

**A. [ ]** Whether the substance is a CNS depressant during a routine session as clinicians typically apply it now
> "Whether the substance is a CNS depressant during a routine s…" is not what the item tests — Substance-induced depressive disorder: symptoms develop during or within 1 month of intoxication/withdrawal and resolve when the substance is remov

**B. [ ]** Whether the client meets full MDD criteria as usually described as the reference material frames it
> "Whether the client meets full MDD criteria as usually descri…" is not what the item tests — Substance-induced depressive disorder: symptoms develop during or within 1 month of intoxication/withdrawal and resolve when the substance is remov

**C. [✔]** Whether depressive symptoms preceded substance use or persist beyond the expected withdrawal period
> Substance-induced depressive disorder: symptoms develop during or within 1 month of intoxication/withdrawal and resolve when the substance is removed.

**D. [ ]** Whether the client has a family history of depression at intake as the reference material frames it
> "Whether the client has a family history of depression at int…" is not what the item tests — Substance-induced depressive disorder: symptoms develop during or within 1 month of intoxication/withdrawal and resolve when the substance is remov

**Rationale:** Substance-induced depressive disorder: symptoms develop during or within 1 month of intoxication/withdrawal and resolve when the substance is removed. Primary MDD: symptoms preceded substance use, persist more than 1 month after cessation, or have a prior history independent of substance use. Timeline and temporal relationship are the key diagnostic tools.

> "Brief Psychotic Disorder typically has an identifiable stres…" is not what the item tests — Duration: Brief Psychotic Disorder (F23) = 1 day to less than 1 month, with full return to premorbid functioning.

> "The presence of negative symptoms, which occur only in Schiz…" is not what the item tests — Duration: Brief Psychotic Disorder (F23) = 1 day to less than 1 month, with full return to premorbid functioning.

> Duration: Brief Psychotic Disorder (F23) = 1 day to less than 1 month, with full return to premorbid functioning.

**Rationale:** Duration: Brief Psychotic Disorder (F23) = 1 day to less than 1 month, with full return to premorbid functioning. Schizophreniform Disorder = 1–6 months. Schizophrenia requires symptoms for at least 6 months (including prodromal and residual phases). Symptom type alone does not differentiate them.

> "At least six symptoms of inattention, regardless of age now …" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 ADHD: Criterion B requires that "several inattentive or hyperactive-impulsive symptoms were present prior to age 12 years." Adults must prese

**Rationale:** DSM-5 ADHD: Criterion B requires that "several inattentive or hyperactive-impulsive symptoms were present prior to age 12 years." Adults must present with 5 (not 6) symptoms in each domain. Adults often present primarily with inattention symptoms as hyperactivity diminishes with age. Retrospective history is often obtained from school records or family report.

**B. [✔]** Whether behaviors include aggression, property destruction, deceitfulness, or rule violations (CD)
> ODD: persistent pattern of angry/irritable mood, argumentative/defiant behavior, or vindictiveness — without physical aggression, property destruction, theft, or deceit.

**C. [ ]** Whether the behavior occurs across multiple settings as the reference material typically frames it here

> "Whether the child has callous-unemotional traits as the refe…" is not what the item tests — ODD: persistent pattern of angry/irritable mood, argumentative/defiant behavior, or vindictiveness — without physical aggression, property destruct

**Rationale:** ODD: persistent pattern of angry/irritable mood, argumentative/defiant behavior, or vindictiveness — without physical aggression, property destruction, theft, or deceit. CD includes these more serious violations. CD is more severe and carries worse long-term prognosis. ODD can develop into CD but is not the same disorder.

**B. [ ]** The cultural values that make certain DSM diagnoses more acceptable than others as the reference material frames it
> "The cultural values that make certain DSM diagnoses more acc…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 cultural concepts of distress (replacing "culture-bound syndromes"): includes cultural syndromes (clusters of symptoms occurring in specific

> DSM-5 cultural concepts of distress (replacing "culture-bound syndromes"): includes cultural syndromes (clusters of symptoms occurring in specific cultural contexts), cultural idioms of distress (shared expressions like…

**Rationale:** DSM-5 cultural concepts of distress (replacing "culture-bound syndromes"): includes cultural syndromes (clusters of symptoms occurring in specific cultural contexts), cultural idioms of distress (shared expressions like "nerves"), and cultural explanations (explanatory models of causation). They inform how clients present and what is diagnostically meaningful.

> "A personality disorder that better explains the presentation…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 Criterion E (or similar exclusion criteria): the disturbance must not be attributable to the physiological effects of a substance (drug of ab

**C. [ ]** Recent life stressors that could explain the symptoms during counseling
> "Recent life stressors that could explain the symptoms during…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 Criterion E (or similar exclusion criteria): the disturbance must not be attributable to the physiological effects of a substance (drug of ab

**D. [✔]** A general medical condition or the physiological effects of a substance
> DSM-5 Criterion E (or similar exclusion criteria): the disturbance must not be attributable to the physiological effects of a substance (drug of abuse, medication) or another medical condition.

**Rationale:** DSM-5 Criterion E (or similar exclusion criteria): the disturbance must not be attributable to the physiological effects of a substance (drug of abuse, medication) or another medical condition. Thyroid conditions, autoimmune diseases, neurological conditions, and medications commonly produce psychiatric symptoms.

### 273. dx013 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: VSyndromes · difficulty: medium

Z-codes (formerly V-codes) in DSM-5/ICD-10 are clinically useful because they:

**A. [✔]** Document psychosocial and contextual factors that may affect mental health without assigning a mental disorder diagnosis
> Z-codes (ICD-10/DSM-5 Chapter on Other Conditions): document relational problems, abuse and neglect, housing/economic problems, social environment factors, and other psychosocial contexts affecting health.

**B. [ ]** Provide diagnostic specificity for subclinical presentations during a routine session as the reference material frames it
> "Provide diagnostic specificity for subclinical presentations…" is not what the item tests — Z-codes (ICD-10/DSM-5 Chapter on Other Conditions): document relational problems, abuse and neglect, housing/economic problems, social environment

**C. [ ]** Are required by insurance before any mental disorder diagnosis can be billed as used as the reference material frames it
> "Are required by insurance before any mental disorder diagnos…" is not what the item tests — Z-codes (ICD-10/DSM-5 Chapter on Other Conditions): document relational problems, abuse and neglect, housing/economic problems, social environment

**D. [ ]** Are used when the clinician is uncertain which diagnosis applies at the intake stage as the reference material frames it
> "Are used when the clinician is uncertain which diagnosis app…" is not what the item tests — Z-codes (ICD-10/DSM-5 Chapter on Other Conditions): document relational problems, abuse and neglect, housing/economic problems, social environment

**Rationale:** Z-codes (ICD-10/DSM-5 Chapter on Other Conditions): document relational problems, abuse and neglect, housing/economic problems, social environment factors, and other psychosocial contexts affecting health. They provide clinical documentation of context without pathologizing the client and may be the primary focus of treatment.

### 274. dx014 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: Specifiers · difficulty: medium

The "with anxious distress" specifier in DSM-5 mood disorder diagnoses is clinically significant because:

**A. [ ]** It is required when prescribing anxiolytics alongside antidepressants by counselors
> "It is required when prescribing anxiolytics alongside antide…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 "with anxious distress" specifier: MDD or bipolar with tension, restlessness, difficulty concentrating due to worry, fear of losing control.

**B. [✔]** It identifies a subgroup with higher suicide risk and different treatment response
> DSM-5 "with anxious distress" specifier: MDD or bipolar with tension, restlessness, difficulty concentrating due to worry, fear of losing control.

**C. [ ]** It shifts the primary diagnosis from mood disorder to anxiety disorder in practice
> "It shifts the primary diagnosis from mood disorder to anxiet…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 "with anxious distress" specifier: MDD or bipolar with tension, restlessness, difficulty concentrating due to worry, fear of losing control.

**D. [ ]** It indicates the client also meets criteria for a separate anxiety disorder itself
> "It indicates the client also meets criteria for a separate a…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 "with anxious distress" specifier: MDD or bipolar with tension, restlessness, difficulty concentrating due to worry, fear of losing control.

**Rationale:** DSM-5 "with anxious distress" specifier: MDD or bipolar with tension, restlessness, difficulty concentrating due to worry, fear of losing control. Research shows this subgroup has higher suicide risk, worse treatment response to standard antidepressants, and longer time to remission. It does not constitute a separate anxiety disorder diagnosis.

### 275. dx015 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: Comorbidity · difficulty: medium

In clinical practice, comorbidity (multiple co-occurring diagnoses) is:

**A. [ ]** Only relevant when diagnoses are from different diagnostic families
> "Only relevant when diagnoses are from different diagnostic f…" is not what the item tests — Epidemiological data (NCS-R and similar studies) consistently show that over 50% of individuals with one mental disorder meet criteria for at least

**B. [ ]** Unusual and suggests diagnostic error when present by counselors
> "Unusual and suggests diagnostic error when present by counse…" is not what the item tests — Epidemiological data (NCS-R and similar studies) consistently show that over 50% of individuals with one mental disorder meet criteria for at least

**C. [✔]** The norm rather than the exception in mental health populations
> Epidemiological data (NCS-R and similar studies) consistently show that over 50% of individuals with one mental disorder meet criteria for at least one more.

**D. [ ]** Caused by diagnostic category overlap in the DSM system as used
> "Caused by diagnostic category overlap in the DSM system as u…" is not what the item tests — Epidemiological data (NCS-R and similar studies) consistently show that over 50% of individuals with one mental disorder meet criteria for at least

**Rationale:** Epidemiological data (NCS-R and similar studies) consistently show that over 50% of individuals with one mental disorder meet criteria for at least one more. Comorbidity is the rule, not the exception. It complicates treatment planning and generally worsens prognosis — requiring integrated rather than sequential treatment approaches.

### 276. dx016 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: SeverityRatings · difficulty: easy

In DSM-5, severity specifiers (mild, moderate, severe) are primarily based on:

**A. [ ]** The clinician's global assessment of functioning (GAF) as clinicians typically apply it
> "The clinician's global assessment of functioning (GAF) as cl…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 severity ratings are typically based on symptom count beyond the minimum diagnostic threshold and level of functional impairment: mild (few e

**B. [ ]** Score ranges on a standardized severity rating scale as the reference material frames it
> "Score ranges on a standardized severity rating scale as the …" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 severity ratings are typically based on symptom count beyond the minimum diagnostic threshold and level of functional impairment: mild (few e

**C. [ ]** Duration of symptoms above the minimum criterion now as the reference material frames it
> "Duration of symptoms above the minimum criterion now as the …" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 severity ratings are typically based on symptom count beyond the minimum diagnostic threshold and level of functional impairment: mild (few e

**D. [✔]** The number of symptoms present beyond threshold and the degree of functional impairment
> DSM-5 severity ratings are typically based on symptom count beyond the minimum diagnostic threshold and level of functional impairment: mild (few extra symptoms, mild distress/impairment), moderate (symptoms/impairment b…

**Rationale:** DSM-5 severity ratings are typically based on symptom count beyond the minimum diagnostic threshold and level of functional impairment: mild (few extra symptoms, mild distress/impairment), moderate (symptoms/impairment between mild and severe), severe (substantially more symptoms, marked impairment). Specific severity criteria vary by diagnosis.
