### 277. dx017 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: MalingeringVsFactitious · difficulty: hard

The KEY distinction between Factitious Disorder and Malingering is:

**A. [✔]** Malingering involves external incentives (financial, legal); factitious disorder is motivated by assuming the sick role without external gain
> Both involve intentional symptom production.

**B. [ ]** In factitious disorder, symptoms are not intentionally produced as the reference material frames it at intake as the reference material frames it
> "In factitious disorder, symptoms are not intentionally produ…" is not what the item tests — Both involve intentional symptom production.

**C. [ ]** Malingering typically involves physical symptoms; factitious disorder involves psychological symptoms now as the reference material frames it
> "Malingering typically involves physical symptoms; factitious…" is not what the item tests — Both involve intentional symptom production.

**D. [ ]** Factitious disorder is diagnosed in adults; malingering is more common in adolescents at the intake stage as the reference material frames it
> "Factitious disorder is diagnosed in adults; malingering is m…" is not what the item tests — Both involve intentional symptom production.

**Rationale:** Both involve intentional symptom production. Distinction is motivation: Malingering = external incentives (disability payments, avoiding legal consequences, obtaining medications). Factitious Disorder = internal psychological need to assume the sick role without obvious external rewards. Malingering is not a mental disorder in DSM-5; Factitious Disorder is.

### 278. dx018 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: SomaticDiagnosis · difficulty: medium

Somatic Symptom Disorder (SSD) in DSM-5 differs from prior editions' Somatization Disorder in that SSD:

**A. [ ]** Is classified under neurodevelopmental disorders in DSM-5 (as clinicians typically apply it now)
> "Is classified under neurodevelopmental disorders in DSM-5 as…" is not what the item tests — SSD (DSM-5): a major change from prior somatoform categories is that medical explanation is NOT required for exclusion.

**B. [✔]** Can be diagnosed even when medical explanation exists, based on maladaptive thoughts and behaviors
> SSD (DSM-5): a major change from prior somatoform categories is that medical explanation is NOT required for exclusion.

**C. [ ]** Requires symptoms to be medically unexplained as the reference material frames it in practice today
> "Requires symptoms to be medically unexplained as the referen…" is not what the item tests — SSD (DSM-5): a major change from prior somatoform categories is that medical explanation is NOT required for exclusion.

**D. [ ]** Requires a longer symptom history than prior criteria (as the reference material frames it now)
> "Requires a longer symptom history than prior criteria as the…" is not what the item tests — SSD (DSM-5): a major change from prior somatoform categories is that medical explanation is NOT required for exclusion.

**Rationale:** SSD (DSM-5): a major change from prior somatoform categories is that medical explanation is NOT required for exclusion. The diagnosis is based on excessive thoughts, feelings, and behaviors related to symptoms (disproportionate worry, high health anxiety, excessive time/energy devoted to symptoms) — regardless of whether symptoms have a medical basis.

### 279. dx019 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: NeurodevelopmentalDX · difficulty: medium

Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD) in DSM-5 consolidated prior categories (Autistic Disorder, Asperger's, PDD-NOS) primarily because:

**A. [ ]** The consolidation reduced stigma for higher-functioning individuals in the outpatient setting as the reference material frames it
> "The consolidation reduced stigma for higher-functioning indi…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 ASD consolidation: reliability studies showed clinicians inconsistently applied the prior subtypes, and the core features (social communicati

**B. [ ]** Research showed Asperger's was not a valid clinical construct as clinicians typically apply it as the reference material frames it
> "Research showed Asperger's was not a valid clinical construc…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 ASD consolidation: reliability studies showed clinicians inconsistently applied the prior subtypes, and the core features (social communicati

**C. [✔]** Evidence showed the prior distinctions lacked diagnostic reliability and shared the same core features at varying severity levels
> DSM-5 ASD consolidation: reliability studies showed clinicians inconsistently applied the prior subtypes, and the core features (social communication deficits + restricted/repetitive behaviors) appeared continuous across severity levels.

**D. [ ]** Insurance reimbursement required a single diagnosis for ABA therapy coverage during counseling as the reference material frames it
> "Insurance reimbursement required a single diagnosis for ABA …" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 ASD consolidation: reliability studies showed clinicians inconsistently applied the prior subtypes, and the core features (social communicati

**Rationale:** DSM-5 ASD consolidation: reliability studies showed clinicians inconsistently applied the prior subtypes, and the core features (social communication deficits + restricted/repetitive behaviors) appeared continuous across severity levels. Severity specifiers (Level 1–3 for each domain) replaced separate categories.

### 280. dx020 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: DiagnosisDevelopmentalContext · difficulty: hard

When diagnosing depression in adolescents, which modification to adult criteria does DSM-5 specify?

**A. [ ]** Duration is shortened to 1 week instead of 2 weeks during counseling
> "Duration is shortened to 1 week instead of 2 weeks during co…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 MDD in children/adolescents: irritable mood can be substituted for depressed mood as the primary mood symptom (Criterion A1).

**B. [ ]** Adolescents must meet 7 rather than 5 criteria at the intake stage
> "Adolescents must meet 7 rather than 5 criteria at the intake…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 MDD in children/adolescents: irritable mood can be substituted for depressed mood as the primary mood symptom (Criterion A1).

**C. [ ]** Vegetative symptoms are excluded from consideration in adolescents
> "Vegetative symptoms (sleep, appetite) are excluded from cons…" is not what the item tests — DSM-5 MDD in children/adolescents: irritable mood can be substituted for depressed mood as the primary mood symptom (Criterion A1).

**D. [✔]** Irritable mood can substitute for depressed mood as a core symptom
> DSM-5 MDD in children/adolescents: irritable mood can be substituted for depressed mood as the primary mood symptom (Criterion A1).

**Rationale:** DSM-5 MDD in children/adolescents: irritable mood can be substituted for depressed mood as the primary mood symptom (Criterion A1). This is a developmentally sensitive modification — adolescents may present depression as irritability, anger, or behaviorally rather than sad affect.

### 281. tp001 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: GoalWriting · difficulty: easy

A well-written short-term treatment goal for a client with MDD should be:

**A. [✔]** Specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART)
> SMART goals provide direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and increase client investment.

**B. [ ]** Focused exclusively on symptom elimination during a routine session
> "Focused exclusively on symptom elimination during a routine …" is not what the item tests — SMART goals provide direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and increase client investment.

**C. [ ]** Written by the clinician and presented to the client for signature
> "Written by the clinician and presented to the client for sig…" is not what the item tests — SMART goals provide direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and increase client investment.

**D. [ ]** Broad enough to allow flexible interpretation across sessions here
> "Broad enough to allow flexible interpretation across session…" is not what the item tests — SMART goals provide direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and increase client investment.

**Rationale:** SMART goals provide direction, allow objective progress monitoring, and increase client investment. Vague goals like "feel better" cannot be tracked or evaluated. Symptom elimination alone is often insufficient and unrealistic as a sole target.

### 282. tp002 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: GoalWriting · difficulty: medium

Which of the following is the BEST example of a measurable short-term goal?

**A. [ ]** Client will work on reducing anxiety through therapy as used as the reference material frames it
> "Client will work on reducing anxiety through therapy as used…" is not what the item tests — Option C is measurable (three techniques, 7/10 threshold), behavioral (identify and use), time-bound (4 weeks), and specific.

**B. [✔]** Client will identify and use three grounding techniques when anxiety reaches 7/10 within 4 weeks
> Option C is measurable (three techniques, 7/10 threshold), behavioral (identify and use), time-bound (4 weeks), and specific.

**C. [ ]** Client will understand the connection between thoughts and feelings as clinicians typically apply it
> "Client will understand the connection between thoughts and f…" is not what the item tests — Option C is measurable (three techniques, 7/10 threshold), behavioral (identify and use), time-bound (4 weeks), and specific.

**D. [ ]** Client will improve coping skills over the next few months now as the reference material frames it
> "Client will improve coping skills over the next few months n…" is not what the item tests — Option C is measurable (three techniques, 7/10 threshold), behavioral (identify and use), time-bound (4 weeks), and specific.

**Rationale:** Option C is measurable (three techniques, 7/10 threshold), behavioral (identify and use), time-bound (4 weeks), and specific. Options A, B, and D use verbs — "improve," "understand," "work on" — that cannot be objectively observed or counted.

### 283. tp003 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: GoalWriting · difficulty: hard

Short-term goals differ from long-term goals in treatment planning primarily in that short-term goals:

**A. [ ]** Are less clinically important and can be skipped when time is limited during a routine session
> "Are less clinically important and can be skipped when time i…" is not what the item tests — Short-term goals are stepping stones — building skills and reducing acute distress in weeks to months.

**B. [ ]** Are typically set by the counselor; long-term goals are set collaboratively during counseling
> "Are typically set by the counselor; long-term goals are set …" is not what the item tests — Short-term goals are stepping stones — building skills and reducing acute distress in weeks to months.

**C. [✔]** Address immediate stabilization and skill-building that scaffolds toward long-term outcomes
> Short-term goals are stepping stones — building skills and reducing acute distress in weeks to months.

**D. [ ]** Cover a fixed period of 6 months while long-term goals cover a full year at the intake stage
> "Cover a fixed period of 6 months while long-term goals cover…" is not what the item tests — Short-term goals are stepping stones — building skills and reducing acute distress in weeks to months.

**Rationale:** Short-term goals are stepping stones — building skills and reducing acute distress in weeks to months. Long-term goals address functional recovery and identity-level outcomes over months to years. Short-term goals create early wins that build momentum and alliance.

### 284. tp004 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: InterventionSequencing · difficulty: medium

A client presents with severe PTSD and active alcohol use disorder. The CORRECT treatment sequencing is:

**A. [ ]** Stabilize PTSD first through trauma processing, then address the alcohol use as clinicians typically apply it
> "Stabilize PTSD first through trauma processing, then address…" is not what the item tests — Research (Seeking Safety, COPE): integrated concurrent treatment of PTSD + SUD produces better outcomes than sequential approaches.

**B. [ ]** Address alcohol use exclusively since it is the primary disorder at intake as the reference material frames it
> "Address alcohol use exclusively since it is the primary diso…" is not what the item tests — Research (Seeking Safety, COPE): integrated concurrent treatment of PTSD + SUD produces better outcomes than sequential approaches.

**C. [ ]** Require complete abstinence before any trauma work begins during counseling as the reference material frames it
> "Require complete abstinence before any trauma work begins du…" is not what the item tests — Research (Seeking Safety, COPE): integrated concurrent treatment of PTSD + SUD produces better outcomes than sequential approaches.

**D. [✔]** Integrated, concurrent treatment of PTSD and SUD; only acute withdrawal justifies medical stabilization first
> Research (Seeking Safety, COPE): integrated concurrent treatment of PTSD + SUD produces better outcomes than sequential approaches.

**Rationale:** Research (Seeking Safety, COPE): integrated concurrent treatment of PTSD + SUD produces better outcomes than sequential approaches. The outdated "get sober first" model is exam-penalized. Exception: acute medical withdrawal requires medical stabilization before trauma work.

---

### 285. tp005 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: InterventionSequencing · difficulty: medium

For a client with OCD, the correct sequencing of treatment components is:

**A. [✔]** Psychoeducation → hierarchy construction → graduated ERP with response prevention
> ERP for OCD: psychoeducation (the obsession-compulsion model and ERP rationale) must precede exposure so the client understands why response prevention works.

**B. [ ]** Cognitive restructuring → psychoeducation → ERP as a final phase during counseling
> "Cognitive restructuring → psychoeducation → ERP as a final p…" is not what the item tests — ERP for OCD: psychoeducation (the obsession-compulsion model and ERP rationale) must precede exposure so the client understands why response preven

**C. [ ]** Begin ERP immediately → add psychoeducation later to maintain momentum in practice
> "Begin ERP immediately → add psychoeducation later to maintai…" is not what the item tests — ERP for OCD: psychoeducation (the obsession-compulsion model and ERP rationale) must precede exposure so the client understands why response preven

**D. [ ]** Relaxation training → cognitive restructuring → ERP as clinicians typically apply it
> "Relaxation training → cognitive restructuring → ERP as clini…" is not what the item tests — ERP for OCD: psychoeducation (the obsession-compulsion model and ERP rationale) must precede exposure so the client understands why response preven

**Rationale:** ERP for OCD: psychoeducation (the obsession-compulsion model and ERP rationale) must precede exposure so the client understands why response prevention works. Hierarchy construction follows. ERP then proceeds from lowest to highest anxiety items. Starting without psychoeducation undermines buy-in.

### 286. tp006 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: InterventionSequencing · difficulty: hard

A client with DID presents for treatment. Phase-oriented treatment prescribes the FIRST phase as:

**A. [ ]** Integration of all personality states as quickly as possible at intake
> "Integration of all personality states as quickly as possible…" is not what the item tests — Phase-oriented trauma treatment (ISSTD guidelines): Phase 1 = safety and stabilization (grounding, affect regulation, reducing crisis, building int

**B. [✔]** Safety, stabilization, and building internal communication among parts
> Phase-oriented trauma treatment (ISSTD guidelines): Phase 1 = safety and stabilization (grounding, affect regulation, reducing crisis, building internal cooperation).

**C. [ ]** Hypnosis to access and retrieve traumatic memories at the intake stage
> "Hypnosis to access and retrieve traumatic memories at the in…" is not what the item tests — Phase-oriented trauma treatment (ISSTD guidelines): Phase 1 = safety and stabilization (grounding, affect regulation, reducing crisis, building int

**D. [ ]** Processing traumatic memories held by child parts as usually described now
> "Processing traumatic memories held by child parts as usually…" is not what the item tests — Phase-oriented trauma treatment (ISSTD guidelines): Phase 1 = safety and stabilization (grounding, affect regulation, reducing crisis, building int

**Rationale:** Phase-oriented trauma treatment (ISSTD guidelines): Phase 1 = safety and stabilization (grounding, affect regulation, reducing crisis, building internal cooperation). Phase 2 = trauma processing. Phase 3 = integration. Skipping Phase 1 causes destabilization and retraumatization.

### 287. tp007 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: LevelOfCare · difficulty: easy

The "least restrictive environment" principle in treatment planning means:

**A. [ ]** Using the least amount of structure to respect client preferences today as the reference material frames it
> "Using the least amount of structure to respect client prefer…" is not what the item tests — Least restrictive environment: match the level of care to clinical need.

**B. [ ]** Typically starting with outpatient services regardless of symptom severity as clinicians typically apply it
> "Typically starting with outpatient services regardless of sy…" is not what the item tests — Least restrictive environment: match the level of care to clinical need.

**C. [✔]** Placing the client in the least intensive setting that can safely and effectively meet their clinical needs
> Least restrictive environment: match the level of care to clinical need.

**D. [ ]** Avoiding hospitalization under all circumstances to preserve client autonomy as clinicians typically apply it
> "Avoiding hospitalization under all circumstances to preserve…" is not what the item tests — Least restrictive environment: match the level of care to clinical need.

**Rationale:** Least restrictive environment: match the level of care to clinical need. Too much is unnecessarily disruptive; too little leaves the client unsafe. The NCMHCE rewards accurate calibration — not reflexive avoidance or overuse of intensive services.

### 288. tp008 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: LevelOfCare · difficulty: medium

A client with moderate MDD shows minimal improvement after 10 weekly sessions. The MOST appropriate treatment plan adjustment is:

**A. [ ]** Continue the current plan — 10 sessions is insufficient to judge progress during a routine session
> "Continue the current plan — 10 sessions is insufficient to j…" is not what the item tests — Lack of adequate response after 8–12 sessions triggers a plan review: increase frequency, reconsider the approach, add medication augmentation.

**B. [ ]** Terminate and refer since the client is not responding to treatment as clinicians typically apply it
> "Terminate and refer since the client is not responding to tr…" is not what the item tests — Lack of adequate response after 8–12 sessions triggers a plan review: increase frequency, reconsider the approach, add medication augmentation.

**C. [ ]** Add a relaxation component to the existing treatment plan now as the reference material frames it
> "Add a relaxation component to the existing treatment plan no…" is not what the item tests — Lack of adequate response after 8–12 sessions triggers a plan review: increase frequency, reconsider the approach, add medication augmentation.

**D. [✔]** Step up frequency or intensity, review the treatment approach, and consider a medication referral
> Lack of adequate response after 8–12 sessions triggers a plan review: increase frequency, reconsider the approach, add medication augmentation.

**Rationale:** Lack of adequate response after 8–12 sessions triggers a plan review: increase frequency, reconsider the approach, add medication augmentation. Continuing the same plan without adjustment when progress is stalled is a clinical error specifically tested on the NCMHCE.

### 289. tp009 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: LevelOfCare · difficulty: medium

ASAM criteria are used in substance use disorder treatment planning to:

**A. [✔]** Match the client to the appropriate level of care across six multidimensional assessment areas
> ASAM criteria assess six dimensions: acute intoxication/withdrawal potential, biomedical conditions, emotional/behavioral/cognitive conditions, readiness to change, relapse/continued use potential, and recovery environment.

**B. [ ]** Determine exclusively whether medication-assisted treatment is indicated during a routine session
> "Determine exclusively whether medication-assisted treatment …" is not what the item tests — ASAM criteria assess six dimensions: acute intoxication/withdrawal potential, biomedical conditions, emotional/behavioral/cognitive conditions, rea

**C. [ ]** Diagnose the specific substance use disorder and its DSM-5 severity level as usually described
> "Diagnose the specific substance use disorder and its DSM-5 s…" is not what the item tests — ASAM criteria assess six dimensions: acute intoxication/withdrawal potential, biomedical conditions, emotional/behavioral/cognitive conditions, rea

**D. [ ]** Establish the minimum number of sessions insurance will authorize as clinicians typically apply it
> "Establish the minimum number of sessions insurance will auth…" is not what the item tests — ASAM criteria assess six dimensions: acute intoxication/withdrawal potential, biomedical conditions, emotional/behavioral/cognitive conditions, rea

**Rationale:** ASAM criteria assess six dimensions: acute intoxication/withdrawal potential, biomedical conditions, emotional/behavioral/cognitive conditions, readiness to change, relapse/continued use potential, and recovery environment. Together they determine the least restrictive clinically appropriate level of care.

### 290. tp010 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: ModalityMatching · difficulty: medium

A client presents with Panic Disorder. The treatment plan should specify which CBT protocol?

**A. [ ]** Systematic desensitization to external feared situations only
> "Systematic desensitization to external feared situations onl…" is not what the item tests — Panic disorder treatment: interoceptive exposure (Barlow) deliberately induces feared bodily sensations — racing heart, dizziness, shortness of bre

**B. [✔]** Interoceptive exposure targeting fear of physical sensations
> Panic disorder treatment: interoceptive exposure (Barlow) deliberately induces feared bodily sensations — racing heart, dizziness, shortness of breath — to break the catastrophic misinterpretation cycle.

**C. [ ]** Behavioral activation targeting avoidance and withdrawal now
> "Behavioral activation targeting avoidance and withdrawal now" is not what the item tests — Panic disorder treatment: interoceptive exposure (Barlow) deliberately induces feared bodily sensations — racing heart, dizziness, shortness of brea

**D. [ ]** ERP targeting obsessional triggers and compulsive rituals now
> "ERP targeting obsessional triggers and compulsive rituals no…" is not what the item tests — Panic disorder treatment: interoceptive exposure (Barlow) deliberately induces feared bodily sensations — racing heart, dizziness, shortness of bre

**Rationale:** Panic disorder treatment: interoceptive exposure (Barlow) deliberately induces feared bodily sensations — racing heart, dizziness, shortness of breath — to break the catastrophic misinterpretation cycle. This is distinct from specific phobia (in-vivo graduated exposure to external stimuli) and OCD (ERP).

### 291. tp011 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: ModalityMatching · difficulty: hard

A 9-year-old presents with PTSD following documented chronic abuse. The evidence-based treatment plan should specify:

**A. [ ]** Non-directive play therapy until the child is ready to process the trauma
> "Non-directive play therapy until the child is ready to verba…" is not what the item tests — TF-CBT (Cohen, Mannarino, Deblinger) has the strongest evidence for childhood PTSD.

**B. [ ]** EMDR as the only validated approach for children under 12 now
> "EMDR as the only validated approach for children under 12 no…" is not what the item tests — TF-CBT (Cohen, Mannarino, Deblinger) has the strongest evidence for childhood PTSD.

**C. [✔]** Trauma-Focused CBT (TF-CBT) with parallel caregiver sessions
> TF-CBT (Cohen, Mannarino, Deblinger) has the strongest evidence for childhood PTSD.

**D. [ ]** The standard adult CPT protocol adapted for the child’s developmental level
> "The standard adult CPT protocol adapted for the child’s deve…" is not what the item tests — TF-CBT (Cohen, Mannarino, Deblinger) has the strongest evidence for childhood PTSD.

**Rationale:** TF-CBT (Cohen, Mannarino, Deblinger) has the strongest evidence for childhood PTSD. It includes parallel caregiver components, direct trauma processing (trauma narrative), and conjoint child-caregiver sessions. Non-directive play therapy alone does not meet the evidence standard for PTSD.

---

### 292. tp012 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: SessionFrequency · difficulty: medium

A client with Borderline Personality Disorder beginning DBT should be informed that standard DBT treatment requires:

**A. [ ]** Weekly individual sessions only, with skills group added if progress stalls as clinicians typically apply it
> "Weekly individual sessions only, with skills group added if …" is not what the item tests — Standard DBT (Linehan) has four required components: weekly individual therapy (targets hierarchy: life-threatening → therapy-interfering → quality

**B. [ ]** Twice-weekly individual sessions until crisis frequency decreases below a threshold during a routine session
> "Twice-weekly individual sessions until crisis frequency decr…" is not what the item tests — Standard DBT (Linehan) has four required components: weekly individual therapy (targets hierarchy: life-threatening → therapy-interfering → quality

**C. [ ]** Group skills training alone for mild BPD presentations during counseling as the reference material frames it
> "Group skills training alone for mild BPD presentations durin…" is not what the item tests — Standard DBT (Linehan) has four required components: weekly individual therapy (targets hierarchy: life-threatening → therapy-interfering → quality

**D. [✔]** Individual therapy, skills training group, between-session phone coaching, and therapist consultation team
> Standard DBT (Linehan) has four required components: weekly individual therapy (targets hierarchy: life-threatening → therapy-interfering → quality-of-life behaviors), weekly skills group, between-session phone coaching…

**Rationale:** Standard DBT (Linehan) has four required components: weekly individual therapy (targets hierarchy: life-threatening → therapy-interfering → quality-of-life behaviors), weekly skills group, between-session phone coaching, and therapist consultation team. Partial DBT (skills only) produces significantly weaker outcomes.

### 293. tp013 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: SessionFrequency · difficulty: medium

Increasing session frequency from weekly to twice-weekly is MOST clinically indicated when:

**A. [✔]** Acute safety risk, crisis instability, or insufficient progress warrants more intensive contact
> Frequency increases are clinically driven by: active suicidality requiring closer monitoring, recent crisis or hospitalization, insufficient progress at current intensity, or the demands of intensive trauma processing.

**B. [ ]** The client’s insurance plan covers the additional sessions now as clinicians typically apply it
> "The client’s insurance plan covers the additional sessions n…" is not what the item tests — Frequency increases are clinically driven by: active suicidality requiring closer monitoring, recent crisis or hospitalization, insufficient progre

**C. [ ]** The client requests more support regardless of clinical presentation at the level the item tests
> "The client requests more support regardless of clinical pres…" is not what the item tests — Frequency increases are clinically driven by: active suicidality requiring closer monitoring, recent crisis or hospitalization, insufficient progre

**D. [ ]** This is standard practice for all clients beginning trauma processing work as usually described
> "This is standard practice for all clients beginning trauma p…" is not what the item tests — Frequency increases are clinically driven by: active suicidality requiring closer monitoring, recent crisis or hospitalization, insufficient progre

**Rationale:** Frequency increases are clinically driven by: active suicidality requiring closer monitoring, recent crisis or hospitalization, insufficient progress at current intensity, or the demands of intensive trauma processing. Insurance coverage informs feasibility but is never the clinical indication.

---

### 294. tp014 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: ProgressMonitoring · difficulty: medium

Routine outcome monitoring (ROM) in treatment planning involves:

**A. [ ]** Reviewing progress notes at the end of each calendar quarter as the reference material frames it
> "Reviewing progress notes at the end of each calendar quarter…" is not what the item tests — ROM (Lambert, PCOMS): brief validated measures (PHQ-9, ORS/SRS) administered each session provide real-time feedback.

**B. [✔]** Systematically tracking client-reported outcomes at each session to guide treatment adjustments
> ROM (Lambert, PCOMS): brief validated measures (PHQ-9, ORS/SRS) administered each session provide real-time feedback.

**C. [ ]** Conducting formal diagnostic reassessment every six months now as clinicians typically apply it
> "Conducting formal diagnostic reassessment every six months n…" is not what the item tests — ROM (Lambert, PCOMS): brief validated measures (PHQ-9, ORS/SRS) administered each session provide real-time feedback.

**D. [ ]** Administering the full intake battery at the treatment midpoint as clinicians typically apply it
> "Administering the full intake battery at the treatment midpo…" is not what the item tests — ROM (Lambert, PCOMS): brief validated measures (PHQ-9, ORS/SRS) administered each session provide real-time feedback.

**Rationale:** ROM (Lambert, PCOMS): brief validated measures (PHQ-9, ORS/SRS) administered each session provide real-time feedback. Research shows clinicians are often unaware when clients are deteriorating without ROM — and feedback loops significantly improve outcomes by enabling earlier plan adjustment.

### 295. tp015 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: ProgressMonitoring · difficulty: hard

A client’s PHQ-9 scores have remained unchanged after 8 sessions of CBT for MDD. The FIRST treatment plan adjustment the counselor should consider is:

**A. [ ]** Switching to a completely different theoretical orientation by counselors as the reference material frames it
> "Switching to a completely different theoretical orientation …" is not what the item tests — Lack of progress triggers a systematic review before major changes: Is the formulation accurate?

**B. [ ]** Transferring the client to a more experienced clinician during counseling as the reference material frames it
> "Transferring the client to a more experienced clinician duri…" is not what the item tests — Lack of progress triggers a systematic review before major changes: Is the formulation accurate?

**C. [✔]** Reviewing the case conceptualization, assessing engagement barriers, and considering medication augmentation
> Lack of progress triggers a systematic review before major changes: Is the formulation accurate?

**D. [ ]** Adding relaxation training as a supplementary component during counseling as the reference material frames it
> "Adding relaxation training as a supplementary component duri…" is not what the item tests — Lack of progress triggers a systematic review before major changes: Is the formulation accurate?

**Rationale:** Lack of progress triggers a systematic review before major changes: Is the formulation accurate? Are there maintaining factors not addressed (sleep, substance use, medical)? Is the client engaging with between-session work? Alliance issues? Is medication warranted? Switching orientations entirely before this review is premature.

### 296. tp016 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: DischargeCriteria · difficulty: easy

Discharge criteria in a treatment plan should be:

**A. [ ]** Left open-ended to allow flexibility as treatment progresses at the level the item tests now
> "Left open-ended to allow flexibility as treatment progresses…" is not what the item tests — Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target.

**B. [ ]** Set after the client achieves at least 80% symptom reduction at the level the item tests
> "Set after the client achieves at least 80% symptom reduction…" is not what the item tests — Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target.

**C. [ ]** Determined by the insurance company’s authorized number of sessions as usually described
> "Determined by the insurance company’s authorized number of s…" is not what the item tests — Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target.

**D. [✔]** Defined at the outset as observable, measurable indicators that goals have been achieved
> Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target.

**Rationale:** Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target. They typically include symptom thresholds (e.g., PHQ-9 < 5), functional indicators (return to work, rebuilt relationships), and skill mastery (client applies techniques independently). Insurance authorization is administrative, not a clinical criterion.

### 297. tp017 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: DischargeCriteria · difficulty: medium

A client with panic disorder has had no panic attacks for 6 weeks and reports confidence managing symptoms independently. The BEST treatment plan decision is:

**A. [✔]** Begin tapering sessions, review relapse prevention, and establish a plan for returning if needed
> When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end.

**B. [ ]** Terminate immediately to prevent the development of therapeutic dependency during a routine session
> "Terminate immediately to prevent the development of therapeu…" is not what the item tests — When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end.

**C. [ ]** Continue at the same frequency indefinitely to prevent any relapse as clinicians typically apply it
> "Continue at the same frequency indefinitely to prevent any r…" is not what the item tests — When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end.

**D. [ ]** Switch to open-ended maintenance therapy focused on general wellbeing at the level the item tests
> "Switch to open-ended maintenance therapy focused on general …" is not what the item tests — When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end.

**Rationale:** When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end. Moving from weekly to biweekly to monthly builds confidence in independent functioning. Relapse prevention — identifying early warning signs, reviewing coping strategies, clarifying when to return — is required for anxiety disorder termination.

---

### 298. tp018 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: CollaborativePlanning · difficulty: medium

Collaborative treatment planning — involving the client in goal-setting — is MOST important because:

**A. [ ]** Clinicians lack sufficient expertise to set goals without client input at the level the item tests
> "Clinicians lack sufficient expertise to set goals without cl…" is not what the item tests — Collaborative goal-setting increases client ownership, aligns treatment with the client’s own values and priorities, and strengthens alliance.

**B. [✔]** Client-generated goals are associated with stronger therapeutic alliance, motivation, and outcomes
> Collaborative goal-setting increases client ownership, aligns treatment with the client’s own values and priorities, and strengthens alliance.

**C. [ ]** It satisfies managed care and documentation requirements today as the reference material frames it now
> "It satisfies managed care and documentation requirements tod…" is not what the item tests — Collaborative goal-setting increases client ownership, aligns treatment with the client’s own values and priorities, and strengthens alliance.

**D. [ ]** It shifts clinical responsibility to the client if goals are not achieved during a routine session
> "It shifts clinical responsibility to the client if goals are…" is not what the item tests — Collaborative goal-setting increases client ownership, aligns treatment with the client’s own values and priorities, and strengthens alliance.

**Rationale:** Collaborative goal-setting increases client ownership, aligns treatment with the client’s own values and priorities, and strengthens alliance. Goal consensus research consistently shows that goals clients perceive as their own — not imposed by the clinician — predict stronger engagement and better outcomes.

### 299. tp019 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: CulturalTreatmentPlanning · difficulty: hard

When developing a treatment plan with a client from a collectivist cultural background, the counselor MOST importantly should:

**A. [ ]** Avoid all family involvement to maintain individual client confidentiality as clinicians typically apply it
> "Avoid all family involvement to maintain individual client c…" is not what the item tests — Culturally responsive treatment planning adapts both goal content and structure to the client’s cultural context.

**B. [ ]** Apply Western individualistic goal frameworks since these are empirically validated as usually described
> "Apply Western individualistic goal frameworks since these ar…" is not what the item tests — Culturally responsive treatment planning adapts both goal content and structure to the client’s cultural context.

**C. [✔]** Explore whether including family or community in goals and interventions aligns with the client’s values
> Culturally responsive treatment planning adapts both goal content and structure to the client’s cultural context.

**D. [ ]** Refer to a counselor from the same cultural background for more appropriate care during a routine session
> "Refer to a counselor from the same cultural background for m…" is not what the item tests — Culturally responsive treatment planning adapts both goal content and structure to the client’s cultural context.

**Rationale:** Culturally responsive treatment planning adapts both goal content and structure to the client’s cultural context. In collectivist cultures, goals framed around family harmony, community contribution, or relational functioning may be more motivating and clinically meaningful than individually-framed goals. The counselor explores this rather than assumes.

### 300. tp020 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: StepDownPlanning · difficulty: medium

A client is being discharged from inpatient psychiatric hospitalization. The MOST critical component of the step-down plan is:

**A. [ ]** The client’s verbal agreement to contact the inpatient unit if symptoms worsen during counseling
> "The client’s verbal agreement to contact the inpatient unit …" is not what the item tests — The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission.

**B. [ ]** A written summary of the inpatient treatment provided to the client at the level the item tests
> "A written summary of the inpatient treatment provided to the…" is not what the item tests — The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission.

**C. [ ]** A 30-day medication supply with written administration instructions at the level the item tests
> "A 30-day medication supply with written administration instr…" is not what the item tests — The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission.

**D. [✔]** A confirmed outpatient appointment within 7 days of discharge, with crisis resources identified
> The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission.

**Rationale:** The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission. A confirmed follow-up appointment within 7 days (ideally 24–72 hours) is the single strongest predictor of successful transition. Without a scheduled appointment, the step-down plan is clinically incomplete regardless of other components.
